. When conditioning on
v\in S_{\ell}
, this determines at most
\eta
edges between
v
and
T
because of assumption 3. To estimate the probability that the remaining edges between
v
and
T
are also present, we use the fact that the number of duplicate labels
\mathcal{D}_{T}
is small so it is unlikely for
v
to choose such a label and the contribution of this event to
p_{\overline{L}}
is negligible. If no label in
\mathcal{D}_{T}
is chosen, then (over the remaining randomness) the remaining edges between
v
and
T
are independent since each label in
[d]\setminus\mathcal{D}_{T}
is chosen by at most one vertex in
T
.

Formally, we distinguish the following two cases. In the first case,
v
choses a label in
\mathcal{D}_{T}
. By the bound on
|\mathcal{D}_{T}|
due to the occurrence of
\mathcal{E}(A,t,b)
, this happens with probability at most
Ct\log(n)\delta
. In the second case, no label in
\mathcal{D}_{T}
is chosen, and thus (using
Lemma

4.2
), the probability that
v\sim T
is at most
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p\bigl(1+O\bigl(\log(n)d^{-1/4}\bigr)\bigr)}}\right)^{t-\eta}
. Both cases together yield the bound

\mathbb{P}(v\in N_{G}(T)\mid v\in S_{\ell})\leqslant Ct\log(n)\delta+\Bigl(p\bigl(1+O\bigl(\log(n)d^{-1/4}\bigr)\bigr)\Bigr)^{t-\eta}\leqslant(1+o(1))p^{t-\eta}+o(1).

In total (recalling that
k=n\delta
), this yields that

\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[|S_{\ell}\cap N_{G}(T)|}}\right]\leqslant 2\delta k|L|+n\delta\bigl((1+o(1))p^{t-\eta}+o(1)\bigr)\leqslant(1+o(1))p^{t-\eta}k+o(k).

Further, we get from a Chernoff/Bernstein bound and a subsequent union bound over all
\ell\in[d]\setminus L
that
|S_{\ell}\cap N_{G}(T)|\leqslant(1+o(1))p^{t-\eta}k+o(k)
holds for all such labels with probability
1-n^{-\omega(1)}
.

Finally, since
|\mathcal{B}_{S\cup R}|\leqslant b
by the occurrence of
\mathcal{E}(A,t,b)
, this implies that

\displaystyle|U_{S,R}^{(\text{bad})}|

\displaystyle\leqslant\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{r}\sum_{\ell\in\mathcal{B}_{S\cup R}}|S_{\ell}\cap N_{G}(T)|

\displaystyle\leqslant(1+o(1))b\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{r}\cdot p^{t-\eta}k+o(k)

with probability
1-n^{-\omega(1)}
. Using a union bound, the above holds for all
S,R\subseteq A
with
|S|=|R|=r
with high probability.

Estimating
U_{S,R}^{(\text{good})}

To estimate
U_{S,R}^{(\text{good})}
, we afford a union bound over all
S,R\subseteq A
with
|S|=|R|=r
. To this end, first consider an arbitrary fixed pair of sets
S,R\subseteq A
with
|S|=|R|=r
. We note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[U_{S,R}^{(\text{good})}]=\sum_{v\in B}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathds{1}(M_{v}\cap\mathcal{B}_{S\cup R}=\emptyset)\cdot\mathds{1}(v\in N_{G}(T))\cdot u_{S,p}(v)u_{R,p}(v)}}\right].

To estimate the above expectation, we fix a
v\in B
and condition on the event that
M_{v}\cap\mathcal{B}_{S\cup R}=\emptyset
since the case
M_{v}\cap\mathcal{B}_{S\cup R}\neq\emptyset
is covered by our bounds on
U_{S,R}^{(\text{bad})}
. In this conditional probability space
\Omega
, we first draw the labels in
\mathcal{D}_{S\cup R\cup T}
for all vertices in
B
, and afterwards draw all the remaining labels for vertices in
B
. Regarding the first step, denote by
\mathcal{E}_{(0)},\mathcal{E}_{(1)}
, and
\mathcal{E}_{(>1)}
the event that
v
draws zero, exactly one, or strictly more than one label from
\mathcal{D}_{S\cup R\cup T}
, respectively. Using the law of total expectation, we bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\Omega}[\mathds{1}(v\in N_{G}(T))\cdot u_{S,p}(v)u_{R,p}(v)]

\displaystyle\leqslant\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{r}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{(>1)})

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{(1)})\mathbb{E}_{\Omega}[u_{S,p}(v)u_{R,p}(v)\mid\mathcal{E}_{(1)}\cap\{v\in N_{G}(T)\}]

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\mathbb{E}_{\Omega}[u_{S,p}(v)u_{R,p}(v)\mid\mathcal{E}_{(0)}\cap\{v\in N_{G}(T)\}].

Since
|\mathcal{D}_{S\cup R\cup T}|\leqslant 2\log(n)^{2}
by assumption 4, we get that

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{(>1)})\leqslant\binom{|\mathcal{D}_{S\cup R\cup T}|}{2}\delta^{2}\leqslant O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\log(n)^{4}\delta^{2}}}\right)\text{ and }\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{(1)})=O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\log(n)^{2}\delta}}\right).

Since
n\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{(>1)})=o(k)
, that is, smaller than our desired
\Delta_{s}
, we can essentially restrict our attention to the other two cases and assume that at most one label in
\mathcal{D}_{S\cup R\cup T}
and no label in
\mathcal{B}_{S\cup R}
was chosen by
v
. By construction, all labels not in
\mathcal{D}_{S\cup R\cup T}
are chosen by at most one vertex in
S\cup R\cup T
, so after revealing the labels in
\mathcal{D}_{S\cup R\cup T}
, the random variables
\mathds{1}(v\in N_{G}(T))
and
u_{S,p}(v)u_{R,p}(v)
are independent, and every edge between
v
and
S\cup T
appears independently. By
Lemma

4.2
, for each such edge
e=\{u,v\}
, this happens with probability
p\pm\gamma
, for some
\gamma=O(\log(n)\delta\sqrt{\delta d})
. Hence,

\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[H_{p}(u,v)}}\right]\leqslant\sqrt{\frac{p}{1-p}}(1-p+\gamma)-\sqrt{\frac{1-p}{p}}(p-\gamma)\leqslant 2\gamma\sqrt{\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}}.

Now, assume that
\mathcal{E}_{(0)}
occurs after drawing the labels in
\mathcal{D}_{S\cup R\cup T}
. Since
|S\triangle R|\geqslant 3
, there are at least three edges between
S\triangle R
and
v
, whose contribution to
\mathbb{E}_{\Omega}[u_{S,p}(v)u_{R,p}(v)\mid\mathcal{E}_{(0)}\cap\{v\in N_{G}(T)\}]
is
O\bigl(\log(n)\delta\sqrt{\delta d}\bigr)^{3}\sqrt{\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{(1-p)/p,p/(1-p)}}\right\}^{3}}
. All remaining edges have a contribution of at most
\sqrt{\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{(1-p)/p,p/(1-p)}}\right\}^{2r-3}}
. In total,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\Omega}[u_{S,p}(v)u_{R,p}(v)\mid\mathcal{E}_{(0)}\cap\{v\in N_{G}(T)\}]

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\leqslant O\bigl(\log(n)\delta\sqrt{\delta d}\bigr)^{3}\sqrt{\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{3}}\sqrt{\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{2r-3}}

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt=O\bigl(\log(n)^{3}\delta^{3}(\delta d)^{3/2}\bigr)\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{r}.

Moreover, note that if
\mathcal{E}_{(1)}
occurs, this determines at most two edges between
v
and
S\cup R
. Since
|S\triangle R|\geqslant 3
, after this happens, there is still some “randomness” left for at least one further edge in
S\triangle R
, which is now (i.e. after revealing the labels of
v
in
\mathcal{D}_{S\cup R\cup T}
) independent of
\mathds{1}(v\in N_{G}(T))
. Therefore, using the same argument as before,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\Omega}[u_{S,p}(v)u_{R,p}(v)\mid\mathcal{E}_{(1)}\cap\{v\in N_{G}(T)\}]

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\leqslant O\bigl(\log(n)\delta\sqrt{\delta d}\bigr)\sqrt{\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}}\sqrt{\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{2r-1}}

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt=O\bigl(\log(n)\delta\sqrt{\delta d}\bigr)\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{r}.

In total, this yields that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[U_{S,R}^{(\text{good})}]\leqslant\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{r}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\log(n)^{4}n\delta^{2}}}\right)+O\bigl(\log(n)^{3}n\delta^{2}\sqrt{\delta d}\bigr)+O\bigl(\log(n)^{3}n\delta^{3}(\delta d)^{3/2}\bigr)}}\right).

Using
Lemma

4.1
and the assumption
d\geqslant n^{\alpha}
, it follows that
\mathbb{E}[U_{S,R}^{(\text{good})}]\leqslant o(k)((1-p)/p)^{r}
.
Now, using a Bernstein bound as in
Lemma

3.1
and setting
z=C\sqrt{n\log(n)}
for
C
sufficiently large, we get that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|U_{S,R}^{(\text{good})}|-\mathbb{E}[U_{S,R}^{(\text{good})}]|\geqslant z}}\right)\leqslant

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt2\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-z^{2}/\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(8\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{2r}n+\frac{4}{3}\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{r}z}}\right)}}\right)\leqslant n^{-2t-2r}

Using a union bound over all
S,R\subseteq A
with
r
vertices yields that for all such
S,R
, we have

\displaystyle U_{S,R}^{(\text{good})}=\bigl(o(k)+O(\sqrt{n\log(n)})\bigr)\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{r}=o(k)\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{r}

since
k\gg\sqrt{n\log(n)}
, and this holds with probability
1-n^{-2t}
.

Plugging it together

In total, we have shown that

\displaystyle|U_{S,R}|\leqslant|U_{S,R}^{(\text{good})}|+|U_{S,R}^{(\text{bad})}|

\displaystyle\leqslant o(k)\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{r}+(1+o(1))b\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{r}\cdot p^{t-\eta}k+o(k)

\displaystyle\leqslant(1+o(1))b\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{r}\cdot p^{t-\eta}k+o(k)

holds for all
S,R\subseteq A
with
|S|=|R|=r
and
|S\triangle R|\geqslant 3
with probability
1-n^{-2t}
, as desired.
∎

4.3
When do balanced subgraphs appear?

After having proved
Lemma

4.8
, it remains to give a criterion for when balanced subgraphs actually appear in a
G\sim{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
. We formalize this in
Lemma

4.10
which on a high level states the following. If we fix a
T\in\binom{[n]}{t}
and use a procedure that constructs a bipartite sub-graph
G^{\prime}=G[A\uplus B]
of
G
while only having access to the information of whether a vertex has chosen a label in a set
L\subseteq[d]
or not, then
G[A\uplus(B\cap N_{G}({T}))]
has good balancedness properties if no vertex in
A
has chosen a label in
L
, and not too many vertices in
T
have chosen any given
\ell\in[d]\setminus L
.

As a natural example consider the case where
L
only contains a single label
\ell
. Then we can think of the procedure described above as some algorithm that outputs a bipartition
A,B
such that
A\cap S_{\ell}=\emptyset
. If every label
\ell^{\prime}\in[d]\setminus\{\ell\}
now appears in only a limited number of vertices in
T
, then
G[A\uplus(B\cap N_{G}({T}))]
has good balancedness.

Formally, we represent a procedure for choosing a such bipartition as an algorithm
Alg
that takes as input two disjoint sets (
S_{\ell}
, and
[n]\setminus S_{\ell}
) and outputs a bipartition
A\uplus B
such that
S_{\ell}\cap A=\emptyset
or
S_{\ell}\cap B=\emptyset
. We write
\textsc{Alg}(S_{\ell},[n]\setminus S_{\ell})
to denote this bipartition
A\uplus B
.

Definition 4.9

(Procedures for Choosing a Bipartition)
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
, fix a subset
M\subseteq[n]
, and let
\{\textsc{Alg}_{i}\}_{i\in[m]}
be any collection of (possibly randomized) algorithms that each on the input of a set
S\subseteq[n]
and
[n]
output a bipartition of
M
. Denote the bipartition computed by
\textsc{Alg}_{i}
by
A_{i}\uplus B_{i}
or alternatively
\textsc{Alg}_{i}(S,[n])
.
If
A_{i}\cap S=\emptyset
for all
i\in[m]
, we call the collection
\{\textsc{Alg}_{i}\}_{i\in[m]}
a
valid
collection of procedures for choosing a bipartiton.

The following lemma states that given any fixed collection of such algorithms
\{\textsc{Alg}_{i}\}_{i\in[m]}
and some assignment from
t
-tuples
T\in\binom{[n]}{t}
to sets
L(T)\subseteq[d]
, it holds w.h.p. over the draw of
G\sim{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
, that the bipartite graphs
\{G[A_{i}\uplus(B_{i}\cap N_{G}({T}))]\}_{i\in[m]}
are all simultaneously balanced for all
t
-tuples
T
such that any label
\ell\in[d]\setminus L(T)
only appears in at most
\eta
vertices of
T
.

Lemma 4.10

(General Criterion for Balancedness of sub-graphs of
G\sim{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
)
.

Let
t,\eta\in\mathbb{N},t\geqslant 3
be fixed constants and let further
\{\textsc{Alg}_{i}\}_{i\in[m]}
be a valid collection of procedures for choosing a bipartition according to
Definition

4.9
for some
m\leqslant n
. Moreover, associate to each
T\in\binom{[n]}{t}
a set
L(T)\subseteq[d]
, and denote by
S(T)\coloneqq\bigcup_{\ell\in L(T)}S_{\ell}
. For a given
T
and
i\in[m]
, denote by
A_{i}\uplus B_{i}
the bipartition output by
\textsc{Alg}(S(T),[n])
.

Then, with high probability over the draw of
G\sim{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
and the randomness in
\{\textsc{Alg}_{i}\}_{i\in[m]}
, the following holds. For all
t
-tuples
T\in\binom{[n]}{t}
such that no label in
[d]\setminus L(T)
appears in more than
\eta
vertices of
T
, the bipartite graphs
G[A_{i}\uplus(B_{i}\cap N_{G}({T}))]
have
r
-fold balancedness

\frac{16p^{-\eta}}{\alpha}\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{r}\cdot tp^{t}k+o(k)

for all
i\in[m]
and
3\leqslant r\leqslant t
.

Proof.

First, fix some
T\in\binom{[n]}{t}
(we shall later afford a union bound over all
T
) and think of sampling
G\sim{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
in 3 phases as follows.

1.

Phase 1:

Reveal the labels in
T
. This uncovers the set
L(T)
.

2.

Phase 2:

Reveal for all vertices
v\in[n]
the labels in
L(T)
. This uncovers the set
S(T)
.

3.

Phase 3:
Reveal all the remaining labels.

Conditioned on the event that no label in
[d]
appears in more than
\eta
vertices in
T
after Phase 1, we now show that all the
G[A_{i}\uplus(B_{i}\cap N_{G}({T}))]
are balanced after Phase 3 w.h.p.

We will later use a union bound over all
m
algorithms in
\{\textsc{Alg}_{i}\}_{i\in[m]}
, so it suffices that the desired balancedness properties hold for any fixed
Alg
in the collection
\{\textsc{Alg}_{i}\}_{i\in[m]}
after Phase 3 with sufficiently high probability. To this end, let
Alg
be one fixed such algorithm and split Phase 3 into three sub-phases as follows.

1.

Phase 3.1:

Compute the bipartition
A\uplus B=\textsc{Alg}(S(T),[n])
.

2.

Phase 3.2:

Reveal all the remaining labels for vertices in
A\setminus T
.

3.

Phase 3.3:

Reveal all the remaining labels for vertices in
B\setminus T
.

To show that
G[A\uplus(B\cap N_{G}({T}))]
is balanced, after these phases, we wish to use
Lemma

4.8
, and proceed by showing that the prerequisites for this are met after Phase 3.2 with probability
1-O(n^{-2t})
.

1.

For condition one of
Lemma

4.8
, we note that after Phase 3.2 all labels in
A\cup T
are revealed, while in
B
only the labels in
L(T)
have been uncovered. Thus, prerequisite one is met for
L=L(T)
.

2.

Regarding the second condition, note that no vertex in
A
has chosen label
L(T)
, by the fact that
Alg
is valid according to
Definition

4.9
.

3.

The third condition is met since we condition on the event that that no label in
[d]
appears in more than
\eta
vertices in
T
after Phase 1.

4.

For condition four, we get from
Lemma

4.7
that
\mathcal{E}(A,t,\frac{8t}{\alpha})
and
\mathcal{E}(T,t,\frac{8t}{\alpha})
are met with probability
1-O(n^{-2t})
.

5.

For condition five, we note that by the occurrence of
\mathcal{E}(T,t,\frac{8t}{\alpha})
, the number of duplicate labels in
T
is already at most
\log(n)^{2}
, and the total number of labels is
\leqslant 2t\delta d
. Hence, for every fixed
S,R\subseteq A
with
|S|=|R|=r
, the expected number of additional duplicate labels in
S\cup R\cup T
is at most
2t\delta^{2}d+|S\cup R|^{2}\delta^{2}d=O(1)
. Using a Chernoff bound like in the proof of
Lemma

4.7
, we get that the probability that there are at least
\log(n)^{2}
additional duplicates is
n^{-\omega(1)}
.

6.

Finally, for condition 6, we note that a simple Chernoff bound with subsequent union bound in Phase 2 yields that for all labels
\ell\in L(T)
, the number of vertices that choose
\ell
in step 2 is at most
2k
with probability
1-n^{-2t}
.

Hence, applying
Lemma

4.8
after Phase 3.2 (over the randomness in Phase 3.3), we get that
G[A_{i}\uplus(B_{i}\cap N_{G}({T}))]
has
r
-fold balancedness

\displaystyle(1+o(1))\frac{8t}{\alpha}p^{-\eta}\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{r}\cdot tp^{t}k+o(k)\leqslant\frac{16p^{-\eta}}{\alpha}\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{r}\cdot tp^{t}k+o(k)

for all
3\leqslant r\leqslant t
with probability
1-n^{-\omega(1)}
. Taking a union bound over all
m\leqslant n
elements in
\{\textsc{Alg}_{i}\}_{i\in[m]}
, shows that all the
G[A_{i}\uplus(B_{i}\cap N_{G}({T}))]
have the desired balancedness properties after Phase 3 given that no label in
[d]\setminus L(T)
appears in more than
\eta
vertices of
T
, and this holds with probability
1-O(mn^{-2t})
.
A final union bound over all
T
yields that our lemma holds with probability
1-O(n^{-t+1})
, as desired.
∎

A useful implication of the above used multiple times when analysing our algorithms is the following statement telling us that for
most

t
tuples in any
S_{\ell}
, the graph
G[([n]\setminus S_{\ell})\uplus([n]\cap N_{G}({T}))]
has good balancedness.

Lemma 4.11

(Balancedness for most
T\subseteq S_{\ell}
)
.

Let
t\in\mathbb{N},t\geqslant 6
be any fixed constant and let further
\{\textsc{Alg}_{i}\}_{i\in[m]}
be a valid collection of procedures for choosing a bipartition according to
Definition

4.9
. For a given
\ell\in[d]
and
T\in\binom{S_{\ell}}{t}
and
i\in[m]
, denote by
A_{i,\ell}\uplus B_{i,\ell}
the bipartition output by
\textsc{Alg}(S_{\ell},[n])
.

Then, it holds with probability
1-o(1)
over the randomness of
G\sim{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
and
\{\textsc{Alg}_{i}\}_{i\in[m]}
that for all labels
\ell\in[d]
, a
1-o(1)
fraction of all
t
-tuples
T\in\binom{S_{\ell}}{t}
is such that
G[A_{i,\ell}\uplus(B_{i,\ell}]
has
r
-fold balancedness

\frac{16p^{-6}}{\alpha}\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{r}\cdot tp^{t}k+o(k)

for all
i\in[m]
and
3\leqslant r\leqslant t
.

Proof.

First of all, applying
Lemma

4.10
with
\eta=6
and
L(T)=\{\ell\}
and
S(T)=[n]\setminus\{\ell\}
and union bounding over all
\ell
yields that our conclusion holds with probability
1-O(dn^{-t+1})
for all
\ell\in[d]
and
T\in\binom{S_{\ell}}{t}
such that at most
6
vertices in
T
have chosen any
\ell^{\prime}\in[d]\setminus\{\ell\}
.

Hence, it only remains to show that a
(1-o(1))
fraction of all
t
-tuples have this property. To this end, we simply note that when uncovering the labels of any given
T\subseteq S_{\ell}
, the probability that there is a label in
\ell^{\prime}\in[d]\setminus\{\ell\}
that is chosen by at least
6
vertices in
T
is at most

d\binom{t}{6}\delta^{6}=O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{dt^{6}}{\sqrt{d}^{6}}}}\right)=O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{d^{2}}}}\right).

Hence, the expected fraction of
t
tuples in
S_{\ell}
for which this bad event occurs is
O(1/d^{2})
. By Markov’s inequality, we get that with probability
1-O(1/(d\sqrt{d}))
, at most a
1/(d\sqrt{d})=o(1)
fraction of such
t
-tuples exists in
S_{\ell}
. Union bounding over all
\ell\in[d]
yields that this holds for all
\ell
with probability
1-O(1/\sqrt{d})=1-o(1)
, as desired.
∎

4.4
Cliques Intersecting other cliques

Another crucial set of statements used in multiple places throughout this work concerns showing that cliques do (with high probability) not occur in certain parts of a
G\sim{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
.

To this end, we are crucially interested in the following event

Definition 4.12
.

Define the event
\mathcal{E}_{a,b}
as the event that there exist two sets
S,T\subseteq[n]
in a
G\sim{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
with the following properties (see also
Figure

2
).

1.

S\cap T=\emptyset
,
|S|=a,|T|=b

2.

There is a label
\ell\in[d]
such that all vertices in
S
have chosen
\ell
, while
none
of the vertices in
T
has chosen
\ell
.

3.

All edges in
S\times T
are part of
G\sim{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
.

We show that
\mathcal{E}_{a,b}
is extremely unlikely when choosing some small
b=\text{polylog}(n)
and
a\geqslant\frac{n^{\varepsilon}k}{\sqrt{d}}
for arbitrarily small
\varepsilon>0
. We remark that this order of magnitude is almost optimal for
a
, since for all
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in[d]
, among all
\approx k
vertices that have chosen label
\ell_{1}
, there will be a set of
\approx k/\sqrt{d}
vertices which has also chosen
\ell_{2}
. Accordingly, choosing
S
as this subset and
T
as an arbitrary set of vertices which have chosen
\ell_{2}
but not
\ell_{1}
contradicts
\overline{\mathcal{E}_{a,b}}
for
a\approx k/\sqrt{d}
and all
b\ll k
w.h.p.

Figure 2:
(a) Sketch of event
\mathcal{E}_{a,b}
. Vertices
S_{\ell}
(yellow area) is the set of vertices with label
\ell
. The set of vertices
S
(hatched area) is a subset of
S_{\ell}
, while no vertex of
T
(red area) has label
\ell
. Any vertex of
T
has all possible edges the set
S
. (b) Illustration of revealing three label revealing phases in
Lemma

4.13
1 (yellow), 2 (red) and 3 (blue). The grey area represents a set
R
with
b
vertices with bounded duplicate labels in
4.14
.

Lemma 4.13
.

For any
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
d=n^{\alpha}
, we get that
\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{E}_{a,b}}}\right)=n^{-\omega(1)}
for all
a\geqslant\frac{n^{\varepsilon}k}{\sqrt{d}}
and
b=\lfloor\log(n)^{2}\rfloor
.

Proof.

For any label
\ell\in[d]
, denote by
\mathcal{E}_{a,b,\ell}
the event that there exist sets
S\subseteq S_{\ell},T\subseteq[n]\setminus S_{\ell}
with
|S|=a,|T|=b
such that all edges in
S\times T
are part of
G
. Clearly, if we show that
\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{E}_{a,b,\ell}}}\right)\leqslant n^{-\omega(1)}
for all
\ell\in[d]
, then a simple union bound implies that
\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{E}_{a,b}}}\right)\leqslant n^{-\omega(1)}
and we are done. Hence, for the rest of the proof, we fix an arbitrary
\ell\in[d]
and show that
\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{E}_{a,b,\ell}}}\right)\leqslant O(1/d^{2})
.

Given a fixed
\ell
, uncover the labels of
G\sim{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
in the following three phases

1.

Phase 1:
Uncover the label
\ell
for all
v\in[n]
and thus reveal the set
S_{\ell}
.

2.

Phase 2:
Uncover the missing labels for vertices in
[n]\setminus S_{\ell}
.

3.

Phase 3:
Uncover the missing labels for vertices in
S_{\ell}
.

First, uncover the set
S_{\ell}
, then uncover the missing labels of all vertices in
[n]\setminus S_{\ell}
, and finally uncover the missing labels of all vertices in
S_{\ell}
. We show that the following event occurs w.h.p. after Phase 2 (see also
Figure

2
) asserting that the number of duplicate labels is small in every set of vertices of size
b
.

Claim 4.14
.

There is a constant
C>0
such that with probability
1-n^{-\omega(1)}
, the following occurs after Phase 2. For all sets
R\subseteq[n]\setminus S_{\ell}
with
|R|=b
, there are at most
C\log(n)^{4}
labels that were chosen by at least two vertices in
R
, such labels are called
duplicates
. Moreover, for every
v\in[n]\setminus S_{\ell}
,
|M_{v}|=\delta d\pm\log(n)\sqrt{\delta d}
.

Proof.

Given a fixed label
\ell^{\prime}\in[d]\setminus\{\ell\}
, the probability that at least 2 vertices in any fixed
R
choose
\ell^{\prime}
is at most

\binom{b}{2}\ \delta^{2}=O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\log(n)^{2}}{\sqrt{d}}}}\right)^{2}.

Hence the expected number of labels for which this happens is
O(\log(n)^{4})
. Now, applying a Chernoff bound yields that the probability that the number of such labels is
\omega(\log(n)^{4})
is at most
n^{-\log(n)^{3}}
.

Finally, a union bound over all
\binom{n}{b}\leqslant n^{\log(n)^{2}}
sets yields the first part of the claim. The second part follows directly by
Lemma

4.3
.
∎

Given this fact, we now bound the probability that all edges between some
T\subseteq[n]\setminus S_{\ell}
with
|T|=b
and some
S\subseteq S_{\ell}
with
|S|\geqslant a
exist after Phase 3. For a fixed
v\in S
, the probability that all edges between
v
and
S
exist is at most

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v\sim T}}\right)\leqslant C\log(n)^{4}\delta+\big(p+O(\log(n)\delta\sqrt{d\delta})\big)^{b}

where the first term accounts for the possibility that
v
choses a duplicate in
T
. The second term accounts for the event that this does not happen, here we applied
Lemma

4.2
and noted that all edges between
v
and
T
are independent if no duplicate is chosen. Noting that
\log(n)\delta\sqrt{d\delta}=n^{-\Omega(1)}
since
d\geqslant n^{\alpha}
, and that
p\leqslant 1/2
yields that
\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v\sim T}}\right)\leqslant C\log(n)^{4}\delta+(1+o(1))p^{b}
. Since (conditional on the fixed lables in
T
), the event
v\sim T
is independent for all
v\in S_{\ell}
, we get that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S\sim T}}\right)\leqslant\big(C\log(n)^{4}\delta+(1+o(1))p^{b}\big)^{a}=O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\log(n)^{4}}{\sqrt{d}}}}\right)^{a}

since
\delta=O(1/\sqrt{d})
and
p^{b}=o(1/\sqrt{d})
because
b=\Omega(\log(n)^{2})
. Now, noting that
|S_{\ell}|\leqslant 2k
with probability
1-n^{-\omega(1)}
(by using a Chernoff bound in conjunction with expected number of vertices with label
\ell
being
\delta n=k=n^{\Omega(1)}
) after Phase 1, and employing a union bound over all
S
and
T
in Phase 3 yields (by the law of total probability) that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{E}_{a,b,\ell}}}\right)-n^{-\omega(1)}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{m=a}^{2k}\binom{2k}{m}\binom{n}{b}O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\log(n)^{4}}{\sqrt{d}}}}\right)^{m}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{m=a}^{2k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{4ek}{m}}}\right)^{m}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2en}{b}}}\right)^{b}O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\log(n)^{4}}{\sqrt{d}}}}\right)^{m}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{m=a}^{2k}O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{k\log(n)^{4}}{m\sqrt{d}}}}\right)^{m}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2en}{b}}}\right)^{b}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{m=a}^{2k}\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(m\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{k}{m\sqrt{d}}}}\right)+O(m\log\log(n))+O(b\log(n))}}\right).

Since
k/(m\sqrt{d})\leqslant n^{-\varepsilon}
by our assumption
a\geqslant n^{\varepsilon}k/\sqrt{d}
and
a\gg b
, we get that the above is at most

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{E}_{a,b,\ell}}}\right)\leqslant\sum_{m=a}^{2k}\exp(-(1-o(1))\varepsilon m\log(n))\leqslant n^{-\omega(1)}.

A final union bound over all
\ell\in[d]
then finishes the proof.
∎

We further bound the maximum degree any vertex outside of a ground-truth clique
S_{\ell}
can have into
S_{\ell}
.

Lemma 4.15
.

With high probability over the draw of
G\sim{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
, the following holds. For all
\ell\in[d]
and all
v\in[n]\setminus S_{\ell}
, the number of neighbors of
v
in
S_{\ell}
is at most
(1+o(1))pk
.

Proof.

Fix a label
\ell\in[d]
and reveal the labels of
[n]
in the same three phases we used in the proof of
Lemma

4.13
. Fixing a vertex
v\in[n]\setminus S_{\ell}
, we obtain via
Lemma

4.3
that
|M_{v}|
satisfies the requirement of
Lemma

4.2
after Phase 2 with probability
1-n^{-\omega(1)}
. Then, making use of
Lemma

4.2
, it follows that for any
u\in S_{\ell}
that
\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(u\sim v}}\right)\leqslant(1+o(1))p
over the randomness in Phase 3. Moreover, via a Chernoff bound we obtain that
|S_{\ell}|\leqslant(1+o(1))k
with probability
1-n^{-\omega(1)}
. Using another Chernoff bound it then follows that
v
has at most
(1+o(1))pk
with probability
1-n^{-\omega(1)}
. In conclusion, our fixed vertex
v
has at most
(1+o(1))pk
neighbours in
S_{\ell}
for our fixed label
\ell
with probability
1-n^{-\omega(1)}
by a union bound over the complementary events of the ones we considered throughout the three phases. Hence, a union bound over all
|[n]\setminus S_{\ell}|\leqslant n
vertices yields that the event that there exists a vertex
v\in[n]\setminus S_{\ell}
where the size of the neighbourhood in
S_{\ell}
exceeds
(1+o(1))pk
has probability at most
n^{-\omega(1)}
. Finally, another union bound over all
d
labels finishes the proof. ∎

5
Using Balancedness to Certify the Absence of Large (Bi)cliques

After having established criteria for when balanced bipartitions appear in an RIG, we go over to showing how these properties can be used to certify the absence of (bi)cliques within the SoS-proof system.

5.1
Preliminaries on SoS and Pseudo-Distributions

We start with some preliminaries on SoS-proofs, pseudo-distributions and their algorithmic feasibility. We refer to the book
[
36
]
for a more comprehensive introduction.

Pseudo-distributions

Given two functions
\mu,f:\{0,1\}^{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, we define the
pseudo-expectation
of
f
under
\mu
as
\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[f}}\right]=\sum_{x\in\{0,1\}^{m}}\mu(x)f(x)
. A degree-
d
pseudo-distribution is a function
\mu:\{0,1\}^{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that
\sum_{x\in\{0,1\}^{m}}\mu(x)=1
and
for every
polynomial of degree at most
d/2
, we have
\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[f^{2}}}\right]\geqslant 0
. Given a set
\mathcal{A}=\{f_{1}\geqslant 0,f_{2}\geqslant 0,\ldots,f_{m^{\prime}}\geqslant 0\}
of polynomial inequality constraints, we say that a degree-
d
pseudo-distribution satisfies the system of constraints
\mathcal{A}
at degree
r
if
for every
S\subseteq[m^{\prime}]
and every polynomial
g
such that
\deg(g^{2})+\sum_{i\in S}\max\{\deg(f_{i}),r\}\leqslant d
, we have
\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[g^{2}\prod_{i\in S}f_{i}}}\right]\geqslant 0
. Given some
\eta>0
, we say that the pseudo-distribution
\mu
satisfies the system
\mathcal{A}

\eta
-approximately at degree
r
if
\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[g^{2}\prod_{i\in S}f_{i}}}\right]\geqslant-\eta\|g^{2}\|_{2}\prod_{i\in S}\|f_{i}\|_{2}
again for every polynomial
g
such that
\deg(g^{2})+\sum_{i\in S}\deg(f_{i})\leqslant d
. Here
\|f\|_{2}
denotes the
\ell_{2}
-norm of the coefficient vector of the polynomial
f
. We say that
\mu
(
\eta
-approximately) satisfies
\mathcal{A}
if it does so at degree
0
.

In this setting, it has been observed that there exists an efficient algorithm that given a system of polynomial constraints
\mathcal{A}
computes a degree-
d
pseudo distribution that approximately satisfies
\mathcal{A}
in polynomial time.

Theorem 5.1

(Efficient optimization over pseudo-distributions
[
64
,
61
,
49
]
)
.

There exists an algorithm that takes as input a system of
m
polynomial constraints
\mathcal{A}
in
n
variables with rational coefficients that for every
\eta>0
outputs a degree-
d
pseudo-distribution that
\eta
-approximately satisfies
\mathcal{A}
. It runs in time
(n+m)^{O(d)}\text{polylog}(1/\eta)
.

We further use the following standard fact about pseudo-distributions (see
[
3
, Theorem 2]
).

Lemma 5.2

(Hölder’s Inequality for pseudo-distributions)
.

Let
f,g
be polynomial of degree at most
d
. Then, for any
t\in\mathbb{N}
and any degree-
dt
pseudo-distribution
\mu
, the following holds.

\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[f^{t-1}g}}\right]\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[f^{t}}}\right]}}\right)^{(t-1)/t}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[g^{t}}}\right]}}\right)^{1/t}.

Sum-of-Squares proofs

Very useful for analysing pseudo-distributions are
sum-of-squares proofs
. A degree-
d
sum-of-squares (SoS) proof that the system of constraints
\mathcal{A}=\{f_{1}\geqslant 0,\ldots,f_{m}\geqslant 0\}
implies the constraint
\{g\geqslant 0\}
is a collection
\{p_{S}\}_{S\subseteq[m]}
of sum-of-squares polynomials (i.e. polynomials that can be represented as the sum of squared polynomials) such that

g=\sum_{S\subseteq[m]}p_{S}\cdot\prod_{i\in S}f_{i}
, while for all
S\subseteq[m]
, the polynomial
p_{S}\cdot\prod_{i\in S}f_{i}
has degree at most
d
. We write
\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.53467pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{d}$}}}$}}}\{g\geqslant 0\}
to express that there is a degree-
s
SoS-proof in variables
x
that
\mathcal{A}
implies
\{g\geqslant 0\}
.

The connection to pseudo-distributions is given by the following fact which states that whenever there is a degree-
d
SoS-proof that
\mathcal{A}
implies
\{g\geqslant 0\}
, then every degree-
d
pseudo distribution that satisfies
\mathcal{A}
also satisfies
\{g\geqslant 0\}
.

Fact 5.3

(Soundness)
.

If
\mu
is a degree-
d
pseudo distribution that (approximately) satisfies the system of constraints
\mathcal{A}
and if
\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.53467pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{r}$}}}$}}}\{g\geqslant 0\}
, then
\mu
satisfies
\{g\geqslant 0\}
at degree
r
.

Moreover, we have to constrain the so-called
bit-complexity
of a SoS-proof, in order to ensure that the above soundness is meaningfully reflected in pseudo-distributions that can be computed in polynomial time.

Definition 5.4

(Bit-complexity of SoS-proofs)
.

We say that the system of constraints
\mathcal{A}=\{f_{1}\geqslant 0,f_{2}\geqslant 0,\ldots,f_{m}\geqslant 0\}
derives
\{g\geqslant 0\}
in degree
d
and bit-complexity
B
if there is a degree-
d
sum-of-squares proof
g=\sum_{S\subseteq[m]}p_{S}\cdot\prod_{i\in S}f_{i}
such that the total number of bits required to describe all the coefficients of the polynomials
f_{i},g,p_{S}
is at most
B
.

We further introduce some useful SoS-proofs that we shall use later.
The following is taken from
[
21
, Lemma 3.13]
with minor modifications.

Lemma 5.5

(SoS Cancellation Inequalities)
.

Let
f
be a polynomial of degree
d
in indeterminate
x
. Then,

\displaystyle\{f^{2}\leqslant Cf\}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{12.14894pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-12.14894pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[12.14894pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-12.14894pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[12.14894pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2d}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{f^{2}\leqslant C^{2}}}\right\}

\displaystyle\{f^{2}\leqslant C\}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{12.14894pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-12.14894pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[12.14894pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-12.14894pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[12.14894pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2d}$}}}$}}}\{f\leqslant\sqrt{C}\}

Proof.

For the first part, note that

\{f^{2}\leqslant Cf\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{12.14894pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-12.14894pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[12.14894pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-12.14894pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[12.14894pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2d}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{f^{2}\leqslant f^{2}+(f-C)^{2}=2f^{2}-2fC+C^{2}=C^{2}+2(f^{2}-Cf)\leqslant C^{2}}}\right\}.

Moreover, we note that
\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{12.14894pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-12.14894pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[12.14894pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-12.14894pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[12.14894pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2d}$}}}$}}}\{(f+\sqrt{C})^{2}\leqslant 2f^{2}+2C\}
, which allows us to conclude that

\displaystyle\{f^{2}\leqslant C\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{12.14894pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-12.14894pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[12.14894pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-12.14894pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[12.14894pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2d}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{f=\frac{1}{4\sqrt{C}}(f+\sqrt{C})^{2}-\frac{1}{4\sqrt{C}}(\sqrt{C}-f)^{2}\leqslant\frac{1}{2\sqrt{C}}f^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{C}\leqslant\sqrt{C}}}\right\}.

∎

Lemma 5.6

(SoS Almost Triangle Inequality, Fact 3.9 in
[
21
]
)
.

Let
f_{1},f_{2},\ldots,f_{s}
be indeterminates for
1\leqslant i\leqslant s
and some
s\in\mathbb{N}
. For any
t\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{44.00027pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-44.00027pt\raisebox{-8.96112pt}{\makebox[44.00027pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f_{1},f_{2},\ldots,f_{s}}$}}}\hskip-44.00027pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[44.00027pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{s}f_{i}}}\right)^{2t}\leqslant s^{2t-1}\sum_{i=1}^{s}f_{i}^{2t}}}\right\}.

We further use the following lemma that relates the sum over all variables
x_{S}
for
S\subseteq A
with
|S|=r
to
|x|^{r}
from

[
21
]
.

Lemma 5.7

(Lemma 4.10 in
[
21
]
)
.

For every
\varepsilon>0
,
r\in\mathbb{N}
there is an SoS-proof with coefficients of bit complexity
\text{poly}(|A|,\log(1/\varepsilon))
for the following statement.

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{x_{v}^{2}=x_{v}}}\right\}_{v\in A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.53467pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{r}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1}{2^{r}r!}|x|^{r}-\frac{2r^{r}}{r!}-\varepsilon\leqslant\sum_{S\subseteq A,|S|=r}x_{S}\leqslant\frac{1}{r!}|x|^{r}+\varepsilon}}\right\}.

Related to this, we use the following proposition, which is occasionaly more convenient than the above.

Lemma 5.8
.

There is an SoS proof with bit complexity
\text{poly}(|A|,\log(1/\varepsilon))
for the following statement

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{x_{v}^{2}=x_{v}}}\right\}_{v\in A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.53467pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{r}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{S\subseteq A,|S|=r}x_{S}}}\right)=\frac{1}{r!}\prod_{i=1}^{r}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{v\in A}x_{v}}}\right)-(i-1)}}\right)}}\right\}.

Consquently, for any
s\in\mathbb{N}
,

\bigg\{\sum_{v\in A}x_{v}\geqslant s\bigg\}\cup\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{x_{v}^{2}=x_{v}}}\right\}_{v\in A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.53467pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{r}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{S\subseteq A,|S|=r}x_{S}}}\right)\geqslant\binom{s}{r}}}\right\}.

Proof.

It is not hard to see that under the set of axioms
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{x_{v}^{2}=x_{v}}}\right\}_{v\in A}
when evaluating the product
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{S\subseteq A,|S|=r}x_{S}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{v\in A}x_{v}}}\right)
, we enumerate each set
S\subseteq A
with
|S|=r+1
for
r+1
times and each
S\subseteq A
with
|S|=r
for
r
times. Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{x_{v}^{2}=x_{v}}}\right\}_{v\in A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{18.05324pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-18.05324pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[18.05324pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-18.05324pt\raisebox{2.79446pt}{\makebox[18.05324pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{r+1}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{S\subseteq A,|S|=r+1}x_{S}}}\right)=\frac{1}{r+1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{S\subseteq A,|S|=r}x_{S}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{v\in A}x_{v}}}\right)-r}}\right)}}\right\}.

Iterating this argument yields the first part of the lemma. The second part of the lemma then follows by definition of the binomial coefficient.
∎

5.2
Systems of Axioms for Encoding Cliques

We use the following axioms to describe cliques and bicliques that serve as starting points for our SoS proofs.
The most commonly used axioms for encoding a clique of size
k
at least
k
(and at most
2k
) are as follows (see for example also
[
21
]
).

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(G,k)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} &\forall v\in V&x_{v}^{2}&=x_{v}\\
&&k\leqslant\sum_{v\in A}x_{v}&\leqslant 2k\\
&\forall u,v\in V\text{ s.t. }\{u,v\}\not\in E&x_{u}x_{v}&=0\end{aligned}}}\right\}\,.

Above, we have one variable
x_{v}
for each
v\in V
indicating whether or not
v
participates in a clique or not.

Very similar axioms
\mathcal{B}
can be used in a bipartite graph
H=(A\uplus B,E_{H})
to find a biclique of size at least
k\times k
. Now, we denote the variables for vertices
v\in A
by
x_{v}
and variables for vertices
v\in B
by
y_{v}
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}(H,k)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} &\forall v\in A&x_{v}^{2}&=x_{v}\\
&\forall v\in B&y_{v}^{2}&=y_{v}\\
&&k\leqslant\sum_{v\in A}x_{v}&\leqslant 2k\\
&&k\leqslant\sum_{v\in A}y_{v}&\leqslant 2k\\
&\forall u\in A,v\in B\text{ s.t. }\{u,v\}\notin E_{H}&x_{u}y_{v}&=0\end{aligned}}}\right\}.

Removing the size constraint from the above, we end up with what we call the
reduced biclique axioms

\mathcal{R}
which we use at some parts in our proofs.

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(H)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} &\forall v\in A&x_{v}^{2}&=x_{v}\\
&\forall v\in B&y_{v}^{2}&=y_{v}\\
&\forall u\in A,v\in B\text{ s.t. }\{u,v\}\notin E_{H}&x_{u}y_{v}&=0\end{aligned}}}\right\}.

For the proofs in
Section

8
, we further need the following constraints that specify not a clique but a dense sub-graph in which up to
\gamma
edges incident to all vertices can be missing. These are called
\mathcal{R}(H,\gamma)
and defined follows.

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(H,\gamma)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} &\forall v\in A&x_{v}^{2}&=x_{v}\\
&\forall v\in B&y_{v}^{2}&=y_{v}\\
&\forall u\in A,v\in B&z_{u,v}^{2}&=z_{u,v}\\
&\forall u\in A,v\in B\text{ s.t. }\{u,v\}\notin E_{H}&x_{u}y_{v}(1-z_{u,v})&=0\\
&\forall v\in A&\sum_{u\in B}z_{u,v}&\leqslant\gamma\end{aligned}}}\right\}.

Here, the additional variables
z_{u,v}
encode which edges to “add back” into the graph in order to obtain a clique.
The constraint
\sum_{u\in A}z_{u,v}\leqslant\gamma
ensures that only up to
\gamma
such edges can be added incident to every
u\in A
.

5.3
SoS Certificates in balanced bipartite graphs

The core of the analysis of all our algorithms is an SoS-certificate for the absence of bicliques in balanced bipartite graphs.
Before formally describing our certificates, we introduce some notation. Recall that given a bipartite graph
G=A\uplus B
, we denote variables for vertices
v\in A
by
x_{v}
and variables for vertices
v\in B
by
y_{v}
. We further denote by
x\in\mathbb{R}^{|A|}
the vector of all variables
x_{v}
for
v\in A
and by
y\in\mathbb{R}^{|B|}
the vector of all variables
y_{v}
for
v\in B
. Given a vector
x
of variables, we denote by
|x|
its
L_{1}
-norm. Moreover, for a set
S\subseteq[n]
, we use the abbreviation
x_{S}\coloneqq\prod_{i\in S}x_{i}
.

5.3.1
Core certificate

With this, we introduce the main lemma upon which our biclique certificates are built. It can be seen as a modified version of
[
21
, Lemma 4.11]
. The difference is that we have different balancedness guarantees since we only get meaningful balancedness if
|S\triangle R|\geqslant 3
instead of
|S\triangle R|\geqslant 1
as in
[
21
]
. Moreover, when using
\mathcal{R}(H,\gamma)
for non-zero
\gamma
, we further have to account for the fact that some edges within a “clique” can be missing. These factors require us to modify the proof of
[
21
, Lemma 4.11]
and result in a slightly different SoS-certificate than in previous work. The following is the core certificate we use for exact recovery and our refutation algorithms.

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
r\in\mathbb{N},r\geqslant 2
and
H=(A\uplus B,E)
be a bipartite graph which has
r
-wise balancedness. Recall that variables associated to
v\in A,u\in B
are denoted by
x_{v}
and
y_{u}
, respectively. Then,

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(H)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{15.10532pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x,y}$}}}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{4r}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1-p}{p}}}\right)^{r}|y|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{S\subseteq A,|S|=r}x_{S}}}\right)^{2}\\
&\hskip 56.9055pt\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n+rn^{2}}}\right)\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{r}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{S\subseteq A,|S|=r}x_{S}}}\right)+\Delta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{S\subseteq A,|S|=r}x_{S}}}\right)^{2}\end{aligned}}}\right\}.

The bit complexity of the proof is
n^{O(r)}
.

Remark 5.10

(Intuition)
.

To get a sense for what the above lemma tells us, let us for now assume
p=1/2
, and let us assume that
\sum_{S\subseteq A,|S|=r}x_{S}\approx|x|^{r}
for
|x|=\sum_{v\in A}x_{v}
, which is approximately true by
Lemma

5.7
. Then, re-arranging the above, we get a statement of the form
|x|^{2r}(|y|-\Delta)\leqslant(n+rn^{2})|x|^{r}
. While for
|y|<\Delta
, this statement is trivial, it is quite powerful whenever
|y|\gtrsim\Delta
, i.e., if the “right side” of our biclique is larger than our balancedness
\Delta
. In this case, the statement reduces to something like
|x|^{r}\leqslant n+rn^{2}
, which we can see as a certificate that the “left side” of our biclique is of size at most
O(n^{2/r})
.

To prove
Lemma

5.9
, we start with the following lemma that allows us to arrive at the left hand side of the certificate in
Lemma

5.9
.

Lemma 5.11
.

Let
r\in\mathbb{N},r\geqslant 2
and
H=(A\uplus B,E)
be a bipartite graph.
Recall the definition of

H_{p}(u,v)
and
u_{S,p}
from
Definition

4.4
. Then for any
S,R\subseteq A
with
|S|=|R|=r
,

\mathcal{R}(H)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{15.10532pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x,y}$}}}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{4r}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{x_{S\cup R}\sum_{v\in B}y_{v}u_{S,p}(v)u_{R,p}(v)=x_{S\cup R}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1-p}{p}}}\right)^{r}|y|}}\right\}.

Proof.

We note that the constraint
x_{u}y_{v}=0
for all
\{u,v\}\notin E
implies that for any
v\in B
,

\mathcal{R}(H)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{15.10532pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x,y}$}}}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{4r}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{x_{S\cup R}y_{v}u_{S,p}(v)u_{R,p}(v)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1-p}{p}}}\right)^{r}x_{S\cup R}y_{v}}}\right\}

since
u_{S,p}(v)u_{R,p}(v)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1-p}{p}}}\right)^{r}
whenever all edges between
v
and
S\cup R
are present in
H
. Summing over
v\in B
yields the statement.
∎

With this, we are ready to prove
Lemma

5.9
.

Proof of
Lemma

5.9
.

Recall the definition of

u_{S,p}
from
Definition

4.4
. For any
v\in B
, we now define
u^{\prime}_{S,p}(v)\coloneqq u_{S,p}(v)(1-y_{v})
. Using
Lemma

5.11
and further that
\mathcal{R}(H)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{15.10532pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x,y}$}}}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{4}$}}}$}}}\{(1-y_{v})^{2}=1-y_{v}\}
for any
v\in B
, we get that for all
S,R\subseteq A
with
|S|=|R|=r
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(H)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{15.10532pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x,y}$}}}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{4r}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} x_{S\cup R}\sum_{v\in B}u^{\prime}_{S,p}(v)u^{\prime}_{R,p}(v)&=x_{S\cup R}\sum_{v\in B}u_{S,p}(v)u_{R,p}(v)(1-y_{v})^{2}\\
&\leqslant x_{S\cup R}\sum_{v\in B}u_{S,p}(v)u_{R,p}(v)-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1-p}{p}}}\right)^{r}x_{S\cup R}|y|\end{aligned}}}\right\}.

By our balancedness assumption, we get that for any
S,R\subseteq A
with
|S|=|R|=r
such that
|S\triangle R|\geqslant 3
, we have
\sum_{v\in B}u_{S,p}(v)u_{R,p}(v)\leqslant\Delta
. Accordingly, for such
S,R
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(H)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{15.10532pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x,y}$}}}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{4r}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{x_{S\cup R}\langle u^{\prime}_{S,p},u^{\prime}_{R,p}\rangle\leqslant\Delta x_{S\cup R}-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1-p}{p}}}\right)^{r}|y|x_{S\cup R}}}\right\},

Otherwise (if
|S\triangle R|<3
), we use the trivial bound

\mathcal{R}(H)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{15.10532pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x,y}$}}}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{4r}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{x_{S\cup R}\langle u_{S,p},u_{R,p}\rangle\leqslant n\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{r}x_{S\cup R}}}\right\}.

With this,

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(H)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{22.03937pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-22.03937pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[22.03937pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x,y}$}}}\hskip-22.03937pt\raisebox{2.79446pt}{\makebox[22.03937pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{4r+2}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} 0&\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\sum_{|S|=r}x_{S}u^{\prime}_{S,p}}}\right\|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S,R\subseteq A,|S|,|R|=r\end{subarray}}x_{S\cup R}\langle u^{\prime}_{S,p},u^{\prime}_{R,p}\rangle\\
&\hskip 2.84544pt=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S,R\subseteq A,|S|,|R|=r\\
|S\triangle R|\leqslant 2\end{subarray}}x_{S\cup R}\langle u^{\prime}_{S,p},u^{\prime}_{R,p}\rangle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S,R\subseteq A,|S|,|R|=r\\
|S\triangle R|\geqslant 3\end{subarray}}x_{S\cup R}\langle u^{\prime}_{S,p},u^{\prime}_{R,p}\rangle\\
&\hskip 2.84544pt=n\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{r}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S,R\subseteq A,|S|,|R|=r\\
|S\triangle R|\leqslant 2\end{subarray}}x_{S\cup R}}}\right)-|y|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1-p}{p}}}\right)^{r}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S,R\subseteq A,|S|,|R|=r\\
|S\triangle R|\leqslant 2\end{subarray}}x_{S\cup R}}}\right)\\
&\hskip 56.9055pt+\Delta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S,T\subseteq A,|S|,|R|=r\\
|S\triangle R|\geqslant 3\end{subarray}}x_{S\cup R}}}\right)-|y|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1-p}{p}}}\right)^{r}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S,R\subseteq A,|S|,|R|=r\\
|S\triangle R|\geqslant 3\end{subarray}}x_{S\cup R}}}\right)\\
&\hskip 2.84544pt=n\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{r}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S,R\subseteq A,|S|,|R|=r\\
|S\triangle R|\leqslant 2\end{subarray}}x_{S\cup R}}}\right)+\triangle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S,T\subseteq A,|S|,|R|=r\\
|S\triangle R|\geqslant 3\end{subarray}}x_{S\cup R}}}\right)\\
&\hskip 170.71652pt-|y|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1-p}{p}}}\right)^{r}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S,R\subseteq A,|S|,|R|=r\end{subarray}}x_{S\cup R}}}\right)\\
&\hskip 2.84544pt\leqslant n\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{r}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S,R\subseteq A,|S|,|R|=r\\
|S\triangle R|\leqslant 2\end{subarray}}x_{S\cup R}}}\right)+\triangle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq A,|S|=r\end{subarray}}x_{S}}}\right)^{2}\\
&\hskip 170.71652pt-|y|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1-p}{p}}}\right)^{r}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq A,|S|=r\end{subarray}}x_{S}}}\right)^{2}\end{aligned}}}\right\}

Given a fixed
R\subseteq A
with
|R|=r
, we further get that
\mathcal{R}(H)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{15.10532pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x,y}$}}}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2r}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{x_{R}\leqslant 1}}\right\}
, so for any fixed
S\subseteq A
with
|S|=r

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(H)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{15.10532pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x,y}$}}}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{4r}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\subseteq A,|R|=r\\
|S\triangle T|\leqslant 2\end{subarray}}x_{R}\leqslant 1+rn}}\right\}

since the number of sets
R
with the desired properties is at most
1+rn
. Multiplying this with
x_{S}
and summing yields that

\mathcal{R}(H)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{15.10532pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x,y}$}}}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{4r}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{n\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{r}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq A,|S|,|R|=r\\
|S\triangle R|\leqslant 2\end{subarray}}x_{S\cup R}}}\right)\leqslant(n+rn^{2})\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{r}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq A,|S|=r\end{subarray}}x_{S}}}\right)}}\right\},

so in total,

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(H)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{15.10532pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x,y}$}}}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{4r}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} 0&\leqslant(n+rn^{2})\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{r}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{S\subseteq A,|S|=r}x_{S}}}\right)+\Delta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq A,|S|=r\end{subarray}}x_{S}}}\right)^{2}\\
&\hskip 256.0748pt-|y|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1-p}{p}}}\right)^{r}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq A,|S|=r\end{subarray}}x_{S}}}\right)^{2}\end{aligned}}}\right\},

as desired.
∎

5.3.2
Core certificate for the adversarial version

At the cost of a higher degree, we can get a similar statement for the axioms
\mathcal{R}(H,\gamma)
that we use for approximate recovery against a bounded adversary.

Lemma 5.12
.

Let
r\in\mathbb{N},r\geqslant 2
and
H=(A\uplus B,E)
be a bipartite graph which has
r
-wise balancedness. Recall that variables associated to
v\in A,u\in B
are denoted by
x_{v}
and
y_{u}
, respectively. Then,

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(H,\gamma)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{15.10532pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x,y}$}}}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{8r}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1-p}{p}}}\right)^{r}(|y|-2\gamma r)\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{S\subseteq A,|S|=r}x_{S}}}\right)^{2}\\
&\hskip 56.9055pt\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n+rn^{2}}}\right)\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{r}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{S\subseteq A,|S|=r}x_{S}}}\right)+\Delta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{S\subseteq A,|S|=r}x_{S}}}\right)^{2}\end{aligned}}}\right\}.

The bit complexity of the proof is
n^{O(r)}
.

In order to establish the stronger version
Lemma

5.11
of
Lemma

5.12
, we use the following modification of
Lemma

5.12
that incorporates the effect of the additional variables
z_{u,v}
in
\mathcal{R}(H,\gamma)
and intuitively states that SoS “knows” that the balancedness properties of a grah can only change by a limited amount if we restrict the number of edges being added “back in” incident to every vertex.

Lemma 5.13
.

Let
r\in\mathbb{N},r\geqslant 2
and
H=(A\uplus B,E)
be a bipartite graph. Recall the definition of

H_{p}(u,v)
from
Definition

4.4
. For any
S\subseteq A
and
v\in B
, define

u_{S,p}^{(z)}(v)\coloneqq\prod_{u\in S}(1-z_{u,v})H_{p}(u,v).

Then for any
S,R\subseteq A
with
|S|=|R|=r
,

\mathcal{R}(H,\gamma)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{22.03937pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-22.03937pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[22.03937pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x,y}$}}}\hskip-22.03937pt\raisebox{2.79446pt}{\makebox[22.03937pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{8r+2}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{x_{S\cup R}\sum_{v\in B}y_{v}u_{S,p}^{(z)}(v)u_{R,p}^{(z)}(v)\geqslant x_{S\cup R}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1-p}{p}}}\right)^{r}(|y|-2\gamma r)}}\right\}.

Proof.

Due to the constraint
\{x_{u}y_{v}(1-z_{u,v})=0\}
for all
(u,v)\in A\times B
such that
\{u,v\}\notin E
, we get that

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(H,\gamma)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{15.10532pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x,y}$}}}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{8r}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} x_{S\cup R}y_{v}u_{S,p}^{(z)}(v)u_{R,p}^{(z)}(v)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1-p}{p}}}\right)^{r}x_{S\cup R}y_{v}\prod_{u\in S}(1-z_{u,v})\prod_{u\in R}(1-z_{u,v})\end{aligned}}}\right\}.

We wish to find a lower bound on the right hand side. We show how to obtain a such bound for
\prod_{u\in S}(1-z_{u,v})
in the following claim

Claim 5.14
.

For any set of vertices
M
,

\{z_{u,v}=z_{u,v}^{2}\}_{u,v\in[n]}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.91432pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.91432pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.91432pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2r}$}}}\hskip-11.91432pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.91432pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{z}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\prod_{u\in M}(1-z_{u,v})\geqslant 1-\sum_{u\in M}z_{u,v}}}\right\}

Proof.

Fix an arbitrary ordering
u_{1},\ldots,u_{r}
of the vertices in
M
and note that

\prod_{u\in M}(1-z_{u,v})=\prod_{i=1}^{r}(1-z_{u_{i},v})=\prod_{i=1}^{r-1}(1-z_{u_{i},v})-z_{u_{1},v}\prod_{i=1}^{r-1}(1-z_{u_{i},v}).

Since
\{z_{u,v}=z_{u,v}^{2}\}_{u,v\in[n]}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.10762pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.10762pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[8.10762pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-8.10762pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.10762pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{z}$}}}$}}}\{z_{u,v}>0\}
, we get

\{z_{u,v}=z_{u,v}^{2}\}_{u,v\in[n]}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.91432pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.91432pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.91432pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2r}$}}}\hskip-11.91432pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.91432pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{z}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\prod_{u\in M}(1-z_{u,v})\geqslant\prod_{i=1}^{r-1}(1-z_{u_{i},v})-z_{u_{1},v}}}\right\}.

Iterating this argument yields the claim.
∎

With the above, we get

\{z_{u,v}=z_{u,v}^{2}\}_{u,v\in[n]}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.91432pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.91432pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.91432pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2r}$}}}\hskip-11.91432pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.91432pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{z}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\prod_{u\in S}(1-z_{u,v})\prod_{u\in R}(1-z_{u,v})\geqslant 1-\sum_{u\in S}z_{u,v}-\sum_{u\in R}z_{u,v}}}\right\}

Thus, using the same argument for
R
, the axiom
\sum_{v\in B}z_{u,v}\leqslant\gamma
, and summing over
v\in B
,

\mathcal{R}(H,\gamma)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.91432pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.91432pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[11.91432pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{z}$}}}\hskip-11.91432pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.91432pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{4r}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\sum_{v\in B}y_{v}\prod_{u\in S}(1-z_{u,v})\prod_{u\in R}(1-z_{u,v})\geqslant|y|-2\gamma r}}\right\}.

Multiplying by
x_{S\cup R}
yields the desired statement.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

5.12
.

The proof is analogous to the proof of
Lemma

5.9
. We simply replace
u_{S,p}^{\prime}(v)
by
{u^{\prime}}_{S,p}^{(z)}(v)\coloneqq u_{S,p}^{(z)}(v)(1-y_{v})
(cf.
Lemma

5.13
for a definition of
u_{S,p}^{(z)}
). Furthermore, we replace
Lemma

5.11
by
Lemma

5.13
.
∎

6
Proof of identifiability and refutation: the single label clique theorem with and without SoS

The goal of this section is to prove
Theorem

1.7
.
See
1.7

Remark 6.1

(The parameter
\alpha
)
.

Above, and throughout the rest of this paper, the constant
\alpha
is to be understood as controlling our lower bound on
d
in the sense that we always assume
d\gg n^{\alpha}
(see also the discussion in
Section

3.1
). However, in this section, we shall also use
\alpha
to refer to the quantity
\log(d)/\log(n)
. Concretely, instead of writing
n^{\alpha}\ll d\ll n^{1-\alpha}
and
n^{\alpha}\ll d\ll n^{2-\alpha}
, we shall simply write
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
\alpha\in(0,2)
, respectively. While this is somewhat of an abuse of notation, we think that it greatly simplifies readability and further makes our work easier to be compared with previous results where this notaion is common as well.

The proof of
Theorem

1.7
is split into two parts. First, in
Section

6.1
, we adapt the proof of Spirakis et al.
[
63
]
which applies to the case of
\alpha\in(0,2)
but needs some more restrictive condition on
p,q
. To address this issue (at least in the case
\alpha\in(0,1)
), we present a novel proof in
Section

6.2
based on the notions of balancedness as developed in
Section

4.2
. This is one of our main contributions and constitutes precisely the “proof of identifiability” described in
Section

2
. This proof is phrased entirely in constant-degree SoS and the ideas introduced there further form the foundations of our algorithms.

To see that the cases considered in
Section

6.1
and
Section

6.2
indeed cover all cases needed for
Theorem

1.7
, we provide a short proof.

Proof of
Theorem

1.7
.

We let
\varepsilon>0
be a sufficiently small constant and assume that the parameters are such that
k\geqslant n^{\varepsilon}
. If
\alpha\in(0,1)
,
p\geqslant\varepsilon
, and
p-q\geqslant\varepsilon
, we apply
Theorem

6.8
. If
\alpha\in(0,1)
and one of these conditions on
p,q
is not met, it follows that
p-q\leqslant\varepsilon
since
p-q\leqslant p
. Then,
Theorem

6.2
is applicable provided
\varepsilon>0
is small enough. This finishes case (i). To handle the assumptions in (ii), we can directly apply
Theorem

6.2
.
∎

6.1
Identifiability for
\alpha\in(0,2)

Throughout this entire section, we asssume that either
\max\big\{\log\big(\frac{1-q}{1-p}\big),q\big\}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon
or
p-q=o(1)
.
The goal of this section is to prove the following.

Theorem 6.2
.

Let
G\sim{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
and assume that
n^{\varepsilon}\ll d\ll n^{2-\varepsilon}
such that either
p-q=o(1)
or
\max\big\{\log\big(\frac{1-q}{1-p}\big),q\big\}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon
. Recall that
k\coloneqq\delta n
. Then with high probability, the following holds. For any sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
, every clique
K\subseteq G
of size
\geqslant(1-\varepsilon)k
is spanned by a single label. That is, for every such
K
, there is some
\ell\in[d]
such that
K\subseteq S_{\ell}
.

For the proof, we recall from
Section

4.4
that the event
\mathcal{E}_{a,b}
occurs if and only if there is some
\ell\in[d]
and sets
S\subseteq S_{\ell},T\in[n]\setminus S_{\ell}
with
|S|=a
and
|T|=b
such that
G[S\uplus T]
is a biclique (
Definition

4.12
). Due to
Lemma

4.13
,
\mathcal{E}_{a,b}
is unlikely for suitable
a\geqslant\frac{n^{\varepsilon}k}{\sqrt{d}}
and
b=\lfloor\log(n)^{2}\rfloor
. A simple corollary of this fact is that every clique
K
that is not spanned by a single label can contain at most
k^{\gamma}
vertices of the same label for some constant
\gamma<1
.

Corollary 6.3
.

With high probability over the draw of
G\sim{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
, the following holds.
Every clique
K
of size
(1-\varepsilon)k
in
G
is either formed by a single label, or for every label
\ell\in[d]
, there are at most
k^{\gamma}
vertices in
K
that have chosen
\ell
, where
0<\gamma<1
is a constant.

Proof.

Consider a clique
K
where
|K\cap S_{\ell}|=|K|-t
for
1\leqslant t\leqslant\lfloor\log(n)^{2}\rfloor
. Then any
v\in K\setminus S_{\ell}
has degree at least
|K|-t
into
S_{\ell}
, which can be ruled out using
Lemma

4.15
stating that this degree is at most
(1+o(1))pk
. Given sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
, this is a contradiction.

Next, consider the case that
t>\lfloor\log(n)^{2}\rfloor
. Then,
Lemma

4.13
immediately implies that for every
\ell
, we have
|K\cap S_{\ell}|\leqslant a
as otherwise, this contradicts the occurence of
\mathcal{E}_{a,b}
. Since by
Lemma

4.13
, we can choose
a=n^{\varepsilon}k/\sqrt{d}\leqslant k^{\gamma}
for some
\gamma<1
, the statement follows. ∎

We proceed by ruling out the case that
K
is not spanned by a single label using the conclusion of the above lemma by showing that it implies the existence of the following structure in
K
.

Definition 6.4

(Duplicate-free sets)
.

Given a
G\sim{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
, we call a set of verices
M\subseteq[n]

duplicate-free
if there is an assignment
g:\binom{M}{2}\rightarrow[d]\cup\{\emptyset\}
with the following properties.

1.

For every
\ell\in[d]
, there is at most one
\{u,v\}\in\binom{M}{2}
such that
g(\{u,v\})=\ell
.

2.

For every
\{u,v\}\in\binom{M}{2}
such that
g(\{u,v\})\in[d]
, we have that
g(\{u,v\})\in M_{u}\cap M_{v}
.

Intuitively, the function
g
maps every
\{u,v\}\in\binom{M}{2}
to either
\emptyset
or a label
\ell
that both
u
and
v
have in common, while assigning each label
\ell\in[d]
at most once. We show that every clique not formed by a single label contains a decently large duplicate-free set.

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
be a sufficiently small constant. Then, with high probability over the draw of
G\sim{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
, it holds for every clique
K
of size
\geqslant(1-\varepsilon)k
not formed by a single label, that
K
contains a duplicate-free set of size
k^{c}
where
c>0
is a constant.

Proof.

We make use of the following:

Claim 6.6
.

The following event occurs with high probability over the draw of
G\sim{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
.
For every pair
\{u,v\}\in\binom{[n]}{2}
, there are at most
C\log(n)
labels in
M_{u}\cap M_{v}
.

Before we prove our claim, we show how this implies the existence of a
n^{\Omega(1)}
-sized duplicate-free set in
K
. To this end, consider the following simple algorithm for constructing such a set
Q\subseteq K
.

1.

Initialize
Q_{0}=\emptyset
and set
i=0
.

2.

While
K
is not empty, choose an arbitrary vertex
v\in K
, and remove from
K
all vertices that have chosen a label in
\bigcup_{u\in Q_{i}}(M_{u}\cap M_{v})
. For each
u\in Q_{i}
, set
g(\{u,v\})
to an arbitrary label in
M_{u}\cap M_{v}
if nonempty, and
g(\{u,v\})=\emptyset
otherwise. Finally, set
Q_{i+1}=Q_{i}\cup\{v\}
and update
i\leftarrow i+1
.

3.

Output
Q_{i}
.

We point out, that the above algorithm maintains the following invariant: at the beginning of each iteration described in step 2, it holds that for all
u,u^{\prime}\in Q
and all
v\in K
, we have
M_{u}\cap M_{u^{\prime}}\cap M_{v}=\emptyset
. This follows since all vertices that have chosen a label in
M_{u}\cap M_{u^{\prime}}
were removed from
K
in a previous iteration, and it implies that the set
Q
together with the assignment
g
is duplicate-free.

Moreover, using that with high probability
|M_{u}\cap M_{v}|\leqslant C\log(n)
for all
u,v
(
6.6
) in conjunction that for every label
\ell
, the number of vertices in
K
that have chosen
\ell
is at most
k^{\gamma}
(
Corollary

6.3
), the number of vertices removed in iteration
i
is at most
C|Q_{i}|k^{\gamma}\log(n)
with high probability. Since
|Q_{i}|=i
, after
j
iterations, we have removed at most

Ck^{\gamma}\log(n)\sum_{i=1}^{j}|Q_{i}|=Ck^{\gamma}\log(n)\frac{j(j+1)}{2}

vertices from the initial set
K
. While
j\leqslant k^{\frac{1-\gamma}{2}-\varepsilon}
, this is smaller than
(1-\varepsilon)k
, so we can run the above algorithm for at least
k^{c}
iterations, where
c>0
is a constant. All that remains for proving the statement is the following.

Proof of
6.6
.

Fix a pair of vertices
u,v
and fix a single label
\ell
. Then reveal the label
\ell
for the two vertices. The probability that both vertices share the fixed label is
\delta^{2}=O(1/d)
by
Lemma

4.1
. Hence, the expected number of shared labels among the fixed pair is
\mathbb{E}[|M_{u}\cap M_{v}|]=O(1)
and a Chernoff bound yields that there exists a constant
C
, such that the probability that
|M_{u}\cap M_{v}|
exceeds
C\log(n)
is at most
n^{-3}
. Taking a union bound over all
O(n^{2})
pairs of vertices the desired claim follows.
∎

Finally, we prove that duplicate-free sets of size
k^{c}
do not exist with high probability.

Lemma 6.7
.

For every constant
c>0
, the following holds with high probability over the draw of
G\sim{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
. No set
K\subseteq[n]
of size at least
k^{c}
is both duplicate-free and a clique.

Proof.

The lemma follows because it is very unlikely that a duplicate-free set contains many edges. To show this, let
M
be a subset of
[n]
such that
|M|\geqslant k^{c}
. We bound the probability that
M
is duplicate-free. To this end, we consider all possible mappings
g:\binom{M}{2}\rightarrow[d]\cup\{\emptyset\}
consistent with the constraints in
Definition

6.4
. To enumerate them, we denote by
h=h(g)
the number of pairs in
\binom{M}{2}
that are mapped to
\emptyset
by a given
h(g)
. For a fixed
h
and
M\subseteq[n]
, the number of mappings
g
conformal with
Definition

6.4
and
h(g)=h
are at most

\displaystyle\binom{\binom{|M|}{2}}{h}\ d^{\binom{|M|}{2}-h}.

(6.1)

The probability that there is a duplicate free set of size
|M|
is thus (by the union bound) at most

S\coloneqq n^{|M|}\sum_{h=0}^{\binom{|M|}{2}}\binom{\binom{|M|}{2}}{h}d^{\binom{|M|}{2}-h}\delta^{2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\binom{|M|}{2}-h}}\right)}q^{h}=n^{|M|}\sum_{h=0}^{\binom{|M|}{2}}\binom{\binom{|M|}{2}}{h}(d\delta^{2})^{\binom{|M|}{2}-h}q^{h},

where we used that
q^{h}
is the probability that there are
h
edges due to "noise". To show that
S
is small, we now distinguish the two parameter regimes we assumed in the beginning.

Case 1:

p-q\leqslant\varepsilon
. Using symmetry of the binomial coefficient,

S=n^{|M|}\sum_{h=0}^{\binom{|M|}{2}}\binom{\binom{|M|}{2}}{h}(d\delta^{2})^{\binom{|M|}{2}-h}q^{h}=n^{|M|}\sum_{h=0}^{\binom{|M|}{2}}\binom{\binom{|M|}{2}}{h}(d\delta^{2})^{h}q^{\binom{|M|}{2}-h}.

We now split the sum into two parts, based on
h
. To this end, we let
\mu
be a constant in
(0,1/2)
to be fixed later, we define
m\coloneqq\binom{|M|}{2}
, and we split

S=\underbrace{n^{|M|}\sum_{h=0}^{\mu m}\binom{m}{h}(d\delta^{2})^{h}q^{m-h}}_{\eqqcolon S\downarrow}+\underbrace{n^{|M|}\sum_{h=\mu m}^{m}\binom{m}{h}(d\delta^{2})^{h}q^{m-h}}_{\eqqcolon S\uparrow}.

Now, the crucial observation is that by
Lemma

4.1
,

d\delta^{2}\leqslant(1+o(1))\log\big(\frac{1-q}{1-p}\big)
and
\log\big(\frac{1-q}{1-p}\big)
tends to zero as
p-q\rightarrow 0
. Hence, we can choose
\varepsilon
small enough to ensure
\delta^{2}d\leqslant\tilde{q}\coloneqq\max\{q,1/3\}
. Then, the first sum can be bounded as

\displaystyle S\!\!\downarrow\ \ \leqslant n^{|M|}\tilde{q}^{m}\sum_{h=0}^{\mu m}\binom{m}{h}\leqslant n^{|M|}\tilde{q}^{m}\mu m\binom{m}{\mu m}

\displaystyle\leqslant n^{|M|}\tilde{q}^{m}m\frac{1}{(\mu m)!}m^{\mu m}

\displaystyle\leqslant n^{|M|}\tilde{q}^{m}m\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2em}{\mu m}}}\right)^{\mu m}=n^{|M|}m\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tilde{q}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2e}{\mu}}}\right)^{\mu}}}\right)^{m}.

Since
q
is bounded away from
1
, we can choose
\mu
small enough to make the base
\tilde{q}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2e}{\mu}}}\right)^{\mu}
strictly smaller than one such that

S\!\!\downarrow\ \ \leqslant n^{|M|}\binom{|M|}{2}^{2}e^{-\Omega\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\binom{|M|}{2}}}\right)}\leqslant n^{-\omega(1)}.

\displaystyle S\!\!\uparrow\ \ \leqslant n^{|M|}(d\delta^{2})^{\mu m}\sum_{h=0}^{m}\binom{m}{h}\leqslant n^{|M|}2^{m}(d\delta^{2})^{\mu m}.

Choosing
\varepsilon
small enough such that
(d\delta^{2})^{\mu m}\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{3}}}\right)^{m}
then also yields that
S\!\!\uparrow\ \ \leqslant n^{-\omega(1)}
, as desired.

Case 2:

\max\big\{\log\big(\frac{1-q}{1-p}\big),q\big\}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon
.
In this case, it follows that

n^{|M|}\sum_{h=0}^{\binom{|M|}{2}}\binom{\binom{|M|}{2}}{h}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((1+o(1))\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1-q}{1-p}}}\right)}}\right)^{\binom{|M|}{2}-h}q^{h}\leqslant n^{|M|}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon}}\right)^{\binom{|M|}{2}}\sum_{h=0}^{\binom{|M|}{2}}\binom{\binom{|M|}{2}}{h}.

Finally, using that
\sum_{h=0}^{\binom{|M|}{2}}\binom{\binom{|M|}{2}}{h}=2^{\binom{|M|}{2}}
yields

\displaystyle n^{|M|}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon}}\right)^{\binom{|M|}{2}}\sum_{h=0}^{\binom{|M|}{2}}\binom{\binom{|M|}{2}}{h}=n^{|M|}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon}}\right)^{\binom{|M|}{2}}2^{\binom{|M|}{2}},

which is
n^{-\omega(1)}
since
|M|\geqslant k^{c}=n^{\Omega(1)}
.
∎

6.2
Identifiability via SoS for
\alpha\in(0,1)

While the previous subsection needs the assumption that either
p-q=o(1)
or
\max\big\{\log\big(\frac{1-q}{1-p}\big),q\big\}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon
, this leaves a gap in the dense case where
p
is a sufficiently large constant. In this section, we close this gap for the case
\alpha\in(0,1)
using an entirely different argument that relies on the notion of balancedness. Throughout, we therefore assume that
p
and
p-q
are both constant while
\alpha\in(0,1)
. Note that this implies in particular that
n^{\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon}\ll k\ll n^{1-\varepsilon}
.

With this, we show the following theorem, that together with the previous section covers all relevant cases assuming only
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
n^{\varepsilon}\ll k\ll n^{1-\varepsilon}
.

Theorem 6.8

(Strong Single Label Clique Theorem for
\alpha\in(0,1)
)
.

Let
G\sim{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
and assume that
d=\Theta(n^{\alpha})
for some fixed
\alpha\in(0,1)
while both
p
and
p-q
are constants. Then for any sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
, every clique
K\subseteq G
of size
\geqslant(1-\varepsilon)k
is spanned by a single label. That is, for every such
K
, there is some
\ell\in[d]
such that
K\subseteq S_{\ell}
.

The high-level approach is as follows. Assume that in a
G\sim{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
, there were a clique
K
of size
(1-\varepsilon)k
that is not spanned by a single label. Now, fix a constant
t
and consider an arbitrary
t
-tuple
T\in\binom{K}{t}
and note that in the neighbourhood
N_{G}(T)
of
T
in
G
, our clique
K
is preserved. Hence, it suffices to rule out that there is some
T\in\tbinom{[n]}{t}
such that a clique of size
(1-\varepsilon)k-t
exists in
N_{G}(T)
.

Given a fixed
T
, we can use a balancedness-based argument to show that there is only a small number of ground-truth cliques in
N_{G}({T})
that
K
can concentrate on. These cliques are precisely those that correspond to the labels in
\mathcal{B}_{T}
, the set of labels that appear in at least
3
of the vertices in
T
. We denote the set of vertices corresponding to the cliques in
\mathcal{B}_{T}
by
V(\mathcal{B}_{T})
and show that only
o(k)
vertices of
K
are outside of
\mathcal{B}_{T}
. Given this, we can derive the stronger statement that
K
can only intersect one of them by a significant amount using the balancedness
between
the cliques in
\mathcal{B}_{T}
. If we assume that at least one vertex of
K
is outside of every ground-truth clique
S_{\ell}
, this implies that the size of
k
must be
o(k)
which yields our desired conclusion

Remarkably, all of this can be done
within constant-degree SoS
, and the final proof of
Theorem

6.8
can be phrased as a
SoS-refutation
. This further yields an efficient refutation algorithm, which might be of independent interest.

We split the proof of
Theorem

6.8
into three main parts: first, we show that all but
o(k)
vertices of a clique containing
T
concentrate on the vertices in
V(\mathcal{B}_{T})
, afterwards, we show that among the cliques in
\mathcal{B}_{T}
, the clique
K
does not significantly intersect more than one of them. Finally, we finish the proof of
Theorem

6.8
by constructing a set of axioms that encodes a clique of size
(1-\varepsilon)k
that it is
not
contained in any ground-truth clique, and showing how it gives rise to a low-degree SoS-proof of the inequality “
-1>0
”: a contradiction.

6.2.1
Step 1: Few vertices outside
V(\mathcal{B}_{T})

We start by formalizing the idea that a clique containing
T
can only place
o(k)
vertices outside of
V(\mathcal{B}_{T})
.

Lemma 6.9
.

Given a
t
-tuple
T\in\binom{[n]}{t}
, denote by
\mathcal{B}_{T}\subseteq[d]
the set of labels that occurs in at least
3
vertices of
T
. Denote further by
V(\mathcal{B}_{T})=\bigcup_{\ell\in\mathcal{B}_{T}}S_{\ell}
, i.e., the set of vertices that have chosen a label in
\mathcal{B}_{T}
. Then there are
t,r_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
large enough such that w.h.p. over the draw of
G\sim{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
, the following holds for every
T\in\binom{[n]}{t}
and
r\geqslant r_{0}
such that
r\leqslant t
is a power of
2
.

\mathcal{A}(G,(1-\varepsilon)k)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{20.47598pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-20.47598pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[20.47598pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-20.47598pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[20.47598pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(r)}$}}}$}}}\Bigg\{x_{T}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{v\in[n]\setminus V(\mathcal{B}_{T})}x_{v}}}\right)\leqslant O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{n}{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{n^{2}}{k}}}\right)^{1/r}}}\right)x_{T}\Bigg\}.

Remark 6.10

(Intuition)
.

The above can be interpreted as stating that every clique
K
in
G
of size
(1-\varepsilon)k
such that
T\subseteq K
is such that there are at most
O(\frac{n}{k}(\frac{n^{2}}{k})^{1/r})
vertices of
K
that are in
[n]\setminus V(\mathcal{B}_{T})
. Further, note in particular that this bound can be made
o(k)
if choosing
r
large enough since by assumption on
\alpha,p,
and
q
we get that there is some
\varepsilon>0
such that
