k\geqslant n^{\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon}
. Therefore, the conclusion of the lemma can indeed be seen as doing what was promised in the previous paragraph.

We prove this lemma using a sequence of smaller statements. The general idea is to consider a multipartite sub-graph of
G
and to exploit its balancedness properties. Precisely, we consider a multi-partition
V_{1}\uplus V_{2}\uplus\cdots\uplus V_{m}
where the sets
V_{1},V_{2},\ldots,V_{m}
are a fixed partition of
[n]
such that each
V_{i}
has size at most
\lfloor\frac{k}{2}\rfloor
and
m\leqslant 3n/k
.

The first step towards establishing
Lemma

6.9
consists in establishing balancedness of the cut given by
G[A_{i}\uplus(B_{i}\cap N_{G}({T}))]
for
A_{i}=V_{i}\setminus V(\mathcal{B}_{T})\text{ and }B_{i}=[n]\setminus A_{i}
and all
i
, captured in the following claim.

Lemma 6.11
.

Fix any constant
t\in\mathbb{N}
.
With high probability over the choice of
G\sim{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
, the following holds. For any fixed
r\geqslant 2
, every
T\in\binom{[n]}{t}
and every
i\in[m]
, the bipartite graph
G[V_{i}\uplus([n]\setminus V_{i})]\cap N_{G}(T)
has
r
-fold balancedness

\frac{16tp^{-2}}{\alpha}\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{r}\cdot p^{t}k+o(k).

Proof.

We wish to apply
Lemma

4.10
with
L(T)=\mathcal{B}_{T}
,
S(T)=V(\mathcal{B}_{T})
and
\eta=2
. With this choice, we get by construction of
L(T)
that for every
\ell\in[d]\setminus L(T)
, only at most
2
vertices in
T
have chosen
\ell
. Moreover, we use the following collection
\{\textsc{Alg}_{i}\}_{i\in[m]}
of procedures for constructing a bipartition. Each
\textsc{Alg}_{i}(S(T),[n])
outputs (deterministically) the bipartition
A_{i}=V_{i}\setminus V(\mathcal{B}_{T})\text{ and }B_{i}=[n]\setminus A_{i}
, which is valid in the sense of
Definition

4.9
since
A_{i}\cap S(T)=\emptyset
. Hence,
Lemma

4.10
is applicable and yields the desired statement.
∎

With this, we wish to bound the number of vertices outside of
V(\mathcal{B}_{T})
given a fixed
T
. For this, we use the SoS-proofs established in
Section

5
.

Proof of
Lemma

6.9
.

The main part of our proof can be phrased as a degree-
O(r)
SoS proof based on the certificates in
Section

5
and the axioms
\mathcal{A}(G,(1-\varepsilon)k)
.

Consider the cut given by
G[A_{i}\uplus(B_{i}\cap N_{G}({T}))]
and
A_{i}=V_{i}\setminus V(\mathcal{B}_{T})\text{ and }B_{i}=[n]\setminus A_{i}
for some fixed
T\in\binom{[n]}{t}
and
i\in[m]
.
To simplify notation, let for now
A\coloneqq A_{i}
and
B\coloneqq B_{i}
. Considering any clique
K
of size
(1-\varepsilon)k
, we denote by
x_{u}\in\{0,1\}
the indicator variables indicating whether or not
u\in[n]
is in
K
. We further define
|y|\coloneqq\sum_{v\in(B\cap N_{G}({T}))}x_{v}
. Applying the statement from
Lemma

5.9
to the bipartite graph
G[A\uplus(B\cap N_{G}({T}))]
, now yields that

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(G,(1-\varepsilon)k)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{S\subseteq A,|S|=r}x_{S}}}\right)^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1-p}{p}}}\right)^{r}|y|-\Delta}}\right)\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n+rn^{2}}}\right)\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{r}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{S\subseteq A,|S|=r}x_{S}}}\right)}}\right\}.

(6.2)

Now, recall that
|A_{i}|\leqslant|V_{i}(T)|\leqslant\lfloor\frac{k}{2}\rfloor
. Using this fact, and the axiom
\{\sum_{v\in[n]}x_{v}\geqslant(1-\varepsilon)k\}
we get that
\mathcal{A}(G,(1-\varepsilon)k)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.53467pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}$}}}\{\sum_{v\in B}x_{v}\geqslant(1-\varepsilon)k-\frac{k}{2}\geqslant\frac{1-2\varepsilon}{2}k\geqslant\frac{k}{3}\}
. Further, since
\mathcal{A}(G,(1-\varepsilon)k)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{21.13196pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-21.13196pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[21.13196pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-21.13196pt\raisebox{2.79446pt}{\makebox[21.13196pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t+2}$}}}$}}}\{x_{T}x_{v}=0\}
for
v\notin N_{G}({T})
, we get

\mathcal{A}(G,(1-\varepsilon)k)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{x_{T}|y|=x_{T}\sum_{v\in B\cap N_{G}({T})}x_{v}=x_{T}\sum_{v\in B}x_{v}\geqslant x_{T}\frac{k}{3}}}\right\}

We choose
t
as a sufficiently large constant such that
\Delta\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((1-p)/p}}\right)^{r}\frac{k}{4}
, which is possible by
Lemma

6.11
. Therefore, multiplying (
6.2.1
) by
x_{T}
, we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(G,(1-\varepsilon)k)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{x_{T}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{S\subseteq A,|S|=r}x_{S}}}\right)^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1-p}{p}}}\right)^{r}\frac{k}{12}\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n+rn^{2}}}\right)\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{r}x_{T}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{S\subseteq A,|S|=r}x_{S}}}\right)}}\right\}.

using both cancellations from
Lemma

5.5
, and recalling that
\mathcal{A}(G,(1-\varepsilon)k)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{21.13196pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-21.13196pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[21.13196pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-21.13196pt\raisebox{2.79446pt}{\makebox[21.13196pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t+2}$}}}$}}}\{x_{T}^{2}=x_{T}\}
, we get

\mathcal{A}(G,(1-\varepsilon)k)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{x_{T}\sum_{S\subseteq A,|S|=r}x_{S}\leqslant\frac{12rn^{2}}{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}/\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1-p}{p}}}\right)}}\right)^{r}}}\right\}.

Using further that
\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}/\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1-p}{p}}}\right)=\max\{1,(p/(1-p))^{2}\}
and applying
Lemma

5.7
, we get

\mathcal{A}(G,(1-\varepsilon)k)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{x_{T}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{v\in A_{i}}x_{v}}}\right)^{r}\leqslant(2r)^{r}x_{T}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{12rn^{2}}{k}\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{1,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right)^{2}}}\right\}^{r}+\frac{2r^{r}}{r!}+\varepsilon}}\right)}}\right\}.

Summing this statement over all
i\in[m]
and using the SoS almost triangle inequality for any even
r
, (
Lemma

5.6
),

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(G,(1-\varepsilon)k)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{20.47598pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-20.47598pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[20.47598pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-20.47598pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[20.47598pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(r)}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} &x_{T}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{v\in[n]\setminus V(\mathcal{B}_{T})}x_{v}}}\right)^{r}=x_{T}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i\in[m]}\sum_{v\in A_{i}}x_{v}}}\right)^{r}\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{3n}{k}}}\right)^{r-1}x_{T}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{v\in A_{i}}x_{v}}}\right)^{r}\\
&\hskip 56.9055pt\leqslant(2r)^{r}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{3n}{k}}}\right)^{r-1}x_{T}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{12rn^{2}}{k}\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{1,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right)^{2}}}\right\}^{r}+\frac{2r^{r}}{r!}+\varepsilon}}\right)\end{aligned}}}\right\},

where we used that the number of sets
V_{i}
is at most
m\leqslant 3n/k
. Now, we can choose am
r
to be a sufficiently large power of
2
and apply the cancellation
\{f^{2}\leqslant C\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{12.14894pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-12.14894pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[12.14894pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-12.14894pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[12.14894pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2d}$}}}$}}}\{f\leqslant\sqrt{C}\}
from
Lemma

5.5

\log_{2}(r)
times to obtain

\mathcal{A}(G,(1-\varepsilon)k)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{20.47598pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-20.47598pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[20.47598pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-20.47598pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[20.47598pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(r)}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{x_{T}\sum_{v\in[n]\setminus V(\mathcal{B}_{T})}x_{v}\leqslant\frac{6rn}{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{24n^{2}}{k}}}\right)^{1/r}\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{1,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right)^{2}}}\right\}\ x_{T}}}\right\},

as desired.
∎

This constitutes our first step towards the proof of
Theorem

6.8
since it allows us to restrict our attention to
V(\mathcal{B}_{T})
. We proceed by showing that no clique intersects more than one of the cliques in
\mathcal{B}_{T}
by a large amount.

6.2.2
Step 2: Only one clique in
\mathcal{B}_{T}
has significant overlap with
K

Our key lemma here is a certificate analogous to the certificate obtained in
[
21
, Theorem 4.11]
. Before introducing it, we need to establish some further notation. Given a
G\sim{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
any integer
t\in\mathbb{N}
and some
T\in\binom{[n]}{t}
, recall the definition of
\mathcal{B}_{T}
. Define
\mathcal{R}(T)
as the set of vertices in
G
that have chosen at least two of the labels in
\mathcal{B}_{T}
. For any
\ell\in\mathcal{B}_{T}
, define further
S^{\text{(in)}}_{\ell}(T)=S_{\ell}\setminus\mathcal{R}(T)
and
S^{\text{(out)}}_{\ell}(T)=\bigcup_{\ell^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{T}\setminus\{\ell\}}S_{\ell^{\prime}}\setminus\mathcal{R}(T)
. We will mainly be intereseted in the bipartite graphs
G[S^{\text{(in)}}_{\ell}(T)\uplus S^{\text{(out)}}_{\ell}(T)]
. The key lemma proved in this section is the following.

Lemma 6.12
.

The following holds with high probability over the draw of
G
and any
t\in\mathbb{N}
sufficiently large. For every
T\in\binom{[n]}{t}
and every
\ell\in\mathcal{B}_{T}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(G,(1-\varepsilon)k)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{x_{T}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{v\in[n]\setminus S_{\ell}}x_{v}}}\right)\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{S\subseteq S^{\text{(in)}}_{\ell}(T),|S|=t}x_{S}}}\right)^{2}\leqslant O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{n^{5}}{k^{2}}}}\right)\ x_{T}}}\right\}.

(6.3)

Remark 6.13

(Intuition)
.

Recall that
S^{\text{(in)}}_{\ell}(T)
covers most of
S_{\ell}
, while
S^{\text{(out)}}_{\ell}(T)
covers most of the rest of
V(\mathcal{B}_{T})
. Then, the above lemma states that no clique of size
(1-\varepsilon)k
can simultaneously have a large overlap with
S_{\ell}
and
[n]\setminus S_{\ell}
.

The proof does again rely on balancedness of
G[S^{\text{(in)}}_{\ell}(T)\uplus S^{\text{(out)}}_{\ell}(T)]
.
This is captured in the following.

Lemma 6.14

(The boundary of
S_{\ell}
is balanced in
V(\mathcal{B}_{T})
)
.

The following holds with high probability over the draw of
G
and any
t\in\mathbb{N}
. For every
T\in\binom{[n]}{t}
and every
\ell\in\mathcal{B}_{T}
, the bipartite graph

G[S^{\text{(in)}}_{\ell}(T)\uplus S^{\text{(out)}}_{\ell}(T)]
has
r
-fold balancedness
o(k)
for every
2\leqslant r\leqslant t
.

Proof.

Again, we sample
G\sim{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
in multiple phases, given a fixed
T\in\binom{[n]}{t}
.

1.

Reveal the labels in
T
, thus uncovering
\mathcal{B}_{T}
.

2.

For all vertices
[n]\setminus T
, reveal the labels in
\mathcal{B}_{T}
.

3.

Reveal all remaining labels.

Thus, after phase 2, we have uncovered which vertices are on the left and right of
G[S^{\text{(in)}}_{\ell}(T)\uplus S^{\text{(out)}}_{\ell}(T)]
. Note that at this point, there are no edges between
S^{\text{(in)}}_{\ell}(T)
and
S^{\text{(out)}}_{\ell}(T)
yet. These only appear after phase 3 where our balancedness properties arise.

To show how, we first note that by
Lemma

4.7
, the labels in
S^{\text{(in)}}_{\ell}(T)
are such that
\mathcal{E}(S^{\text{(in)}}_{\ell}(T),t,\frac{8t}{\alpha})
is met, with probability
1-O(n^{-2t})
. Then, fixing any pair
S,R\subseteq S^{\text{(in)}}_{\ell}(T)
with
|S|=|R|=t
such that
|S\triangle R|\geqslant 3
, and any
v\in S^{\text{(out)}}_{\ell}(T)
, we get that

|\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[u_{S,p}(v)u_{R,p}(v)}}\right]|\leqslant O(\log(n)\delta)\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{r}+O(\log(n)\delta\sqrt{d\delta})^{3}\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{r}

where the first term accounts for the possibility of choosing a duplicate label in
S\cup R
, and the second term for the remaing case assuming that no duplicate was chosen (cf. the proof of
Lemma

4.8
). Summing over all
v\in S^{\text{(out)}}_{\ell}(T)
and defining
U_{T,R,S,\ell}\coloneqq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\sum_{v\in S^{\text{(out)}}_{\ell}(T)}u_{S,p}(v)u_{R,p}(v)}}\right|
, then yields

\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[U_{T,R,S,\ell}}}\right]\leqslant|V(\mathcal{B}_{T})|\ O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\log(n)}{\sqrt{d}}}}\right)\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{r}\leqslant O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\log(n)k}{\sqrt{d}}}}\right)\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{r}=o(k),

where we used
Lemma

4.1
to bound
\delta
by
O(\sqrt{d})
and
|V(\mathcal{B}_{T})|\leqslant\frac{8t}{\alpha}k=O(k)
w.h.p. by
Lemma

4.6
and a Chernoff bound on the size of all
S_{\ell}
with subsequent union bound over
\ell\in[d]
.

Now, applying
Lemma

3.1
, we get that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|U_{T,R,S,\ell}-\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[U_{T,R,S,\ell}}}\right]|\geqslant z}}\right)\leqslant

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt2\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-z^{2}\ /\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(8\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{2r}n+\frac{4}{3}\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{r}z}}\right)}}\right)\leqslant n^{-2t-2r}

for some
z=\Omega(\sqrt{n\log(n)})
. If all of this occurs, then
U_{T,R,S,\ell}=o(k)
as desired. Union bounding over all
R,S,T
and all labels in
\mathcal{B}_{T}
, yields the desired statement.
∎

Moreover, we need to establish that the overlap
\mathcal{R}(T)
is small.

Lemma 6.15

(Small overlap)
.

The following holds with high probability over the draw of
G
, for any integer
t\in\mathbb{N}
. For every
T\in\binom{[n]}{t}
, we have
\mathcal{R}(T)\leqslant O(k\delta+\sqrt{n\log(n)})=o(k)
.

Proof.

First fix a pair of labels
\ell,\ell^{\prime}
. Then the expected number of vertices that have chosen
\ell,\ell^{\prime}
is
n\delta^{2}=k\delta
. Further, by
Lemma

3.1

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\big||S_{\ell}\cap S_{\ell^{\prime}}|-k\delta\big|>\sqrt{n\log(n)}}}\right)\leqslant 2\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\frac{n\log(n)}{2n+\frac{2}{3}\sqrt{n\log(n)}}}}\right)\leqslant n^{-\omega(1)}.

Hence, by a union bound over all
\ell,\ell^{\prime}
we have
|S_{\ell}\cap S_{\ell^{\prime}}|=k\delta\pm\sqrt{n\log(n)}
for all
\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}
.

Fix a tuple
T\in\binom{[n]}{t}
and note that by
Lemma

4.6
, we have
|\mathcal{B}_{T}|\leqslant\frac{4t}{\alpha}
w.h.p. for all
T
. Combined with the previous observation on
|S_{\ell}\cap S_{\ell^{\prime}}|
this yields
|\mathcal{R}(T)|\leqslant|\mathcal{B}_{T}|^{2}(k\delta+\sqrt{n\log(n)})=O(k\delta+\sqrt{n\log(n)})
, as desired.
∎

Now, we can use the established balancedness properties to obtain the following certificate asserting limited overlap between cliques in
\mathcal{B}_{T}
. This statement will also serve an important purpose for our refutation algorithm in the next subsection.

Proof of
Lemma

6.12
.

To simplify notation in some places, we let
|y|\coloneqq\sum_{v\in S^{\text{(out)}}_{\ell}(T)}x_{v}
and
|x|\coloneqq\sum_{v\in[n]}x_{v}
.
Our starting point for the proof is the certificate from
Lemma

6.9
stating that

\mathcal{A}(G,(1-\varepsilon)k)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\Bigg\{x_{T}\ \bigg(\sum_{v\in[n]\setminus V(\mathcal{B}_{T})}x_{v}\bigg)\leqslant O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{n}{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{n^{2}}{k}}}\right)^{1/t}x_{T}}}\right)\Bigg\}.

Choosing
t
sufficiently large such that
O(\frac{n}{k}(\frac{n^{2}}{k})^{1/t})=o(k)
and noting that
\mathcal{A}(G,(1-\varepsilon)k)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\{x_{T}|x|\geqslant x_{T}(1-\varepsilon)k\}
, this implies

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(G,(1-\varepsilon)k)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\Bigg\{x_{T}\sum_{v\in V(\mathcal{B}_{T})}x_{v}=x_{T}\bigg(|x|-\sum_{v\in[n]\setminus V(\mathcal{B}_{T})}x_{v}\bigg)\geqslant(1-2\varepsilon)kx_{T}\Bigg\}.

(6.4)

In other words, if
T
is in the clique we are looking for, then almost all of its mass is in
V(\mathcal{B}_{T})
.

On the other hand, applying the statement from
Lemma

5.9
to the graph
G[S^{\text{(in)}}_{\ell}(T)\uplus S^{\text{(out)}}_{\ell}(T)]
which by
Lemma

6.14
has
t
-fold balancedness
\Delta=o(k)
, we get that

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(G,(1-\varepsilon)k)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{S\subseteq S^{\text{(in)}}_{\ell}(T),|S|=t}x_{S}}}\right)^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1-p}{p}}}\right)^{t}|y|-\Delta}}\right)\\
&\hskip 85.35826pt\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n+tn^{2}}}\right)\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{t}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{S\subseteq S^{\text{(in)}}_{\ell}(T),|S|=t}x_{S}}}\right)\end{aligned}}}\right\},

or alternatively—to make the above easier to work with—we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(G,(1-\varepsilon)k)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{S\subseteq S^{\text{(in)}}_{\ell}(T),|S|=t}x_{S}}}\right)^{2}\big(|y|-\Delta_{p}\big)\leqslant\beta(n,t,p)\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{S\subseteq S^{\text{(in)}}_{\ell}(T),|S|=t}x_{S}}}\right)}}\right\},

(6.5)

where
\beta(n,t,p)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n+tn^{2}}}\right)\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{t}/\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1-p}{p}}}\right)^{t}
and
\Delta_{p}=\Delta/\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1-p}{p}}}\right)^{t}
.

Intuitively, this tells us that our clique cannot simultaneously intersect both
S^{\text{(in)}}_{\ell}(T)
and
S^{\text{(out)}}_{\ell}(T)
, provided that
|y|\gg\Delta_{p}
. While this is a-priori not given, we can make use of the same little trick as used in
[
21
]
to enforce a statement that is useful for us. Precisely, we multiply both sides by
x_{i}
for
i\in[n]\setminus S_{\ell}
and afterwards sum over
i
. The fact that the maximum degree of vertex
i
into
S_{\ell}
is bounded then leads to an “all or nothing” phenomenon: if only a single
x_{i}=1
for
i\in[n]\setminus S_{\ell}
, then this implies that in fact
\Omega(k)
vertices in
[n]\setminus S_{\ell}
are in our clique. Together with the certificate established in step 1, this also gives
|y|=\Omega(k)
. We capture all this in the following claim.

Claim 6.16
.

For all
i\in[n]\setminus S_{\ell}
,

\mathcal{A}(G,(1-\varepsilon)k)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{x_{T}x_{i}\big(|y|-\Delta_{p}\big)\geqslant x_{T}x_{i}(1-p-5\varepsilon)k}}\right\}

Proof.

We use
Lemma

4.15
to note that w.h.p., all
i\in[n]\setminus S_{\ell}
have degree at most
(1+\varepsilon)pk
into
S_{\ell}
. Therefore

\mathcal{A}(G,(1-\varepsilon)k)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\big\{x_{i}\sum_{j\in S_{\ell}}x_{j}\leqslant(1+\varepsilon)pkx_{i}\big\}.
On the other hand, by (
6.4
),

\mathcal{A}(G,(1-\varepsilon)k)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\big\{x_{T}\sum_{j\in V(\mathcal{B}_{T})}x_{j}\geqslant x_{T}(1-2\varepsilon)k\big\}.
Moreover, since
|\mathcal{R}(T)|=o(k)
by
Lemma

6.15
, we get

\mathcal{A}(G,(1-\varepsilon)k)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{x_{T}x_{i}|y|\geqslant x_{T}x_{i}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{v\in V(\mathcal{B}_{T})}x_{v}-\sum_{v\in S_{\ell}}x_{v}-\sum_{v\in\mathcal{R}(T)}x_{v}}}\right)\geqslant x_{T}x_{i}(1-p-4\varepsilon)k}}\right\}.

Subtracting
x_{i}x_{T}\Delta_{p}
and noting
\Delta_{p}=o(k)
yields the statement.
∎

Using the above together with
Equation

6.5
, we get that

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(G,(1-\varepsilon)k)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{x_{T}x_{i}(1-p-5\varepsilon)k\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{S\subseteq S^{\text{(in)}}_{\ell}(T),|S|=t}x_{S}}}\right)^{2}\leqslant x_{T}x_{i}\ \beta(n,t,p)\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{S\subseteq S^{\text{(in)}}_{\ell}(T),|S|=t}x_{S}}}\right)}}\right\},

which is already a lot more useful. Now, using the cancellation
\{f^{2}\leqslant Cf\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{12.14894pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-12.14894pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[12.14894pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-12.14894pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[12.14894pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2d}$}}}$}}}\{f^{2}\leqslant C^{2}\}
, we get (after multiplying both sides by
x_{T}x_{i}
) that

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(G,(1-\varepsilon)k)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{x_{T}x_{i}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{S\subseteq S^{\text{(in)}}_{\ell}(T),|S|=t}x_{S}}}\right)^{2}\leqslant x_{T}x_{i}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\beta(n,t,p)}{(1-p-5\varepsilon)k}}}\right)^{2}}}\right\}.

Summing over
i\in[n]\setminus S_{\ell}
and noting that
\mathcal{A}(G,(1-\varepsilon)k)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\{\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus S_{\ell}}x_{i}\leqslant n\}
finally yields that

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(G,(1-\varepsilon)k)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{x_{T}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{v\in[n]\setminus S_{\ell}}x_{v}}}\right)\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{S\subseteq S^{\text{(in)}}_{\ell}(T),|S|=t}x_{S}}}\right)^{2}\leqslant O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{n^{5}}{k^{2}}}}\right)x_{T}}}\right\},

as desired.
∎

6.2.3
Step 3: Refutation

With
Lemmas

6.9
and
6.12
, we show
Theorem

6.8
. We formulate it as a SoS-proof that ends in the inequality
-1\geqslant 0
yielding a “proof by contradiction” that the premise (that there exists
(1-\varepsilon)k
sized clique that is not ground-truth) is wrong. To this end, we first refomulate a set of axioms that encodes this premise and afterwards use the certificats from
Section

6.2.1
and
Section

6.2.2
.

Axioms

Our axioms depend on the graph
G
, the desired clique size
\widetilde{k}
to be ruled out, and the ground truth cliques
S_{\ell}
,
\ell\in[d]
. We simply add an axiom telling us that the sum over all variables outside of each
S_{\ell}
is larger than 0.

\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\big(G,\widetilde{k},\{S_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in[d]}\big)=\mathcal{A}(G,\widetilde{k})\cup\bigg\{\sum_{v\in[n]\setminus S_{\ell}}x_{v}\geqslant 1\bigg\}_{\ell\in[d]}

Refutation

Our goal is to prove the following.

Lemma 6.17
.

Given a
G\sim{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
such that the assumption of
Theorem

6.8
are met. Then there is a sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
such that the following holds with high probability over the draw of
G

\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}(G,(1-\varepsilon)k,\{S_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in[d]})\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\{-1\geqslant 0\}

and the bit complexity of the proof is
n^{O(1)}
.

The main work towards establishing the above lies in the following lemma.

Lemma 6.18
.

Given
G\sim{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
such that the assumption of
Theorem

6.8
are met and a sufficiently large integer
t
, the following holds with high probability over the draw of
G
. For all
T\in\binom{[n]}{t}
,

\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\big(G,(1-\varepsilon)k,\{S_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in[d]}\big)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\{x_{T}\leqslant-x_{T}\}.

Proof.

We start by using the conclusion of
Lemma

6.12
which states that for every
\ell\in\mathcal{B}_{T}
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\big(G,(1-\varepsilon)k,\{S_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in[d]}\big)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{x_{T}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{v\in[n]\setminus S_{\ell}}x_{v}}}\right)\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{S\subseteq S^{\text{(in)}}_{\ell}(T),|S|=t}x_{S}}}\right)^{2}\leqslant O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{n^{5}}{k^{2}}}}\right)\ x_{T}}}\right\}.

We proceed by finding a suitable lower bound on the left hand side. To this end, we can first observe that the axiom

\{\sum_{v\in[n]\setminus S_{\ell}}x_{v}\geqslant 1\}
allows us to drop the sum
\sum_{v\in[n]\setminus S_{\ell}}x_{v}
entirely. Then, the cancellation
\{f^{2}\leqslant C\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{12.14894pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-12.14894pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[12.14894pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-12.14894pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[12.14894pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2d}$}}}$}}}\{f\leqslant\sqrt{C}\}
from
Lemma

5.5
yields

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\big(G,(1-\varepsilon)k,\{S_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in[d]}\big)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{x_{T}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{S\subseteq S^{\text{(in)}}_{\ell}(T),|S|=t}x_{S}}}\right)\leqslant O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{n^{5/2}}{k}}}\right)\ x_{T}}}\right\}.

To deal with the remaining sum, we apply
Lemma

5.7
to get

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\big(G,(1-\varepsilon)k,\{S_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in[d]}\big)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{x_{T}}{2^{t}t!}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{v\in S^{\text{(in)}}_{\ell}(T)}x_{v}}}\right)^{t}\leqslant x_{T}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2t^{t}}{t!}+\varepsilon+O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{n^{5/2}}{k}}}\right)}}\right)\leqslant O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{n^{5/2}}{k}}}\right)\ x_{T}}}\right\}.

Now, using the cancellation
\{f^{2}\leqslant C\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{12.14894pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-12.14894pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[12.14894pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-12.14894pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[12.14894pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2d}$}}}$}}}\{f\leqslant\sqrt{C}\}
from
Lemma

5.5

\log_{2}(t)
times and choosing
t
as power of
2
, we get that

\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\big(G,(1-\varepsilon)k,\{S_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in[d]}\big)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{x_{T}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{v\in S^{\text{(in)}}_{\ell}(T)}x_{v}}}\right)\leqslant O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{n^{5/2}}{k}}}\right)^{1/t}\ x_{T}}}\right\}.

Now, we can sum over
\ell\in\mathcal{B}_{T}
to obtain

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\big(G,(1-\varepsilon)k,\{S_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in[d]}\big)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{x_{T}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{v\in V(\mathcal{B}_{T})\setminus\mathcal{R}(T)}x_{v}}}\right)\leqslant|\mathcal{B}_{T}|O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{n^{5/2}}{k}}}\right)^{1/t}\ x_{T}}}\right\},

since
\bigcup_{\ell\in\mathcal{B}_{T}}S^{\text{(in)}}_{\ell}(T)=V(\mathcal{B}_{T})\setminus\mathcal{R}(T)
and the
S^{\text{(in)}}_{\ell}(T)
are disjoint by definition.
Using the fact that
|\mathcal{B}_{T}|\leqslant\frac{4t}{\alpha}
by
Lemma

4.6
for all
T
with high probability, we obtain

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\big(G,(1-\varepsilon)k,\{S_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in[d]}\big)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{x_{T}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{v\in V(\mathcal{B}_{T})}x_{v}}}\right)\leqslant O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{n^{5/2}}{k}}}\right)^{1/t}\ x_{T}+|\mathcal{R}(T)|\ x_{T}}}\right\}.

(6.6)

Finally, it only remains to apply
Lemma

6.9
stating that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\big(G,(1-\varepsilon)k,\{S_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in[d]}\big)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\Bigg\{x_{T}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{v\in[n]\setminus V(\mathcal{B}_{T})}x_{v}}}\right)\leqslant O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{n}{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{n^{2}}{k}}}\right)^{1/t}}}\right)\ x_{T}\Bigg\}.

Choosing
t
sufficiently large, we can now achieve that
\max\big\{\frac{n}{k}(n^{2}/k)^{1/t},(n^{5/2}/k)^{1/t}\big\}=o(k)
. Moreover, we get
|\mathcal{R}(T)|=o(k)
by
Lemma

6.15
with high probability. In total, this gives

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\big(G,(1-\varepsilon)k,\{S_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in[d]}\big)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\Bigg\{x_{T}\ \Bigg(\sum_{v\in[n]}x_{v}\Bigg)\leqslant\frac{1}{2}k\ x_{T}\Bigg\}.

On the other hand, due to the constraint
\{\sum_{v}x_{v}\geqslant(1-\varepsilon)k\}
, we get

\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\big(G,(1-\varepsilon)k,\{S_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in[d]}\big)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\Bigg\{(1-\varepsilon)k\ x_{T}\leqslant x_{T}\Bigg(\sum_{v\in[n]}x_{v}\Bigg)\leqslant\frac{1}{2}k\ x_{T}\Bigg\}.

and the lemma follows after re-arranging if
\varepsilon<\frac{1}{2}
. ∎

Lemma

6.17
then essentially follows by summing over
T
and the fact that SoS knows that at least one
t
-tuple
T\subseteq[n]
must be in our clique.

Proof of
Lemma

6.17
.

We sum the conclusion of
Lemma

6.18
over all
T
to obtain (after re-arranging and dividing by
2
) that
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\big(G,(1-\varepsilon)k,\{S_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in[d]}\big)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\sum_{T\subseteq[n],|T|=t}x_{T}\leqslant 0}}\right\}.
The fact that we have the axiom
\{\sum_{v}x_{v}\geqslant(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})k\}
implies by
Lemma

5.8
that

\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\big(G,(1-\varepsilon)k,\{S_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in[d]}\big)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\sum_{T\subseteq[n],|T|=t}x_{T}\geqslant\binom{n}{\lfloor(1-\varepsilon)k\rfloor}\geqslant 1}}\right\}.

Therefore,
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\big(G,(1-\varepsilon)k,\{S_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in[d]}\big)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{-1\geqslant 0}}\right\}
as desired.
∎

Now
Theorem

6.8
is essentially a corollary of the above.

Proof of
Theorem

6.8
.

By
Lemma

6.17
we know that the axioms
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\big(G,(1-\varepsilon)k,\{S_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in[d]}\big)
result in a contradiction. This is equivalent to
Theorem

6.8
.
∎

6.3
Algorithmic Refutation

In addition to the fact that the ideas from
Section

6.2
yield a better single label clique theorem, they have algorithmic consequences. Since everything can be stated in terms of low-degree SoS, a certificate that no cliques outside of the ground-trugh exist
can actually be computed in polynomial time
.

This has a curious consequence in light of our algorithm for exact recovery from
Theorem

1.3
: after termination, the algorithm can actually
certify that it is correct
in the sense that it outputs a list
\mathcal{L}
of cliques and a certificate proving that
no other clique
of size
(1-\varepsilon)k
exists that is not contained in one of the cliques in
\mathcal{L}
. Moreover, if we simply want a certificate that no clique of a certain size
(1+\varepsilon)k
exists
without
prior knowledge of the ground truth cliques, this can also be computed in polynomial time. We capture these two facts in the following theorem.

Theorem 6.19

(Algorithmic refutation)
.

There are polynomial time algorithms with the following guarantees on input
G\sim{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
such that the assumptions of
Theorem

6.8
are met.

1.

Output a list
\mathcal{L}
of cliques in
G
together with a polynomial time verifiable proof that every clique of size
(1-\varepsilon)k
in
G
is contained in one of the cliques in
\mathcal{L}
. This holds for every sufficiently small
\varepsilon
.

2.

Output a polynomial time verifiable proof that no clique of size
(1+\varepsilon)k
exists in
G
. This holds whenever
\varepsilon\gg\sqrt{\log(n)/k}
.

Both algorithms succeed with high probability over the choice of
G
.

Proof.

It follows from standard results in the literature on convex optimization
[
61
,
64
,
49
]
that whenever there exists a degree-
d
SoS-proof of some inequality
\{g\geqslant 0\}
in axioms
\mathcal{A}
of bit complexity
n^{O(1)}
, then we can approximately compute a such proof (in the sense that we prove
\{g\geqslant-\eta\}
for some small
\eta
) in time
(m+n)^{O(d)}\text{polylog}(1/\eta)
where
m
is the number of constraints in
\mathcal{A}
and
n
is the number of variables. This implies statement 1 of our theorem by the SoS proof from
Lemma

6.17
.

For statement 2, we note that for the proof of
Lemma

6.17
, we used the axiom
\sum_{v\in[n]\setminus S_{\ell}}x_{v}\geqslant 1
for all
\ell
. While we cannot hope to use these axioms without knowing the ground-truth
S_{\ell}
, we can simply use the fact that by a Bernstein and union bound (cf.
Lemma

3.1
), every
S_{\ell}
has size at most
k+O(\sqrt{k\log(n)})
w.h.p. By our assumption
\varepsilon\gg\sqrt{k/\log(n)}
, this implies that

\mathcal{A}(G,(1+\varepsilon)k)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\{\sum_{v\in[n]\setminus S_{\ell}}x_{v}\geqslant(1+\varepsilon)k-k+O(\sqrt{k\log(n)})\geqslant 1\}.
Hence, the proof from
Lemma

6.17
carries over, and the statement follows using the same argument as used in the previous paragraph.
∎

7
Analysis for exact recovery

In this section, we show correctness of the algorithm from
Theorem

1.3
, i.e., we prove that
achieves exact recovery, even under presence of a monotone adversary. The analysis is split into two parts: First, we prove that every ground truth clique
S_{\ell}
is contained in the output
\mathcal{L}
, and afterwards, we show that no clique that is not ground-truth is added to the output. For convenience, we restate the algorithm here.

Algorithm 1

Recovering the labels of a dense RIG with One-Sided Noise.

algorithm]alg:splitting-one-sided

Given:

A graph
G\sim{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
on vertex set
[n]
modified by an arbitrary monotone adversary.

Output:

A list
\mathcal{L}
of
d
cliques in
G
such that exactly one
S\in\mathcal{L}
agrees with
S_{\ell}
for every
\ell\in[d]
.

Operation:

1.

Split
[n]
into two disjoint sets
U,V
using an independent, fair coin flip for every
v\in[n]
.

2.

Set
\gamma\coloneqq\frac{1}{\alpha}\max\{16p^{-6},1000\}\max\{1,(p/1-p)^{6}\}
, and
t\coloneqq 2\lceil\log_{1/p}(\gamma)\rceil
. Choose a list
\mathcal{T}
of
O(n^{\alpha(1+t/2)})

t
-tuples from
\binom{[n]}{t}
uniformly at random.

3.

For each
T\in\mathcal{T}
, compute the bipartite graph
H(T)=G[U\uplus V]\cap N_{G}(T)

4.

If feasible, compute a degree-
12
pseudo-distribution
\mu
in variables
w
satisfying the biclique axioms
\mathcal{B}(H(T),k_{\text{(bip)}})
for a sufficiently small constant
\varepsilon>0
and
k_{(\text{bip})}\coloneqq\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}k
. Otherwise, proceed with the next
T\in\mathcal{T}
.

5.

Let
S_{T}=\{v\in[n]\mid\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{v}}}\right]\geqslant 1-\sqrt{\varepsilon}\ \}
.

6.

Combinatorial clean-up: remove from
S_{T}
all vertices that have fewer than
(1-5\sqrt{\varepsilon})k
neighbors in
S_{T}
. Then, add all vertices from
[n]\setminus S_{T}
with at least
(1-5\sqrt{\varepsilon})k
neighbors in
S_{T}
. If
G[S_{T}]
is a clique of size at least
(1-\varepsilon)k
, add
S_{T}
to the output
\mathcal{L}
.

7.

Finally, for every
S\in\mathcal{L}
, delete all cliques
S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}\setminus\{S\}
such that
S^{\prime}\subseteq S
.

7.1
Preliminaries

Before starting with the actual analysis, we introduce the following certificate used in multiple parts of the analysis. It states that a subset of vertices in a bipartite graph that induces a balanced sub-graph can only contain a very limited number of vertices in any clique. This works whenever we have a balancedness of roughly
ck\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((1-p)/p}}\right)^{r}
(for
c>0
being a sufficiently small constant) since the certificates from
Lemma

5.9
and
Lemma

5.12
are meaningful whenever
|y|>\Delta

Lemma 7.1

(Bounding biclique sizes in balanced bipartite graphs)
.

Let
H=(A\uplus B,E)
be a bipartite graph on vertex set
[n]
that contains some set
A_{\text{(sub)}}\subseteq A
such that
\widetilde{H}\coloneqq H[A_{\text{(sub)}}\uplus B]
has
r
-fold balancedness at most
\Delta\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1-p}{p}}}\right)^{r}\frac{11}{12}k
for some fixed
r\in\mathbb{N},r\geqslant 2
. Then for some sufficiently small constant
\varepsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}(H,k)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{15.10532pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x,y}$}}}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{4r}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\bigg(\sum_{i\in A_{\text{(sub)}}}x_{i}\bigg)^{r}\leqslant O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{n^{2}}{k}}}\right)}}\right\}.

Proof.

We invoke
Lemma

5.9
for on the balanced graph
H^{\prime}
with bipartition
A^{\prime}\uplus B
. This yields that

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(\widetilde{H})\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{15.10532pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x,y}$}}}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{4r}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1-p}{p}}}\right)^{r}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{S\subseteq A_{\text{(sub)}},|S|=r}x_{S}}}\right)^{2}|y|\\
&\hskip 56.9055pt\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n+rn^{2}}}\right)\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{r}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{S\subseteq A_{\text{(sub)}},|S|=r}x_{S}}}\right)+\Delta\hskip 1.42271pt\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{S\subseteq A_{\text{(sub)}},|S|=r}x_{S}}}\right)^{2}\end{aligned}}}\right\}.

By the size constraint in
\mathcal{B}(H,\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}k)
, we have that
|y|\geqslant k
. So using
\Delta\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1-p}{p}}}\right)^{r}\frac{11k}{12}
and
\mathcal{R}(H^{\prime})\subseteq\mathcal{B}(H,\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}k)
, we get that

\mathcal{B}(H,k)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{15.10532pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x,y}$}}}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{4r}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1-p}{p}}}\right)^{r}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{S\subseteq A_{\text{(sub)}},|S|=r}x_{S}}}\right)^{2}\frac{k}{12}\leqslant(r+1)n^{2}\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{r}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{S\subseteq A_{\text{(sub)}},|S|=r}x_{S}}}\right)}}\right\}.

Dividing by
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1-p}{p}}}\right)^{r}
and using the cancellations
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{f^{2}\leqslant Cf}}\right\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.91432pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.91432pt\raisebox{-6.82222pt}{\makebox[11.91432pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f}$}}}\hskip-11.91432pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.91432pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2r}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{f^{2}\leqslant C^{2}}}\right\}
, and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{f^{2}\leqslant C}}\right\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.91432pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.91432pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[11.91432pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-11.91432pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.91432pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2r}$}}}$}}}\{f\leqslant\sqrt{C}\}
from
Lemma

5.5
, we get that

\mathcal{B}(H,k)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{15.10532pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x,y}$}}}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{4r}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\bigg(\sum_{S\subseteq A_{\text{(sub)}},|S|=r}x_{S}\bigg)\leqslant\frac{12(r+1)n^{2}}{k}\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{1,\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{2r}}}\right\},

and by
Lemma

5.7
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}(H,k)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{15.10532pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x,y}$}}}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{4r}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} &\frac{1}{2^{r}r!}\bigg(\sum_{i\in A_{\text{(sub)}}}x_{i}\bigg)^{r}\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{S\subseteq A_{\text{(sub)}},|S|=r}x_{S}}}\right)+\frac{2r^{r}}{r!}+\varepsilon\end{aligned}}}\right\},

so using that
r
is a constant and combining the above with the previous observation, we get that

\mathcal{B}(H,k)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{15.10532pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x,y}$}}}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{4r}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\bigg(\sum_{i\in A_{\text{(sub)}}}x_{i}\bigg)^{r}\leqslant O\bigg(\frac{n^{2}}{k}\bigg)}}\right\},

as desired.
∎

Now, the analysis is split into two parts. First, we show that for every
S_{\ell}
, the algorithm encounters a
T\in\mathcal{T}
such that the set
S_{T}
is precisely
S_{\ell}
after step 6. Afterwards, we show that no set
S
with
S\neq S_{\ell}
for all
\ell\in[d]
is ever added to the output.

7.2
Step 1: All ground-truth cliques are found

We crucially rely on the balancedness of the bipartite graph
H(T)\coloneqq G[U\uplus V]\cap N(T)
for some of the
t
-tuples
T\in\mathcal{T}
chosen in step
1
.

Lemma 7.2
.

With probability
\geqslant 0.99
, the following holds after step 2 of
. For every
\ell\in[d]
, there is a
t
-tuple
T\in\mathcal{T}
such that the bipartite graphs

H_{L}(T,\ell)\coloneqq G[(U\setminus S_{\ell})\uplus V]\cap N_{G}(T)\text{ and }H_{R}(T,\ell)\coloneqq G[(V\setminus S_{\ell})\uplus U]\cap N_{G}(T)

have
3
-fold balancedness at most
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1-p}{p}}}\right)^{3}\frac{k}{4}
.

Proof.

We prove the lemma only for
H_{L}(T,\ell)
, the proof for
H_{R}(T,\ell)
works analogously.
Our statement is a consequence of
Lemma

4.11
which states that for all
\ell\in[d]
, the neighbourhood of most
t
-tuples in
S_{\ell}
contains a balanced bipartite graph whose bipartition is formed by a procedure
Alg
that only has access to
S_{\ell}
and
[n]\setminus S_{\ell}
. In our case, we choose
\textsc{Alg}(S_{\ell},[n]\setminus S_{\ell})
to be the (deterministic) procedure that outputs the bipartition
(U\setminus S_{\ell})\uplus V
where
U,V
were decided in step 1 of
. Now let
\gamma\coloneqq\frac{1}{\alpha}\max\{16p^{-6},1000\}\max\{1,(p/1-p)^{6}\}
. Since
t=2\lceil\log_{1/p}(\gamma)\rceil
, we get
3
-fold balancedness

\displaystyle\Delta

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{16p^{-6}}{\alpha}\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{3}\ tp^{t}k+o(k)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{16p^{-6}}{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{\gamma}}}\right)^{2}2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\log_{1/p}(\gamma)+1}}\right)\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{3}k+o(k)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{2}{\gamma}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\log_{1/p}(\gamma)+1}}\right)\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{1,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1-p}{p}}}\right)}}\right\}^{6}\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{3}k+o(k)\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1-p}{p}}}\right)^{3}\frac{k}{4},

where the last step follows since
\frac{2}{x}(\log_{2}(x)+1)\leqslant\frac{1}{5}
for all
x\geqslant 1000
and since
\gamma\geqslant 1000
.

It remains to show that
finds such a tuple for every
\ell\in[d]
in step 1. To this end, note that
Lemma

4.11
tells us that a randomly chosen
t
-tuple from
\binom{S_{\ell}}{t}
is such that the graph
G[(U\setminus S_{\ell})\uplus V]\cap N_{G}(T)
has the desired balancedness with probability
1-o(1)
. Hence, when sampling a
t
-tuple uniformly at random from
[n]
, the probability of finding such a tuple in
S_{\ell}
is
\Omega((k/n)^{t})
. By the analysis of the coupon collector process we get that after
O(d\log(d)(n/k)^{t})
samples, we found one such tuple for each
S_{\ell},\ell\in[d]
with probability
\geqslant 0.99
. We finish the proof by noting that
d\log(d)(n/k)^{t}=O(\log(d)d^{1+\frac{t\alpha}{2}})=O(n^{\alpha\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{t}{2}}}\right)})
.
∎

By the above, we can assume that we are working with a balanced bipartite graph
H(T)=G[U\uplus V]\cap N_{G}(T)
for some
T\subseteq S_{\ell}
as of now. We show that in such a graph, a degree
12
pseudo-distribution satisfying the axioms
\mathcal{B}(H(T),\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}k)
allows us to recover the set
S_{\ell}
. To this end, we need the following lemma that yields a SoS certificate for the absence of a
O(\sqrt{n})\times\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}k
biclique in
H_{L}(T,\ell)
and
H_{R}(T,\ell)
.

Lemma 7.3
.

Assume
T
is a
t
-tuple
T\subseteq S_{\ell}
such that
H_{L}(T,\ell)
, and
H_{R}(T,\ell)
meet the balancedness criteria from
Lemma

7.2
, then

\mathcal{B}(H(T),k_{\text{(bip)}})\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{15.10532pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x,y}$}}}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{12}$}}}$}}}\bigg\{\sum_{i\in U\setminus S_{\ell}}x_{i},\sum_{i\in V\setminus S_{\ell}}y_{i}\leqslant O(\sqrt{n})\bigg\}

and bit complexity of the proof is
n^{O(1)}
.

Proof.

Note that
H_{L}(T,\ell),H_{R}(T,\ell)
are subgraphs of
H(T)
with balancedness at most
\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{r}\frac{k}{4}
for all
3\leqslant r\leqslant t
by
Lemma

7.2
.
Thus, invoking
Lemma

7.1
with
r=3
, we get that

\mathcal{B}(H(T),k_{\text{(bip)}})\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{15.10532pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x,y}$}}}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{12}$}}}$}}}\bigg\{\bigg(\sum_{i\in U\setminus S_{\ell}}x_{i}\bigg)^{3}\leqslant O\Big(\frac{n^{2}}{k}\Big)\bigg\}.

Combining this with the fact that
\mathcal{B}(H(T),k_{\text{(bip)}})\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{15.10532pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x,y}$}}}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{12}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\sum_{i\in U\setminus S_{\ell}}x_{i}\leqslant 2k}}\right\}
, we get that

\mathcal{B}(H(T),k_{\text{(bip)}})\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{15.10532pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x,y}$}}}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{12}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{(\sum_{i\in U\setminus S_{\ell}}x_{i})^{4}\leqslant O(n^{2})}}\right\}.
Applying the cancellation
\{f^{2}\leqslant C\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.68402pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.68402pt\raisebox{-6.82222pt}{\makebox[8.68402pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f}$}}}\hskip-8.68402pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.68402pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}$}}}\{f\leqslant\sqrt{C}\}
from
Lemma

5.5
twice, we arrive at the desired conclusion. An analogous argument for
H_{R}(T,\ell)
also yields the second part of the statement.
∎

The duality between SoS-proofs and pseudo-distributions then immediately yields that we can recover a biclique that has large intersection with
S_{\ell}
and small intersection with
[n]\setminus S_{\ell}
by taking
S_{T}
as specified in step
5
.

Lemma 7.4
.

Assume
T
is a
t
-tuple
T\subseteq S_{\ell}
such that
H_{L}(T,\ell)
, and
H_{R}(T,\ell)
meet the balancedness criteria from
Lemma

7.2
. Then for any degree
12
pseudo-distribution
\mu
satisfying
\mathcal{B}(H(T),\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}k)
in variables
w=\{\{x_{v}\}_{v\in U},\{y_{v}\}_{v\in V}\}
, we have
|S_{T}\triangle S_{\ell}|\leqslant 4\sqrt{\varepsilon}k
after step 5 of
.

Proof.

First of all, observe that for every
S_{\ell}
, we get that over step 1 of the algorithm, we have
\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}k\leqslant|S_{\ell}\cap U|,|S_{\ell}\cap V|\leqslant\frac{1+\varepsilon}{2}k
for any fixed
\varepsilon>0
, with high probability. This follows from standard Chernoff- and subsequent union bounds. Using
Lemma

7.3
combined with the duality between SoS-proofs and pseudo-distributions, we get that
\sum_{i\in(U\cup V)\setminus S_{\ell}}\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}[w_{i}]\leqslant O(\sqrt{n})
.

This already implies that
|S\setminus S_{\ell}|=o(k)
since at most
O(\sqrt{n})
vertices
i
outside of
S_{\ell}
are such that
\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}[x_{i}]\geqslant 1-\varepsilon
. Moreover, since
\sum_{i\in U\cup V}\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}[w_{i}]=2\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}k\geqslant(1-\varepsilon)k
, we get that
\sum_{i\in S_{\ell}}\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}[w_{i}]\geqslant(1-\varepsilon)k-o(k)
. This implies that on average over
i\in S_{\ell}
,
\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}[w_{i}]\geqslant 1-2\varepsilon
. Hence, by Markov’s inequality, there are at most
2\sqrt{\varepsilon}k
vertices in
S_{\ell}
with
\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}[w_{i}]<1-\sqrt{\varepsilon}
. With this, it follows that
|S_{\ell}\triangle S_{T}|\leqslant 4\sqrt{\varepsilon}k
, as desired.
∎

What is remarkable about the above is that the conclusion still holds after allowing a monotone adversary to modify
G
. This is despite the fact that the resulting modified graph
G^{\prime}
and the associated bipartite graphs considered by
might not be balanced anymore. The conclusions hold nonetheless since the computed pseudo-distributions satisfy a useful monotonicity property.

Lemma 7.5
.

Given
G\sim{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
, let
\widetilde{G}
denote
G
after an arbitrary subset of edges
\{u,v\}\in E(G)
with
M_{u}\cap M_{v}=\emptyset
has been deleted. Furthermore, analogously to
H(T)
, define

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}(T)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\widetilde{G}[U\uplus V]\cap N_{\widetilde{G}}(T).

Then
Lemma

7.4
still holds when replacing
H(T)
by
\widetilde{H}(T)
.

Proof.

Lemma

7.4
holds for all pseudo-distributions
\mu\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-1.23135pt]{15.10532pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-15.10532pt\rule[0.75348pt]{15.10532pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{-5.96742pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x,y}$}}}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{2.95354pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{12}$}}}$}}}\mathcal{B}(H_{L}(T,\ell),\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}k)
. Since
\widetilde{H}(T)\subseteq H(T)
if
T\subseteq S_{\ell}
for any
\ell\in[d]
, we get that every degree
\geqslant 12
pseudo-distribution
\widetilde{\mu}
satisfying
\mathcal{B}(\widetilde{H}(T),\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}k)
is also a pseudo-distribution satisfying
\mathcal{B}(H,\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}k)
. Moreover, since all edges within
S_{\ell}
remain unchanged when passing from
G
to
\widetilde{G}
, we get that
S_{\ell}\in\widetilde{H}
, so there further exists at least one pseudo-distribution
\widetilde{\mu}
satisfying
\mathcal{B}(\widetilde{H},\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}k)
(take for example any actual probability distribution that puts all its mass on
\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}k
vertices in
U\cap S_{\ell}
and
V\cap S_{\ell}
, respectively).
∎

Finally, it only remains to argue that the combinatorial clean-up in step 6 exactly recovers
S_{\ell}
.

Lemma 7.6
.

Assume
T
is a
t
-tuple
T\subseteq S_{\ell}
such that
H_{L}(T,\ell)
, and
H_{R}(T,\ell)
meet the balancedness criteria from
Lemma

7.2
. Then, after step 6, we have
S_{T}=S_{\ell}
. The lemma continues to hold if we replace
G
by
\widetilde{G}
as specified in
Lemma

7.5
.

Proof.

We note that by
Lemma

7.4
, we find a set
S_{T}
with
|S_{\ell}\triangle S_{T}|\leqslant 4\sqrt{\varepsilon}k
. Since
|S_{\ell}|\geqslant(1-o(1))k
w.h.p., we get that every
v\in S_{\ell}\cap S_{T}
has at least
(1-5\sqrt{\varepsilon})k
neighbours in
S_{T}
, so these are not deleted. Moreover, by
Lemma

4.15
, each
v\in S_{T}\setminus S_{\ell}
has at most
(1+o(1))pk+4\sqrt{\varepsilon}k<(1-5\sqrt{\varepsilon})k
neighbours in
S_{T}
, so they are removed (if
\varepsilon
is sufficiently small). Finally, vertices in
S_{\ell}\setminus S_{T}
have at least
(1-5\sqrt{\varepsilon})k
neighbours in
S_{T}
, so these vertices are added. Hence,
S_{T}=S_{\ell}
after step 6.
The same argument applies to
\widetilde{G}
by
Lemma

7.5
.
∎

7.3
Step 2: No other cliques are found

We proceed by showing that the algorithm never finds a clique outside of the ground-truth.

Lemma 7.7
.

Let
T\in\binom{[n]}{t}
be any
t
-tuple in
G\sim{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
. Then, after step 6 of
, the set
S_{T}
is either discarded or a subset of
S_{\ell}
for some
\ell\in[d]
.

With the above lemma and
Lemma

7.6
it immediately follows that the clean-up in step
7
yields a list
\mathcal{L}
that contains exactly the ground-truth cliques
S_{\ell}
for all
\ell\in[d]
, so
Theorem

1.3
follows.

To prove
Lemma

7.7
, we recall that given a
T\in\binom{[n]}{t}
, the set
\mathcal{B}_{T}\subseteq[d]
denotes the set of labels that have been chosen by at least three vertices in
T
. Moreover,
V(\mathcal{B}_{T})
is the set of vertices in
[n]
that have chosen a label in
\mathcal{B}_{T}
. The key observation we use (reminiscent of the ideas in
Section

6.2.1
) is that for
all

T\in\binom{[n]}{t}
, the axioms
\mathcal{B}(H(T),k_{\text{(bip)}})
are sufficient to show that not many vertices can be in
V(\mathcal{B}_{T})
.

Lemma 7.8
.

With high probability over the draw of
G\sim{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
, the following holds.
For all
T\subseteq\binom{[n]}{t}
,

\mathcal{B}(H(T),k_{\text{(bip)}})\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{15.10532pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x,y}$}}}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{12}$}}}$}}}\bigg\{\sum_{i\in U\setminus V(\mathcal{B}_{T})}x_{i},\sum_{i\in V\setminus V(\mathcal{B}_{T})}y_{i}\leqslant O(\sqrt{n})\bigg\}.

This has the implication that the pseudo expectation on
[n]\setminus V(\mathcal{B}_{T})
is
O(\sqrt{n})
. Since the number of labels in
\mathcal{B}_{T}
is bounded w.h.p., this allows us to use a simple argument invoking the combinatorial insights from
Section

4.4
to show that exact recovery succeeds.

Proof of
Lemma

7.7
.

By
Lemma

7.8
, we get that for all
T\in\binom{[n]}{t}
, the set
S_{T}
is such that
|S_{T}\cap([n]\setminus V(\mathcal{B}_{T}))|\leqslant O(\sqrt{n})
. Now, whenever
|S_{T}|\geqslant(1-\varepsilon)k
after the clean up in step
6
, then
we get from the fact that
|\mathcal{B}_{T}|\leqslant\frac{4t}{\alpha}
by
Lemma

4.6
, that there is some
\ell\in\mathcal{B}_{T}
such that

|S_{T}\cap S_{\ell}|\geqslant\frac{\alpha}{8t}k.

Since we can further assume
S_{T}
to induce a clique in
G
, we can now rely on the combinatorial properties regarding cliques intersecting ground-truth cliques from
Section

4.4
.
Precisely, by
Lemma

4.13
, we get that with high probability, the event
\mathcal{E}_{a,b}
for
b=\lfloor\log(n)^{2}\rfloor
, any
a\geqslant n^{\varepsilon}k/\sqrt{d}
and any
\varepsilon>0
does not occur. Hence, for sufficiently small
\varepsilon
, we get
a\ll\frac{\alpha}{8t}k
, so the non-occurrence of
\mathcal{E}_{a,b}
implies that
|S_{T}\setminus S_{\ell}|\leqslant\lfloor\log(n)^{2}\rfloor
. Since
|S_{T}|\geqslant(1-\varepsilon)k
, this implies that all
v\in S_{T}\setminus S_{\ell}
have degree at least
(1-2\varepsilon)k
into
S_{\ell}
. For sufficiently small
\varepsilon
, this contradicts the degree bound from
Lemma

4.15
, so the only way that
S_{T}
is not discarded at the end of step
6
is that is is a subset of
S_{\ell}
.
∎

It only remains to prove
Lemma

7.8
. Since the ideas are quite similar to arguments already employed in
Section

6.2.2
and
Section

7.2
, we only sketch the proof to avoid repetition.

Proof of
Lemma

7.8
.

Similar to the definition of
H_{L}(T,\ell)
in
Lemma

7.2
, we define
F_{L}(T)\coloneqq G[(U\setminus V(\mathcal{B}_{T}))\uplus T]\cap N_{G}(T).

Similarly as in the proof of
Lemma

6.11
, we get from
Lemma

4.10
with
\eta=2
,
L(T)=\mathcal{B}_{T}
, and
S(T)=V(\mathcal{B}_{T})
, that
F_{L}(T)
has
3
-fold balancedness

\frac{16tp^{-2}}{\alpha}\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{3}\cdot p^{t}k+o(k),

and w.h.p., this holds for all
T
.
Using the same calculation as in
Lemma

7.2
, we then get that for our choice of
t
,
F_{L}(T)
has
3
-fold balancedness at most
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((1-p)/p}}\right)^{3}\frac{k}{4}
. Then, employing the same reasoning as in
Lemma

7.3
yields the desired statement. ∎

8
Analysis for approximate recovery

Algorithm 2

Recovering the labels of an RIG under a bounded adversary.

algorithm]alg:recovery-approx

Given:

A graph
G\sim{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
on vertex set
[n]
modified by an adversary that alter up to
\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k
edges incident to every
v\in[n]
and arbitrarily alter the neighbourhood of up to
\varepsilon^{\text{(node)}}k
vertices. In addition to that, a monotone adversary can delete an arbitrary amount of edges
\{u,v\}\in E(G)
such that
M_{v}\cap M_{u}=\emptyset
. Denote the modified graph obtained in this way by
\widetilde{G}
,

Output:

A list
\mathcal{L}
of
d
sets of vertices in
[n]
such that for every
\ell\in[d]
, there is exactly one
S\in\mathcal{L}
such that
|S_{\ell}\triangle S|\leqslant O(\varepsilon k)
.

Operation:

1.

Split
[n]
into two disjoint sets
U,V
using an independent, fair coin flip for every
v\in[n]
.

2.

Set
\gamma\coloneqq\frac{1}{\alpha}\max\{16p^{-6},1000\}\max\{1,(p/1-p)^{6}\}
, and
t=2\lceil\log_{1/p}(\gamma)\rceil
. Choose a list
\mathcal{T}
of
O(n^{\alpha(1+t/2)})

t
-tuples from
\binom{[n]}{t}
uniformly at random.

3.

Let
\widetilde{k}=(1-2\varepsilon^{\text{(node)}})k
. For each
T\in\mathcal{T}
, compute a degree-
O(t)
pseudo-distribution
\mu
on variables
w
satisfying the axioms
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\widetilde{G},U,V,\widetilde{k},\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k)\cup\{w_{T}=1\}
(see (
8.1
) for a definition) that maximizes
\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[|w|}}\right]
.

4.

Let
S_{T}=\{v\in[n]\mid\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{v}}}\right]\geqslant 1-\rho\}
for some small constant
\rho\leqslant\frac{1}{8t}
.

5.

If
|S_{T}|<(1-5\varepsilon^{\text{(node)}}/\rho)k
, discard
S_{T}
and continue with the next
T\in\mathcal{T}
. Otherwise, add
S_{T}
to the output
\mathcal{L}
.

6.

Pruning: For every
S\in\mathcal{L}
, delete from
\mathcal{L}
all sets
S^{\prime}\neq S
such that
|S\triangle S^{\prime}|\leqslant 10\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}/\rho
.

We show that
is robust against a stronger adversary.

8.1
Preliminaries

Before starting with the analysis, we introduce some preliminaries.

Notation

Throughout the rest of this section, we denote by
G
a typical sample from
{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
, and by
\widetilde{G}
the resulting graph after an adversary has added or deleted up to
\varepsilon k^{2}
edges in
G
. Given a vertex
v\in[n]
, we denote by
\widetilde{\deg}(v)
the number of edges the adversary added or deleted
incident to

v
. Moreover, for any
\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k\in\mathbb{R}
, we denote by
M_{\leqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k}
the set of vertices
v\in[n]
such that
\widetilde{\deg}(v)\leqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k
. Similarly,
M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}}
is set of vertices
v\in[n]
such that
\widetilde{\deg}(v)\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k
.

Adversarial Capabilities

In addition to a monotone adversary, we allow further edge corruptions as follows.
We fix some small constant
\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}=\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}
and allow an adversary to arbitrarily modify edges with the constraint that
\widetilde{\deg}(v)\leqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k
for every
v\in[n]
. On top of that, we fix a second constant
\varepsilon^{\text{(node)}}
and allow the adversary to modify up to
\varepsilon^{\text{(node)}}k
vertices
v\in[n]
with
unbounded
adversarial degree
\widetilde{\deg}(v)
.

Note that this is more general than simply allowing node corruptions. It further covers an adversary that can modify
\varepsilon k^{2}
edges arbitrarily. For some
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}},\varepsilon^{\text{(node)}})
. To see this note that allowing for
\varepsilon k^{2}
edge corruptions means that there are at most
\frac{2\varepsilon}{\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}}k
vertices with
\widetilde{\deg}(v)\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k
. Thus, if we can handle our more general adversary with parameters
\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}},\varepsilon^{\text{(node)}}
, then we can handle
\varepsilon k^{2}
edge corruptions for
\varepsilon=2\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}\varepsilon^{\text{(node)}}
.
The main theorem of this section can then be stated as follows.

Theorem 8.1

(Approximate recovery)
.

There exists a polynomial time algorithm that on input
G\sim{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
possibly modified by a monotone adversary and the adversary described above, achieves
O(\varepsilon^{\text{(node)}}k)
-approximate recovery. This holds with high probability over the choice of
G
and for all sufficiently small constants
\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}},\varepsilon^{\text{(node)}}
.

Remark 8.2
.

Notice that the above implies that for
\varepsilon^{\text{(node)}}=0
, we obtain
exact recovery
, even under an adversary, which is allowed to corrupt up to
\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k
edges incident to every vertex.

With
Theorem

8.1
,
Theorem

1.5
follows as a special case as shown by the following argument.

Proof of
Theorem

1.5
with
Theorem

8.1
.

Theorem

8.1
implies
Theorem

1.5
as follows. To handle
\varepsilon k^{2}
edge corruptions, we set
\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}
to a fixed, sufficiently small constant as in
Theorem

8.1
. Then, set
\varepsilon^{\text{(node)}}=\frac{\varepsilon}{2\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}}
and choose
\varepsilon
small enough such that
\varepsilon^{\text{(node)}}
is small enough such that
Theorem

8.1
applies. With these parameters, we claim that
\varepsilon k^{2}
edge corruptions are a special case of the more general adversarial model introduced above. To see this, we simply note that the number of vertices with
\widetilde{\deg}(v)\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k
is at most
\frac{2\varepsilon}{\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}}k
as otherwise we contradict the fact that there are at most
\varepsilon k^{2}
edge corruptions. The recovery guarantee we obtain from
Theorem

8.1
is then
O(\varepsilon^{\text{(node)}}k)=O(\varepsilon k)
, as desired.
∎

The rest of this section is dedicated to proving
Theorem

8.1
.

Axioms

Our approach towards achieving robustness requires a couple more ideas, which we formally describe in this section. Specifically, instead of the axioms
\mathcal{B}(H(T),k_{\text{(bip)}})
for exact recovery, we use the following system of axioms given a graph
G
, a bipartite sub-graph
H=(A\uplus B)
of
G
with
A\cup B=[n]
, a set
T\subseteq[n]
, and parameters
k,\gamma

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(G,A,B,k,\gamma)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} &\forall v\in[n]&w_{v}^{2}&=w_{v}\\
&&2k\geqslant\sum_{v\in A}w_{v}&\geqslant k\\
&&2k\geqslant\sum_{v\in B}w_{v}&\geqslant k\\
&\forall u,v\in[n],u\neq v&z_{u,v}^{2}&=z_{u,v}\\
&\forall u,v\in[n],u\neq v\text{ s.t. }\{u,v\}\notin E(G)&w_{u}w_{v}(1-z_{u,v})&=0\\
&\forall u\in A&\sum_{v\in[n]\setminus\{u\}}z_{u,v}&\leqslant\gamma\end{aligned}}}\right\}.

(8.1)

While the first four constraints are standard to encode a
k\times k
biclique, the rest is specifically tailored towards ensuring robustness to our adversary. The idea is as follows.

1.

The variables
z_{u,v}
are used to encode “adding back” edges that were deleted by the adversary. The objective
w_{u}w_{v}(1-z_{u,v})=0
states that the
w
should form a biclique after adding back the edges represented by
z
.

2.

The conditions
\sum_{v\in[n]\setminus\{u\}}z_{u,v}\leqslant\gamma
encode the constraint that incident to any vertex
v
, we only add back up to
\gamma
edges.

8.2
Key SoS-certificates

Before starting with the analysis, we the most important certificates used during the analysis. The first is an analogue of
Lemma

7.1
. The difference is that we are additionally given a
t
-tuple
T
, and deal with a different bipartite sub-graph of
G
.

Lemma 8.3
.

Fix some integer
t
. Assume we are given a graph
G
on vertex set
[n]
, a bipartite sub-graph
H=(A\uplus B,E_{H})
of
G
, a set
T\subseteq[n]
with
|T|=t
, and parameters
k,\gamma
.
Assume further that there is some
A_{\text{(sub)}}\subseteq A
such that
G[A_{\text{(sub)}}\uplus(N_{G}({T})\cap B)]
has
r
-fold balancedness at most
\Delta\coloneqq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((1-p)/p}}\right)^{r}\frac{10k}{12}
for some fixed
r\in\mathbb{N},r\geqslant 2
. Then, there is some
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
such that for all
\gamma\leqslant\varepsilon^{\prime}k
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(G,A,B,k,\gamma)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{w}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{w_{T}\ \bigg(\sum_{v\in A_{\text{(sub)}}}w_{v}\bigg)^{r}\leqslant O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{n^{2}}{k}}}\right)\ w_{T}}}\right\}.

Moreover, we need the following certificate, which states that forcing a tuple
T
to be in our clique implies that there are only a few ground-truth cliques that the clique we are searching for can overlap with.
It holds whenever we force our clique to contain any given
t
-tuple
T
such that
\widetilde{\deg}(v)\leqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k
for all
v\in T
. We call such
t
-tuples
\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k
-
good
. The certificate is then stated as follows.

Lemma 8.4
.

Let
T\in\binom{[n]}{t}
. Let further
\mathcal{B}_{T}\subseteq[d]
be the set of labels that appears in at least
3
vertices of
T
, and let
V(\mathcal{B}_{T})=\bigcup_{\ell\in\mathcal{B}_{T}}S_{\ell}
be the set of vertices that have chosen at least one of these labels. Moreover, let
\widetilde{V}(\mathcal{B}_{T})\coloneqq V(\mathcal{B}_{T})\cup M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k}
and let
\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}
be small enough.
Then, with high probability over the draw of
G\sim{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
, the following holds for all
\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k
-good
t
-tuples
T\in\binom{[n]}{t}
.

\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\widetilde{G},U,V,\widetilde{k},\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{w}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\bigg\{w_{T}\bigg(\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus\widetilde{V}(\mathcal{B}_{T})}w_{i}\bigg)\leqslant O(\sqrt{n})\ w_{T}\bigg\}.

Remark 8.5
.

What is important to note about the above is that we explicitly do not add the axiom
\{x_{T}=1\}
. The above holds for any
\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k
-good
T\in\binom{[n]}{t}

without
the need to give
T
as input to some algorithm. This also applies to the following
Lemma

8.6
.

After having established “concentration” to the cliques in
\mathcal{B}_{T}
(with the certificate above), the following asserts that our clique cannot have large overlap with both
S_{\ell}
and
[n]\setminus S_{\ell}
for all labels
\ell\in\mathcal{B}_{T}
. It states that forcing (in addition to
T
) a single vertex
i\in[n]\setminus S_{\ell}
to be in our clique, the mass on
S_{\ell}
is
o(k)
.

Lemma 8.6
.

With high probability, the following holds over the draw of
G\sim{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
and all sufficiently small
\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}
. For all
T\in\binom{[n]}{t}
that are
\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k
-good, every
\ell\in\mathcal{B}_{T}
, and all
i\in[n]\setminus(S_{\ell}\cup M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}})
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\widetilde{G},U,V,\widetilde{k},\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{w}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\Bigg\{w_{T}w_{i}\ \bigg(\sum_{v\in S_{\ell}\setminus M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}}}w_{v}\bigg)\leqslant\beta(k)\ w_{T}w_{i}\ \Bigg\}.

for some
\beta(k)=o(k)
.

8.3
Pseudo-Concentration

The key tool in our analysis (and one of the main difference to the previous section) is a
concentration property
for pseudo-distributions satisfying the axioms in
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\widetilde{G},U,V,\widetilde{k},\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k)
. It is key to showing that we
never
find a set of vertices that does not have large overlap with some ground-truth clique.

Lemma 8.7

(Concentration lemma for pseudo-distributions)
.

Let
\mu
be a degree-
O(t)
pseudo-distribution satisfying
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\widetilde{G},U,V,\widetilde{k},\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k)
. Fix a constant
\rho
and let
S\coloneqq\big\{i\in[n]\mid\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}[w_{i}]\geqslant 1-\rho\big\}
. Let further
M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}}
be the set of vertices with
\widetilde{\deg}(v)\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k
.

Then, whenever
\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}},\varepsilon^{\text{(node)}}
are sufficiently small and
\rho\leqslant\frac{1}{8t}
, the following holds with high probability over the draw of
G\sim{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
. For every degree-
O(t)
pseudo-distribution
\mu
satisfying
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\widetilde{G},U,V,\widetilde{k},\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k)
such that
|S|\geqslant\frac{1}{2}k
, there is some
\ell\in[d]
such that
S\subseteq S_{\ell}\cup M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}}
.

The idea behind establishing the above is as follows. Since
M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}}\leqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(node)}}k
is sufficiently small and
|S|
is sufficiently large, there must be a
t
-tuple
T
in
S\setminus M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}}
such that
\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{T}}}\right]=\Omega(1)
. Then, by the certificate in
Lemma

8.4
, this already implies that only a small part of
|S|
is not in
\widetilde{V}(\mathcal{B}_{T})
.
This is already a concentration statement, which now can be “bootstrapped” into an even stronger one. Concretely, by the bounded number of labels in
\mathcal{B}_{T}
, we can infer that there is some
\ell\in\mathcal{B}_{T}
such that
|S\cap S_{\ell}|=\Omega(k)
. Using the certificate in
Lemma

8.6
, we can show that this implies that
S
must actually be a subset of
S_{\ell}\cup M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}}
.

Establishing pseudo-concentration
