For the proof, we need the following proposition relating the pseudo-expectation of a product to the product of pseudo-expectations, provided the “variance” is small enough.

Lemma 8.8
.

Let
\mu
be a degree-
2d
pseudo-distribution. For any polynomial
f
, define

\widetilde{\mathbb{V}\text{ar}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[f}}\right]\coloneqq\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[f^{2}}}\right]-\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[f}}\right]^{2}.

Then, for any degree two polynomials
f,g
of degree
d
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[fg}}\right]\geqslant\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[f}}\right]\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[g}}\right]-\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\widetilde{\mathbb{V}\text{ar}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[f}}\right]+\widetilde{\mathbb{V}\text{ar}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[g}}\right]}}\right).

Proof.

By Hölder’s inequality
Lemma

5.2
,

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leqslant\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[(f+g)^{2}}}\right]-\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[(f+g)}}\right]^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt=(\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[f^{2}}}\right]-\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[f}}\right]^{2})+(\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[g^{2}}}\right]-\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[g}}\right]^{2})+2\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[fg}}\right]-2\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[f}}\right]\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[g}}\right].

Re-arranging and applying the definition of
\widetilde{\mathbb{V}\text{ar}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[f}}\right]
then yields the statement.
∎

We start by showing that there is indeed some
t
-tuple
T
in
S\setminus M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}}
such that
\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{T}}}\right]=\Omega(1)
.

Lemma 8.9

(Constant expectation on some
T
)
.

Assume the setting from
Lemma

8.7
and let further
\widetilde{S}\coloneqq S\setminus M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}}
. Then for all
0<\rho<1
and all
\varepsilon^{\text{(node)}}\leqslant\frac{1}{3}
, there is some
T\in\widetilde{S}
such that
\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{T}}}\right]\geqslant(1-\rho)^{t}-o(1)
.

Proof.

By construction of
S
, we have
\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}[\sum_{v\in\widetilde{S}}w_{v}]\geqslant(1-\varepsilon)|\widetilde{S}|.
Hence, by Hölder’s inequality
Lemma

5.2
,

\displaystyle(1-\rho)^{t}|\widetilde{S}|^{t}\leqslant\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\bigg[\sum_{v\in\widetilde{S}}w_{v}\bigg]^{t}

\displaystyle\leqslant\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\bigg[\bigg(\sum_{v\in\widetilde{S}}w_{v}\bigg)^{t}\bigg]=t!\sum_{s=1}^{t}\sum_{T\subseteq\widetilde{S},|T|=s}\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{T}}}\right]\leqslant

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274ptt!\binom{|\widetilde{S}|}{t}\max_{T\subseteq\widetilde{S},|T|=s}\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{T}}}\right]+t!|\widetilde{S}|^{t-1}\leqslant|\widetilde{S}|^{t}\max_{T\subseteq\widetilde{S},|T|=s}\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{T}}}\right]+t!|\widetilde{S}|^{t-1}.

Dividing by
|\widetilde{S}|^{t}
, we get

(1-\rho)^{t}\leqslant\max_{T\subseteq\widetilde{S},|T|=s}\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{T}}}\right]\ +t!/|\widetilde{S}|.

Since
|S|\geqslant\frac{1}{2}k
and
|M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}}|\leqslant\frac{1}{3}k
, we get
\widetilde{S}\geqslant\frac{1}{6}k
, so in fact
t!/|\widetilde{S}|=o(1)
and

\max_{T\subseteq\widetilde{S},|T|=s}\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{T}}}\right]\geqslant(1-\rho)^{t}-o(1),

as desired. ∎

Next, we use the certificate from
Lemma

8.4
to show the following preliminary concentration result stating that only few vertices in
S
are outside of
\widetilde{V}(\mathcal{B}_{T})
for some
T
.

Lemma 8.10
.

Assume the setting from
Lemma

8.7
and let further
\widetilde{S}\coloneqq S\setminus M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}}
. Then whenever
\ \varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}
is sufficiently small and
\rho\leqslant\frac{1}{4t}
, there is some
T\subseteq\widetilde{S}
with
|T|=t
such that

\big|S\setminus\widetilde{V}(\mathcal{B}_{T})\big|\leqslant O(\sqrt{n}).

Proof.

Let
T\subseteq\widetilde{S}
with
|T|=t
such that
\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{T}}}\right]\geqslant(1-\rho)^{t}-o(1)
, which exists due to
Lemma

8.9
.
The certificate from
Lemma

8.4
now tells us that

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in S\setminus\widetilde{V}(\mathcal{B}_{T})}\frac{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{T}w_{i}}}\right]}{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{T}}}\right]}\leqslant\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus\widetilde{V}(\mathcal{B}_{T})}\frac{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{T}w_{i}}}\right]}{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{T}}}\right]}\leqslant O(\sqrt{n}).

(8.2)

We now wish to find a lower bound on the quantity
\frac{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{T}w_{i}}}\right]}{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{T}}}\right]}
in the leftmost sum.

Towards this end, we use
Lemma

8.8
to obtain

\frac{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{T}w_{i}}}\right]}{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{T}}}\right]}\geqslant\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{i}}}\right]-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\widetilde{\mathbb{V}\text{ar}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{T}}}\right]+\widetilde{\mathbb{V}\text{ar}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{i}}}\right]}{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{T}}}\right]}.

We use that

\widetilde{\mathbb{V}\text{ar}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{T}}}\right]=\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{T}}}\right](1-\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{T}}}\right])
because
\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{T}^{2}}}\right]=\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{T}}}\right]
, and that
\widetilde{\mathbb{V}\text{ar}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{v}}}\right]=\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{i}^{2}}}\right]-\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{i}}}\right]^{2}\leqslant 2\rho
because
\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{i}^{2}}}\right]\leqslant 1
and
\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{i}}}\right]^{2}=(1-\rho)^{2}\geqslant 1-2\rho
. Plugging these bounds in yields that

\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{T}w_{i}}}\right]/\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{T}}}\right]>0
for all
t\geqslant 1
and
\rho\leqslant\frac{1}{4t}
. Since for this choice of parameters, this holds uniformly for all
i\in S\setminus\widetilde{V}(\mathcal{B}_{T})
, we get from (
8.2
) that there is some constant
c>0
such that

c\ |S\setminus\widetilde{V}(\mathcal{B}_{T})|\leqslant\sum_{i\in S\setminus\widetilde{V}(\mathcal{B}_{T})}\frac{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{T}w_{i}}}\right]}{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{T}}}\right]}\leqslant O(\sqrt{n})

and the lemma follows.
∎

Now comes the “bootstrapping” step: since by the above most of
S
concentrates on
\widetilde{V}(\mathcal{B}_{T})
, the balancedness properties among the cliques with labels in
\mathcal{B}_{T}
(captured in the certificate from
Lemma

8.6
) yield a significantly improved concentration.

Lemma 8.11

(Bootstrapping the intersection of
S
and
S_{\ell}
)
.

Assume the setting from
Lemma

8.7
.
Assume further that there is some constant
C>0
and
\ell\in
such that
\ell\in\mathcal{B}_{T}
such that
|S\cap(S_{\ell}\setminus M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k})|\geqslant Ck
.
Then, whenever
\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}
is sufficiently small and
\rho\leqslant\frac{1}{8t}
, we have
S\subseteq S_{\ell}\cup M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}}
.

Proof.

We want to show that
S\setminus(S_{\ell}\cup M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k})=\emptyset
. To this end assume for the sake of contradiction that there is a vertex
i\in S\setminus(S_{\ell}\cup M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k})
. Let further
T\subseteq S\setminus M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}}
be a
t
-tuple with
\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{T}}}\right]\geqslant(1-\rho)^{t}-o(1)
, which exists due to
Lemma

8.9
.

By duality between SoS-proof and pseudo-distributions, the certificate from
Lemma

8.6
then yields that

\sum_{v\in S\cap(S_{\ell}\setminus M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}})}\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{T}w_{i}w_{v}}}\right]\leqslant\sum_{v\in S_{\ell}\setminus M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{T}w_{i}w_{v}}}\right]\leqslant\beta(k)\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}[w_{T}w_{i}]\leqslant\beta(k)\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}[w_{T}].

Similarly as in the proof of
Lemma

8.10
, we get

\frac{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{T}w_{i}w_{v}}}\right]}{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{T}}}\right]}\geqslant\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{i}w_{v}}}\right]-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\widetilde{\mathbb{V}\text{ar}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{T}}}\right]+\widetilde{\mathbb{V}\text{ar}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{i}w_{v}}}\right]}{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{T}}}\right]}.

Again, we use
\widetilde{\mathbb{V}\text{ar}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{T}}}\right]=\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{T}}}\right](1-\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{T}}}\right])\leqslant\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{T}}}\right](1-(1-\rho)^{t}+o(1)).
Moreover,

\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{i}w_{v}}}\right]\geqslant\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{i}}}\right]\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{v}}}\right]-\frac{1}{2}(\widetilde{\mathbb{V}\text{ar}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{i}}}\right]+\widetilde{\mathbb{V}\text{ar}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{v}}}\right])\geqslant 1-2\rho-\frac{1}{2}(4\rho)\geqslant 1-4\rho.

Accordingly,

\widetilde{\mathbb{V}\text{ar}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{i}w_{v}}}\right]=\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{i}w_{v}}}\right](1-\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{i}w_{v}}}\right])\leqslant 4\rho.

Plugging these bounds in now yields that

\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{T}w_{i}w_{v}}}\right]/\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{T}}}\right]>0
for all
t\geqslant 1
and
\rho\geqslant\frac{1}{8t}
. Thus, since this holds uniformly for all
v
, going back to the beginning yields

c\ |S\cap(S_{\ell}\setminus M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}})|\leqslant\sum_{v\in S\cap(S_{\ell}\setminus M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}})}\frac{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{T}w_{i}w_{v}}}\right]}{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{T}}}\right]}\leqslant\beta(k).

Since by assumption
|S\cap(S_{\ell}\setminus M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}})|\geqslant Ck
, this is a contradiction since the left side is
\Omega(k)
and
\beta(k)=o(k)
.
∎

With this, we prove
Lemma

8.7
.

Proof of
Lemma

8.7
.

From
Lemma

8.10
, we get that there is a
t
-tuple
T\subseteq S\setminus M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}}
such that
\big|S\setminus\widetilde{V}(\mathcal{B}_{T})\big|\leqslant O(\sqrt{n})
and
\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[w_{T}}}\right]\geqslant(1-\rho)^{t}-o(1)
.
By
Lemma

4.6
, we get that w.h.p.,
|\mathcal{B}_{T}|\leqslant\frac{4t}{\alpha}
for all
T
. Hence for some
\ell\in\mathcal{B}_{T}
, we have

\displaystyle|S\cap(S_{\ell}\setminus M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k})|\geqslant\big(\frac{1}{3}k-|M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}}|\big)\frac{\alpha}{4t}\geqslant\frac{\alpha}{16t}k

(8.3)

for sufficiently small
\varepsilon^{\text{(node)}}
. Now, it follows from
Lemma

8.11
that
S\subseteq S\cup M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}}
, as desired.
∎

8.4
Analysis

8.4.1
Step 1: Ground-truth cliques are found

Again, we rely on balancedness properties. However, clearly we cannot hope to have the same balancedness guarantees as before since an adversary can easily destroy the balancedness properties of a given bipartite graph
F=A\uplus B
already after altering the neighbourhood of only a constant number of vertices. However, it is not hard to see that after removing a small subset of vertices
M\subseteq A
(small in the sense that
|M|\leqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k
), the graph
(A\setminus M)\uplus B
still has sufficiently good balancedness properties. We capture this in the following lemma.

Lemma 8.12

(Balancedness after a bounded adversary)
.

Let
F=(A\uplus B,E)
be a bipartite graph and fix some integer
r\in\mathbb{N},r\geqslant 3
such that
F
has
r
-fold balancedness
\Delta
(cf.
Definition

4.4
). Denote by
\widetilde{F}
the graph
F
after the adversarial modifications and recall that
M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k}
denotes the set of vertices with
\widetilde{\deg}(v)\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k
.
Then, given any
\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}>0
, the graph
F[(A\setminus M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k})\uplus B]
has
r
-fold balancedness at most

\Delta+4r\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{r}.

Proof.

Note that for every
S,R\subseteq A\setminus M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k}
with
|S|=|R|=r
, for all but at most
2r\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k
vertices
v\in B
, the edges between
v
and
S\cup R
remain unchanged because
\widetilde{\deg}(u)\leqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k
for
u\in A\setminus M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k}
. For every
v\in B
for which this is
not
the case, the quantity
u_{S,p}(v)u_{R,p}(v)
can change by at most
2\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{r}
. Accordingly,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\sum_{v\in B}u_{S,p}(v)u_{R,p}(v)}}\right|\leqslant\Delta+4r\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{r}.

∎

We continue start by showing that after step 2 of our algorithm, we again find at least one
t
-tuple
T\in\mathcal{T},T\subseteq S_{\ell}
for each
\ell\in[d]
such that most of
S_{\ell}
is preserved in
N_{G}({T})
while the graphs
\widetilde{H}_{L}(T,\ell,\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k),\widetilde{H}_{R}(T,\ell,\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k)
(defined analogously to
H_{L}(T,\ell),H_{R}(T,\ell)
in the following) are still sufficiently well balanced. These will later serve as a witness for
Lemma

8.3
which in turn yields desirable behaviour of our pseudo-distribution.

Lemma 8.13
.

Given
\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k\in\mathbb{N}
and any
T\in\mathcal{T}
such that
T\subseteq S_{\ell}
, define
\widetilde{S}\coloneqq S_{\ell}\cup M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k}

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}_{L}(T,\ell,\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\widetilde{G}[(U\setminus\widetilde{S})\uplus(V\cap N_{\widetilde{G}}(T))]

\displaystyle\text{ and }\widetilde{H}_{R}(T,\ell,\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\widetilde{G}[(V\setminus\widetilde{S})\uplus(U\cap N_{\widetilde{G}}{T})]

With high probability, the following holds after step 2 of
.
For sufficiently small
\varepsilon^{\text{(node)}}
, every
\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}
, and every
\ell\in[d]
, there is some
T\in\mathcal{T},T\subseteq S_{\ell}
such that
\widetilde{H}_{L}(T,\ell,\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k)
and
\widetilde{H}_{R}(T,\ell,\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k)
have
3
-fold balancedness at most

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1-p}{p}}}\right)^{3}\frac{k}{4}+(2t\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}+4r\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}})k\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{3}.

Furthermore, for all
v\in T
, we have
\widetilde{\deg}(v)\leqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k
.

Proof.

First of all, we can use
Lemma

4.11
to derive that for all
\ell\in[d]
, a
1-o(1)
fraction of
t
-tuples
T\in\binom{S_{\ell}}{t}
are such that

\displaystyle H_{L}(T,\ell,\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k)

\displaystyle\coloneqq G[(U\setminus\widetilde{S})\uplus(V\cap N_{G}(T))]

\displaystyle\text{ and }H_{R}(T,\ell,\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k)

\displaystyle\coloneqq G[(V\setminus\widetilde{S})\uplus(U\cap N_{G}(T))]

have
3
-fold balancedness
\Delta\coloneqq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((1-p)/p}}\right)^{3}\frac{k}{4}
(like in the proof of
Lemma

7.2
). Notice the subtle difference that we have replaced
\widetilde{G}
by
G
in
N_{G}({T})
above.

The rest of the proof is dedicated to addressing this difference. To this end, note that for sufficiently small
\varepsilon^{\text{(node)}}
, the fraction of
t
-tuples
T
in
S_{\ell}
such that for all
v\in T
, we have
v\notin M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}}
while at the same time
H_{L}(T,\ell,\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k),H_{R}(T,\ell,\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k)
have the desired
r
-fold balancedness is still at least
\frac{1}{2}
. Thus, by the same analysis as in
Lemma

7.2
, with high probability, we find a such tuple in
\mathcal{T}
for each
\ell\in[d]
after step 2.

Accordingly, with high probability after step 2,
\mathcal{T}
contains a tuple
T\subseteq S_{\ell}
for all
\ell\in[d]
such that
\widetilde{\deg}(v)\leqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k
for all
v\in T
, while
H_{L}(T,\ell,\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k),H_{R}(T,\ell,\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k)
have the same balancedness guarantees as in
Lemma

7.2
. To lift this result to
\widetilde{H}_{L}(T,\ell,\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k),\widetilde{H}_{R}(T,\ell,\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k)
, we first consider the intermediate graphs

\displaystyle H^{\prime}_{L}(T,\ell,\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k)

\displaystyle\coloneqq G[(U\setminus\widetilde{S})\uplus(V\cap N_{\widetilde{G}}(T))]

\displaystyle\text{ and }H^{\prime}_{R}(T,\ell,\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\widetilde{G}[(V\setminus\widetilde{S})\uplus(U\cap N_{G}{T})].

Here, by the fact that
\widetilde{\deg}(v)\leqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k
for
v\in T
, we get that the right hand side of
H^{\prime}_{L}(T,\ell,\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k)
differs by at most
t\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k
vertices compared to
H_{L}(T,\ell)
. Therefore, the
3
-fold balancedness of
H^{\prime}_{L}(T,\ell,\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k)
is at most

\Delta+2t\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{3}.

Now, the desired balancedness guarantees for
\widetilde{H}_{L}(T,\ell,\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k)
directly follow after applying
Lemma

8.12
to
H^{\prime}_{L}(T,\ell,\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k)
. The same argument applies to
\widetilde{H}_{R}(T,\ell,\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k)
.
∎

Now, we can use our SoS certificates from
Section

5
to conclude that any pseudo-distribution can not put a lot of “mass” on the left hand sided vertices of
H_{L}(T,\ell)
and
H_{R}(T,\ell)
, respectively. This is captured in the following
analogue of
Lemma

7.3
.

Lemma 8.14
.

Assume that
T
is a
t
-tuple
T\subseteq S_{\ell}
such that
\widetilde{H}_{L}(T,\ell,\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k)
, and
\widetilde{H}_{R}(T,\ell,\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k)
(cf.
Lemma

8.13
) meet the balancedness criteria from
Lemma

8.13
. Then for sufficiently small
\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}
,

\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\widetilde{G},U,V,\widetilde{k},\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k)\cup\{w_{T}=1\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{w}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\bigg\{\sum_{i\in U\setminus\widetilde{S}}w_{i},\sum_{i\in V\setminus\widetilde{S}}w_{i}\leqslant O(\sqrt{n})\bigg\}

and the bit complexity of the proof is
n^{O(1)}
.

Proof.

Similarly as in the proof of
Lemma

7.3
, we get from
Lemma

8.13
that

\widetilde{H}_{L}(T,\ell,\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k),\widetilde{H}_{R}(T,\ell,\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k)
are subgraphs of
H(T)
with
r
-fold balancedness at most
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((1-p)/p}}\right)^{3}\frac{k}{3}
if choosing
\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}
small enough (this works since we assume
p
to be bounded away from
1
).
Thus, invoking
Lemma

8.3
with
r=3
to
\widetilde{H}_{L}(T,\ell,\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k)
, we get that

\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\widetilde{G},U,V,\widetilde{k},\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k)\cup\{w_{T}=1\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x,y}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\Bigg\{\bigg(\sum_{i\in U\setminus\widetilde{S}}w_{i}\bigg)^{3}\leqslant O\bigg(\frac{n^{2}}{k}\bigg)\Bigg\},

Again, combining this with the fact that
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\widetilde{G},U,V,\widetilde{k},\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k)\cup\{w_{T}=1\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x,y}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\{\sum_{i\in U\setminus\widetilde{S}}x_{i}\leqslant 2k\}
, we get that

\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\widetilde{G},U,V,\widetilde{k},\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k)\cup\{w_{T}=1\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x,y}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\bigg(\sum_{i\in U\setminus\widetilde{S}}w_{i}\bigg)^{4}\leqslant O(n^{2})}}\right\}.

Applying the cancellation
\{f^{2}\leqslant C\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.68402pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.68402pt\raisebox{-6.82222pt}{\makebox[8.68402pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f}$}}}\hskip-8.68402pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.68402pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}$}}}\{f\leqslant\sqrt{C}\}
from
Lemma

5.5
twice, we arrive at the desired conclusion. An analogous argument for
\widetilde{H}_{R}(T,\ell,\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k)
also yields the second part of the statement.
∎

Finally, we can combine all of the above to show that we can approximately recover all
S_{\ell}
.

Lemma 8.15
.

Assume
T
is a
t
-tuple
T\subseteq S_{\ell}
such that
\widetilde{H}_{L}(T,\ell,\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k)
, and
\widetilde{H}_{R}(T,\ell,\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k)
meet the balancedness criteria from
Lemma

7.2
. Then, after step 5 and for all sufficiently small
\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}},\varepsilon^{\text{(node)}}
and all
\rho
, we have
|S_{T}\triangle S_{\ell}|\leqslant(5\varepsilon^{\text{(node)}}/\rho)k
. Moreover,
S_{T}\subseteq S_{\ell}\cup M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}}
.

Remark 8.16
.

Notice that the above implies that for
\varepsilon^{\text{(node)}}=0
, there is some
\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}>0
such that
|S_{T}\triangle S_{\ell}|=0
. This yields
exact recovery
, even under the adversary.

Proof.

Like in the proof of
Lemma

7.4
, we observe that for every
S_{\ell}
after step 1 of the algorithm, we have
\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}k\leqslant|S_{\ell}\cap U|,|S_{\ell}\cap V|\leqslant\frac{1+\varepsilon}{2}k
for every fixed
\varepsilon>0
, with high probability.

Hence, there is at least one pseudo-distribution
\mu
in axioms
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\widetilde{G},U,V,\widetilde{k},\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k)\cup\{w_{T}=1\}
, set for example
w_{i}=1
if and only if
i\in S_{\ell}\setminus M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k}
, and
z_{u,v}=1
if and only if
x_{u}=1,y_{v}=1
, and
\{u,v\}\notin E(\widetilde{G}[U\uplus V])
. This pseudo-distribution achieves objective
\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[|w|}}\right]=|S_{\ell}\setminus M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}}|
.

Using
Lemma

8.14
and recalling that
\widetilde{S}=S_{\ell}\cup M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k}
, we get that
\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus\widetilde{S}}\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}[w_{i}]\leqslant O(\sqrt{n})
if
\mu
is a degree-
O(t)
pseudo-distribution in axioms
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\widetilde{G},U,V,\widetilde{k},\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k)\cup\{w_{T}=1\}
. Accordingly,

\frac{1}{|S_{\ell}\cup M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}}|}\sum_{i\in S_{\ell}\cup M_{\geqslant\lambda}}\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}[w_{i}]\geqslant\frac{|S_{\ell}\setminus M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}}|-O(\sqrt{n})}{|S_{\ell}\cup M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}}|}\geqslant\frac{|S_{\ell}|-\varepsilon^{\text{(node)}}k-O(\sqrt{n})}{|S_{\ell}|+\varepsilon^{\text{(node)}}k}

Choosing
\varepsilon^{\text{(node)}}
small enough, the right hand side is at least
1-4\varepsilon^{\text{(node)}}
. Thus, by Markov’s inequality there are at most
4\varepsilon^{\text{(node)}}k/\rho
vertices
v
in
S_{\ell}\cup M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}}
such that
\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}[w_{i}]\leqslant 1-\rho
. Hence, for sufficiently small
\varepsilon^{\text{(node)}}
the overlap
|S_{T}\cap(S_{\ell}\setminus M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}})|=\Omega(k)
, so by
Lemma

8.11
, we have
S_{T}\subseteq S_{\ell}\cup M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}}
.
This implies further that the set
S_{T}
satisfies
|S_{\ell}\triangle S_{T}|\leqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(node)}}k+(4\varepsilon^{\text{(node)}}/\rho)k
, as desired. ∎

8.4.2
Step 2: No other cliques are found

We proceed by showing that the the algorithm never finds a set
S_{T}
that does not have large overlap with some ground-truth clique.

Lemma 8.17
.

Let
T\in\binom{[n]}{t}
be any
t
-tuple in
G\sim{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
. Then, after step 5 of
, the set
S_{T}
is either discarded or such that
|S_{T}\triangle S_{\ell}|\leqslant(5\varepsilon^{\text{(node)}}/\rho)k
for some
\ell\in[d]
.

The challenge for the above is that–even for
k\gg n^{\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon}
–the single label clique theorem is not applicable anymore due to the adversary we want to allow. We further want our algorithm to work all the way down to
k\gg\sqrt{n\log(n)}
, so a different approach is needed.
Luckily, our the pseudo-concentration statement from
Lemma

8.7
comes in handy and yields a short proof of
Lemma

8.17

Proof of
Lemma

8.17
.

We check if the
S_{T}
found after step 5 has size at least
(1-4\varepsilon^{\text{(node)}}/\rho)k
. If so,
Lemma

8.7
tells us that there is some
\ell\in\mathcal{B}_{T}
such that
S_{T}\subseteq S_{\ell}\cap M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}}
. Hence
|S_{T}\triangle S_{\ell}|\leqslant(4\varepsilon^{\text{(node)}}/\rho)k+\varepsilon^{\text{(node)}}k+o(k)\leqslant(5\varepsilon^{\text{(node)}}/\rho)k
, as desired.
∎

Finally, we analyze the pruning in step 6.

Lemma 8.18
.

There is a constant
C
such that with high probability, the following holds after step 6 of
. We have
|\mathcal{L}|=d
and for every
\ell\in\ell
, there is exactly one
S\in\mathcal{L}
such that
|S\triangle S_{\ell}|\leqslant(5\varepsilon^{\text{(node)}}/\rho)k
. Moreover, if
|S\triangle S_{\ell}|\leqslant(5\varepsilon^{\text{(node)}}/\rho)\varepsilon^{\text{(node)}}k
, then further
S\subseteq S_{\ell}\cup M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}}
.

Proof.

We get from
Lemma

8.15
and
Lemma

8.13
that with high probability, for every
\ell\in[d]
, there is some
S\in\mathcal{L}
such that
|S\triangle S_{\ell}|\leqslant(5\varepsilon^{\text{(node)}}/\rho)k
and
S\subseteq S_{\ell}\cup M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}}
. Moreover, by
Lemma

8.17
, the same guarantees hold for every
S\in\mathcal{L}
for some
\ell\in[d]
.

It therefore only remains to show that
|\mathcal{L}|=d
. To this end, we note that with high probability over the draw of
G
, we have that for all
\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in[d]
that
|S_{\ell}\cap S_{\ell^{\prime}}|\leqslant(1+o(1))\delta k
. This implies that for sufficiently small
\rho
, exactly one
S\in\mathcal{L}
with
|S\triangle S_{\ell}|\leqslant(1-5\varepsilon^{\text{(node)}}/\rho)k
is kept. ∎

8.5
Deferred proofs

We re-visit the SoS certificates whose prove was deferred above.

8.5.1
Proof of
Lemma

8.3

For establishing
Lemma

8.3
we need the following proposition telling us that imposing the constraint
x_{T}=1
implies that only a small number of vertices is outside of
N_{G}({T})
as a function of
\gamma
.

Lemma 8.19
.

Given a biparite graph
H=(A\uplus B,E)
and any
T\in A
with
|T|=t
,

\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(G,A,B,k,\gamma)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{w}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\Bigg\{w_{T}\sum_{v\in B\setminus N_{G}({T})}w_{v}\leqslant\gamma t\ w_{T}\Bigg\}.

Proof.

Observe that by the axiom
w_{u}w_{v}(1-z_{u,v})=0
,

\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(G,A,B,k,\gamma)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{w,z}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{0=w_{T}\sum_{v\in B\setminus N_{G}({T})}w_{v}\prod_{u\in T}(1-z_{u,v})}}\right\}.

However, using the bound from
5.14
, we get

\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(G,A,B,k,\gamma)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{w,z}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\prod_{u\in T}(1-z_{u,v})\geqslant 1-\sum_{u\in T}z_{u,v}}}\right\}.

Thus,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(G,A,B,k,\gamma)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{w,z}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} 0&=w_{T}\sum_{v\in B\setminus N_{G}({T})}w_{v}\prod_{u\in T}(1-z_{u,v})\\
&\hskip 56.9055pt\geqslant w_{T}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{v\in B\setminus N_{G}({T})}w_{v}-\sum_{v\in B\setminus N_{G}({T})}w_{v}\sum_{u\in T}z_{u,v}}}\right)\\
&\hskip 56.9055pt\geqslant w_{T}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{v\in B\setminus N_{G}({T})}w_{v}-\sum_{u\in T}\sum_{v\in B\setminus N_{G}({T})}z_{u,v}}}\right)\end{aligned}}}\right\}.

Due to the constraint
\sum_{v\in B}z_{u,v}\leqslant\gamma
, we get

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(G,A,B,k,\gamma)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x,y}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{0\geqslant w_{T}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{v\in B\setminus N_{G}({T})}w_{v}-\gamma t}}\right)}}\right\}.

Re-arranging then finishes the proof.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

8.3
.

We denote by
|y_{N_{G}({T})}|=\sum_{y\in B\cap N_{G}({T})}y_{v}
and
invoke
Lemma

5.12
for on
H[A_{\text{(sub)}}\uplus B]
to get

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(G,A,B,k,\gamma)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{16.34492pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-16.34492pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[16.34492pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{w,z}$}}}\hskip-16.34492pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[16.34492pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{8r}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1-p}{p}}}\right)^{r}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{S\subseteq A_{\text{(sub)}},|S|=r}w_{S}}}\right)^{2}(|y_{N_{G}({T})}|-2\gamma r)\\
&\hskip 56.9055pt\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n+rn^{2}}}\right)\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{r}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{S\subseteq A_{\text{(sub)}},|S|=r}w_{S}}}\right)+\Delta\hskip 1.42271pt\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{S\subseteq A^{\prime},|S|=r}w_{S}}}\right)^{2}\end{aligned}}}\right\}.

Now, we rely on the fact that by
Lemma

8.19
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(G,A,B,k,\gamma)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{w,z}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{w_{T}\sum_{v\in B\setminus N_{G}({T})}w_{v}\leqslant\gamma tw_{T}}}\right\}.
and thus, (recalling that
\gamma\leqslant\varepsilon^{\prime}k
),

\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(G,A,B,k,\gamma)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{w,z}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{w_{T}|y_{N_{G}({T})}|\geqslant(k-\gamma t)w_{T}\geqslant(1-\varepsilon^{\prime}t)k\ w_{T}\geqslant(2\gamma r+11k/12)\ w_{T}}}\right\}

for sufficiently small
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
.
Using this and our bound on
\Delta
(
\Delta\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((1-p)/p}}\right)^{r}10k/12
), we get that

\mathcal{B}(H,k)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{w,z}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1-p}{p}}}\right)^{r}w_{T}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{S\subseteq A_{\text{(sub)}},|S|=r}w_{S}}}\right)^{2}\frac{k}{12}\leqslant(r+1)n^{2}\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{r}w_{T}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{S\subseteq A_{\text{(sub)}},|S|=r}w_{S}}}\right)}}\right\}.

almost like in the proof of
Lemma

7.1
. The rest of the proof then follows exactly the proof of
Lemma

7.1
(notice that applying the cancellation inequalities remains valid since
w_{T}=w_{T}^{2}
).
∎

8.5.2
Proof of
Lemma

8.4

Proof of
Lemma

8.4
.

We define the auxiliary graph

F_{L}(T)\coloneqq G[(U\setminus\widetilde{V}(\mathcal{B}_{T})\uplus(V\cap N_{G}(T)s))].

Then, like in the proof of
Lemma

6.11
and
Lemma

8.17
, we get from
Lemma

4.10
with
L(T)=\mathcal{B}_{T}
and
S(T)=V(\mathcal{B}_{T})
that the graph
F_{L}(T)
has
3
-fold balancedness

\frac{16tp^{-2}}{\alpha}\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{3}\cdot p^{t}k+o(k),

for all
T
. By our choice of
t
and the calculation in
Lemma

7.2
, this is at most

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((1-p)/p}}\right)^{3}\frac{k}{4}
. Switching from
F_{L}(T)
to

\widetilde{F_{L}}(T)\coloneqq\widetilde{G}[(U\setminus\widetilde{V}(\mathcal{B}_{T})\uplus(V\cap N_{\widetilde{G}}(T)))].

and using the same argument as in
Lemma

8.13
(here we rely on the
\lambda
-goodness of
T
), we obtain a
3
-fold balancedness in
\widetilde{F_{L}}(T)
of at most

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1-p}{p}}}\right)^{3}\frac{k}{4}+(2t\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}+4r\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}})k\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{3}.

Now, we can use the same argument as in
Lemma

8.14
to get to our conclusion, provided that
\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}
is small enough.
∎

8.5.3
Proof of
Lemma

8.6

Proof of
Lemma

8.6
.

For this lemma, we rely on balancedness properties among the cliques in
V(\mathcal{B}_{T})
like in
Section

6.2.2
and therefore re-introduce some notation from this section.

First, recall that
\mathcal{R}(T)
is the set of vertices in
G
that have chosen at least two of the labels in
\mathcal{B}_{T}
. For any
\ell\in\mathcal{B}_{T}
, recall further that
S^{\text{(in)}}_{\ell}(T)=S_{\ell}\setminus\mathcal{R}(T)
and
S^{\text{(out)}}_{\ell}(T)=\bigcup_{\ell^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{T}\setminus\{\ell\}}S_{\ell^{\prime}}\setminus\mathcal{R}(T)
.
Now, balancedness properties hold in the bipartite graphs
H_{\ell}\coloneqq G[S^{\text{(in)}}_{\ell}(T)\uplus S^{\text{(out)}}_{\ell}(T)]
for all
\ell\in\mathcal{B}_{T}
. Specifically, it follows from
Lemma

6.14
that all the
H_{\ell}
have
3
-fold balancedness
o(k)
.

We lift this balancedness guarantee to
\widetilde{G}
by considering the following bipartite graph for every
\ell\in\mathcal{B}_{T}

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}_{\ell}

\displaystyle\coloneqq G[\widetilde{S}^{\text{(in)}}_{\ell}(T)\uplus\widetilde{S}^{\text{(out)}}_{\ell}(T)]

\displaystyle\text{ with }\widetilde{S}^{\text{(in)}}_{\ell}(T)

\displaystyle\coloneqq S^{\text{(in)}}_{\ell}(T)\setminus M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}}\text{ and }\widetilde{S}^{\text{(out)}}_{\ell}(T)\coloneqq S^{\text{(out)}}_{\ell}(T)\setminus M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}}.

Noting that
\widetilde{\deg}(v)\leqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k
for all
v\in V(\widetilde{H}_{\ell})
, and applying
Lemma

8.12
, we get that
\widetilde{H}_{\ell}
has
3
-fold balancedness at most

\displaystyle\Delta\coloneqq o(k)+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2t\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}+12\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}}}\right)k\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{3}.

Then, we define
|y|\coloneqq\sum_{v\in\widetilde{S}^{\text{(out)}}_{\ell}(T)}w_{v}
and apply the certificate from
Lemma

5.12
to obtain (using
r=3
) that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\widetilde{G},U,V,\widetilde{k},\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{w}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{S\subseteq\widetilde{S}^{\text{(in)}}_{\ell}(T),|S|=3}w_{S}}}\right)^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1-p}{p}}}\right)^{3}|y|-\Delta-12\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k}}\right)\\
&\hskip 85.35826pt\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n+3n^{2}}}\right)\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1-p}{p},\frac{p}{1-p}}}\right\}^{3}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{S\subseteq\widetilde{S}^{\text{(in)}}_{\ell}(T),|S|=3}w_{S}}}\right)\end{aligned}}}\right\}.

To get a lower bound on the left hand side, we use
|w|\geqslant k
and the certificate from
Lemma

8.4
to obtain

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\widetilde{G},U,V,\widetilde{k},\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{w}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} w_{T}w_{i}|y|&\geqslant w_{T}w_{i}\bigg(2\widetilde{k}-\sum_{v\in S_{\ell}}w_{v}-\sum_{v\in[n]\setminus\widetilde{V}(\mathcal{B}_{T})}w_{v}-\sum_{v\in\mathcal{R}(T)}w_{v}-\sum_{v\in M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}}}w_{v}\bigg)\\
&\geqslant w_{T}w_{i}\bigg(2\widetilde{k}-\sum_{v\in S_{\ell}}w_{v}-O(\sqrt{n})-o(k)-\varepsilon^{\text{(node)}}k\bigg)\end{aligned}}}\right\}.

For the remaining sum above, we use the fact that by
Lemma

4.15
, we get that the maximum degree of any
i\in[n]\setminus(S_{\ell}\cup M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}})
into
S_{\ell}\setminus M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}}
is at most
(1+\varepsilon)pk
w.h.p. Since
p
is bounded away from
1
, we get that for any sufficiently small
\varepsilon^{\text{(node)}}
, there is a constant
c>0
, such that
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\widetilde{G},U,V,\widetilde{k},\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{w}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{w_{T}w_{i}|y|\geqslant w_{T}w_{i}ck}}\right\}.
Hence, due to our bound on
\Delta
, we get that for sufficiently small
\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}
,

\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\widetilde{G},U,V,\widetilde{k},\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{w}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{w_{T}w_{i}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1-p}{p}}}\right)^{3}|y|-\Delta-12\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k}}\right)\geqslant c^{\prime}k\ w_{T}w_{i}}}\right\},

for some constant
c^{\prime}>0
. Hence,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\widetilde{G},U,V,\widetilde{k},\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{w}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} &w_{T}w_{i}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{S\subseteq\widetilde{S}^{\text{(in)}}_{\ell}(T),|S|=3}w_{S}}}\right)^{2}\leqslant O\bigg(\frac{n^{2}}{k}\bigg)\ w_{T}w_{i}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{S\subseteq\widetilde{S}^{\text{(in)}}_{\ell}(T),|S|=3}w_{S}}}\right)\end{aligned}}}\right\}.

Using the cancellation
\{f^{2}\leqslant C\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{12.14894pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-12.14894pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[12.14894pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-12.14894pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[12.14894pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2d}$}}}$}}}\{f\leqslant\sqrt{C}\}
, we get

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\widetilde{G},U,V,\widetilde{k},\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{w}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} &w_{T}w_{i}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{S\subseteq\widetilde{S}^{\text{(in)}}_{\ell}(T),|S|=3}w_{S}}}\right)^{2}\leqslant O\bigg(\frac{n^{4}}{k^{2}}\bigg)\ w_{T}w_{i}\end{aligned}}}\right\}.

Using
Lemma

5.7
and multiplying by
\{(\sum_{v\in[n]}w_{v})^{2}\leqslant 8k^{2}\}
, we get
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\widetilde{G},U,V,\widetilde{k},\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{w}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\{w_{T}w_{i}(\sum_{v\in\widetilde{S}^{\text{(in)}}_{\ell}(T)}w_{v})^{8}\leqslant O(n^{4})w_{T}w_{i}\}.
Applying the cancellation the cancellation
\{f^{2}\leqslant C\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{12.14894pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-12.14894pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[12.14894pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\hskip-12.14894pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[12.14894pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2d}$}}}$}}}\{f\leqslant\sqrt{C}\}
three times, we get

\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\widetilde{G},U,V,\widetilde{k},\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{w}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{w_{T}w_{i}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{v\in\widetilde{S}^{\text{(in)}}_{\ell}(T)}w_{v}}}\right)\leqslant O(\sqrt{n})w_{T}w_{i}}}\right\}

and the proof is almost finished. It only remains to note that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\widetilde{G},U,V,\widetilde{k},\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}k)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{w}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{3.71112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}$}}}\bigg\{\sum_{v\in S_{\ell}\setminus M_{\geqslant\varepsilon^{\text{(deg)}}}}w_{v}\leqslant\sum_{v\in\widetilde{S}^{\text{(in)}}_{\ell}(T)}w_{v}+\sum_{v\in\mathcal{R}(T)}w_{v}\leqslant\sum_{v\in\widetilde{S}^{\text{(in)}}_{\ell}(T)}w_{v}+o(k)\bigg\}

since
\mathcal{R}(T)=o(k)
w.h.p. by
Lemma

6.15
. With this, our statement follows.
∎

9
Open questions and future work

We leave two main open problems that we consider to be exciting directions for future work.

Algorithms for RIGs with “two-sided” noise

A natural extension of our (noisy) model of RIGs would be to consider a model where we replace the ground-truth cliques with dense sub-graphs.

Definition 9.1

(Random Intersection graphs with two-sided noise)
.

Define a random graph
\textsc{RIG}_{2}(n,d,p,q\!\!\uparrow,q\!\!\downarrow)
with
q\!\!\downarrow<q\!\!\uparrow
on vertex set
[n]
as follows. Every vertex
v
draws a set
M_{v}\subseteq[d]
by including each
i\in[d]
independently with probability
\delta=\delta(d,p,q\!\!\uparrow,q\!\!\downarrow)
. Then, for every pair
u,v\in V
, we include an edge with probability

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(u\sim v}}\right)=\begin{cases}q\!\!\uparrow&\text{ if }M_{v}\cap M_{u}\neq\emptyset\\
q\!\!\downarrow&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

The probability
\delta=\delta(d,p,q\!\!\uparrow,q\!\!\downarrow)
is chosen such that
\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(u\sim v}}\right)=p
for all
u,v\in[n],u\neq v
.

Here, edges within some ground truth community form with probability
q\!\!\uparrow
while edges outside form with probability
q\!\!\downarrow<q\!\!\uparrow
. Can our algorithms be extended to this case? The techniques presented in
Section

8
partially cover the case where up to a constant fraction of edges (that are adversarially chosen) are missing in the ground-truth
S_{\ell}
, but they do not directly extend to the two-sided case. Here, a significantly larger number of missing edges has to be handled, which, however, is
not
adversarially chosen but random. We believe our techniques to be a good starting point for achieving recovery in
\textsc{RIG}_{2}(n,d,p,q\!\!\uparrow,q\!\!\downarrow)
, but some further ideas will be required. Moreover, it would be interesting to see if the algorithms on RIGs with two-sided noise can still be made robust to a
monotone
adversary that is now not only allowed to delete edges outside of the ground-truth communities, but also to
add back
edges within the communities. We believe this to be the case, however it is known that the capabilities of a such adversary
do
shift the information-theoretic threshold for recoverability in the stochastic block model
[
58
]
, so this question stil deserves a formal investigation.

Hardness of recovery in dense RIGs without noise

Moreover, it would be very interesting to better understand the
computational-statistical trade-offs
for recovering communities in RIGs. We show in
Appendix

B
that recovery is hard in the
noisy model
if
k\ll\sqrt{n}
like it is the case for classical planted clique. Together with the information-theoretic feasibility proved in
Section

6.1
, this shows that there is a computational-statistical gap whenever
n^{\varepsilon}\ll k\ll\sqrt{n}
and
p,q
are a sufficiently small constants. However, our reduction from planted clique in
Theorem

B.3
crucially relies on the fact that we have independent edges outside of the communities in our model
{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
.

It would be very interesting to find out if this hardness carries over to the case where
p
is a constant and
q=0
, i.e., the
dense, noiseless
case. We strongly believe this to be the case, however, finding evidence for a such computational statistical gap seems to be quite challenging. While there are multiple ways of giving evidence for such gaps exist (e.g. low-degree polynomials, SoS lower bounds, the overlap gap property, statistical query algorithms, reductions from other statistical problems), none of the techniques known to us seems to easily handle dense, noiseless RIGs. Finding such techniques would likely be interesting in its own right and might further shed light on other computational aspects of other average-case problems.

Appendix A
Lower bound on the spectral gap of RIGs

In this section we prove the lower-bound on the spectral norm of the centered adjacency matrix of a random intersection graph.
For this, we will use the Berry–Esseen theorem (see e.g.
[
44
]
).

Theorem A.1

(Berry–Esseen)
.

Let, for
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
X_{1},X_{2}\dots,X_{n}
be i.i.d. random variables with
\mathbb{E}[X_{1}]=0
,
\mathbb{E}[X_{1}^{2}]=\sigma^{2}\in(0,\infty)
and
\mathbb{E}[X_{1}^{3}]<\infty
. Let
X=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n\sigma^{2}}}(X_{1}+\dots+X_{n})
and let
\Phi
be the distribution function of the standard normal distribution. Then,

\sup\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S_{n}\leqslant x}}\right)-\Phi(x)}}\right|\leqslant\frac{0.8\mathbb{E}[X_{1}^{3}]}{\sigma^{3}\sqrt{n}}.

See
2.1

Proof.

To prove the lemma we show that
\mathbf{A}
has sufficiently many rows that have sufficiently many “
+1
” entries. In order to do that, we first calculate the probability that, for each
v
, we have sufficiently many elements in
M_{v}
. For
i\in[d]
define
Y_{i}=\mathds{1}(i\in M_{v})-\delta
, and observe that
\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}^{2}]=\delta(1-\delta)
and
\mathbb{E}[|Y_{i}|^{3}]=\delta(1-\delta)(\delta^{2}+(1-\delta)^{2})
. Now define

X=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d\delta(1-\delta)}}\sum_{i\in[d]}Y_{i}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d\delta(1-\delta)}}(|M_{v}|-\delta d).

We can apply
Theorem

A.1
on
X
and obtain that,

\sup\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X\leqslant x}}\right)-\Phi(x)}}\right|\leqslant\frac{0.8\delta(1-\delta)(\delta^{2}+(1-\delta)^{2})}{(\delta(1-\delta))^{3/2}\sqrt{d}}=\frac{0.8(\delta^{2}+(1-\delta)^{2})}{\sqrt{d\delta(1-\delta)}}\in o(1),

(A.1)

since
\delta\in O(d^{-1/2})
for our choice of parameters and
Lemma

4.1
.

We now have that,

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|M_{v}|\geqslant d\delta+\sqrt{d\delta}}}\right)=\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X\geqslant\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\delta}}}}\right)\geqslant 1-\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\delta}}}}\right)-o_{d}(1),

where the last inequality follows from (
A.1
). Observe that
\Phi(\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\delta}})
can be bounded above by a constant, yielding that, for all
v\in V
,
|M_{v}|\geqslant d\delta+\sqrt{d\delta}
with constant probability.

Let
L
be the set of vertices
v
such that
|M_{v}|\geqslant d\delta+\sqrt{d\delta}
. Since each vertex has at least constant probability of being in
L
, using Chernoff’s bound on the set of vertices shows that w.h.p. over the draw of
G\sim RIG(n,d,1/2,0)
,
|L|=\Theta(n)
.

Next, we lower bound the number of edges for any
u\in L
. To this end, we fix a vertex
u\in L
and consider a vertex
v\in[n]\setminus\{u\}
. Then revealing the labels of
v
we obtain

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(u\sim v}}\right)\geqslant 1-(1-\delta)^{d\delta+\sqrt{d\delta}}\geqslant 1-e^{-(d\delta^{2}+\delta\sqrt{d\delta})}.

Using that
\exp(\delta\sqrt{d\delta})=1+\Theta(\delta\sqrt{d\delta})
in conjunction with
\delta=\sqrt{1-2^{1/d}}
(for our choices
p=1/2
and
q=0
) yields that for any
v\in[n]\setminus\{u\}
,

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(u\sim v}}\right)=1/2+\Omega(\delta\sqrt{d\delta}).

Using a standard Chernoff bound in combination of a union bound it now follows that w.h.p. for all
u\in L
it holds that
u
has
(1/2+\Omega(\delta\sqrt{d\delta}))n
edges.

Thus, we have that w.h.p. the centered adjacency matrix
\mathbf{A}
has at least
|L|=\Theta(n)
rows with at least
(1/2+\Theta(\delta\sqrt{d\delta}))n
many “
+1
” entries.

Now let
\mathbf{x}
be the all
n^{-1/2}
vector and let
\mathbf{y}
be the vector assigning

y_{i}=\begin{cases}|L|^{-1/2}&\textrm{ if }i\in L\\
0&\textrm{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Clearly
\|\mathbf{x}\|=\|\mathbf{y}\|=1
. For
i,j\in[n]
, let
A_{i,j}
be the entries of
\mathbf{A}
and let
r_{i}=\sum_{j\in[n]}A_{i,j}
. Hence, for
i\in L
, we have
r_{i}=\Theta(n\delta\sqrt{d\delta})
.

Then,

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{A}\|\geqslant\mathbf{x}^{T}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{y}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n|L|}}\sum_{i\in L}\sum_{j\in[n]}A_{i,j}\geqslant\frac{1}{\sqrt{n|L|}}\sum_{i\in L}r_{i}\geqslant\frac{|L|\Theta(n\delta\sqrt{d\delta})}{\sqrt{n|L|}}=\Theta(nd^{-1/4}),

which yields the statement.
∎

Remark A.2
.

Note that the above proof equally applies to a model of planting equal-sized cliques: instead of each vertex
v
drawing elements in
M_{v}
from
[d]
independently and with probability
\delta
, we can also consider the model where each element in
[d]
is assigned to
k
vertices of
G
uniformly at random. Then, the application of the Berry–Esseen theorem holds also in this setting, and the rest of the proof follows with minor modifications.

Appendix B
Hardness for
k\ll\sqrt{n}

In this section, we provide a short proof that exact recovery in
{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
for
p,q=\Omega(1)
is hard whenever
k\ll\sqrt{n}
, assuming the planted clique hypothesis. Our proof uses a simple average-case reduction, similar to the proof of Theorem 2.7 in
[
45
]

Definition B.1

(Planted Clique)
.

For
p=\Theta(1)
, let
G^{\prime}\sim G(n,p)
, choose any subset of vertices
K\subseteq V(G^{\prime})
of size
|K|=k
and let
G=(V(G^{\prime}),E(G^{\prime})\cup\binom{K}{2})
. Then the
planted clique
problem is to recover
K
from
G
.

Conjecture B.2

(Planted Clique Hardness)
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
be any constant. Then for
k\leqslant n^{1/2-\varepsilon}
the planted clique problem (
Definition

B.1
) cannot be solved in polynomial time.

Theorem B.3

(Lower bound)
.

Assume planted clique hardness
B.2
and let
k=\delta n=n^{1/2-\Omega(1)}
,
q=\Theta(1)
. Then there is no polynomial time algorithm that given a graph
G\sim{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
, outputs a set of
d
cliques that agrees with
\{S_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in[d]}
w.h.p.

Proof.

Planting the clique.
Consider a graph
G^{\prime}\sim G(n,p){q}
and initialize
K_{1}=\emptyset
. Then for each vertex
v\in V(G^{\prime})
flip a coin such that with probability
\delta
,
v
is included in
K_{1}
. Notice that by assumption
\mathbb{E}{|K_{1}|}=\delta n\leqslant n^{1/2-\Omega(1)}
and since
|K_{1}|
is a binomial random variable it follows by a Chernoff bound that
|K_{1}|\leqslant n^{1/2-\Omega(1)}
with probability
1-o(1)
. Thus, "planting"
K_{1}
as a clique into
G^{\prime}
we obtain
G_{1}=(V(G^{\prime}),E(G^{\prime})\cup\binom{K_{1}}{2})
and thus, assuming
B.2
, there is no polynomial time algorithm to recover
K_{1}
from
G_{1}
w.h.p. since
q
is non-vanishing.

Algorithm.
Next, assume there is a polynomial time algorithm
Alg
that, given a graph
G\sim{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
, outputs a set of
d
cliques that agrees with
\{S_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in[d]}
w.h.p. We show that this would contradict the planted clique conjecture
B.2
as we could recover
K_{1}
from
G_{1}
w.h.p. by which our desired statement is proven.

Reduction.
To this end, recalling that
G_{1}
is the graph
G^{\prime}\sim G(n,p){q}
with the planted clique
K_{1}
, we add edges to
G_{1}
such that the resulting graph follows the distribution of
G\sim{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
. We achieve this as follows: For all
\ell\in[2\ldots d]
, initialize
K_{\ell}=\emptyset
and for rounds
\ell=1,\ldots,d-1
, we flip in each round
\ell
a coin for each vertex
v\in V(G^{\prime})
. With probability
\delta
include
v
in
K_{\ell+1}
and at the end of each round
\ell
, "plant" clique
K_{\ell+1}
into
G_{\ell}
, i.e.,
G_{\ell+1}:=(V(G_{\ell}),E(G_{\ell})\cup\binom{K_{\ell+1}}{2})
. The resulting graph
G_{d}
after
d-1
rounds is
G_{1}
with additional
d-1
cliques. Notice that the this procedure requires
O(d\cdot n)
time and thus, if
Alg
solves planted clique on
G_{d}
in polynomial time, we have a polynomial time algorithm for planted clique on
G_{1}
.

Solving planted clique via
Alg
.
Note that after the reduction outlined in the paragraph above, we obtain a graph
G_{d}\sim{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
where each clique
K_{\ell}
represents the clique with label
\ell\in d
and thus,
G_{d}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}G\sim{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
. Moreover, we observe that the initial planted clique
K_{1}
in
G_{1}
is also included in the set
\{S_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in[d]}
of
G_{d}
. Thus, if
Alg
outputs a set of
d
cliques that agrees with
\{S_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in[d]}
w.h.p., it also outputs
K_{1}
w.h.p. We then exploit this as follows: Fix any
\ell\in d
and check if clique
K_{\ell}
is included in
G_{1}
. We can do this in time
|E(K_{\ell})|=O(n^{2})
by checking if all edges of
K_{\ell}
exist in
G_{1}
. Moreover, since
d\in o(n)
we can check for all
\ell\leqslant d
cliques if
K_{\ell}
is included in
G_{1}
in time
O(d\cdot n^{2})=o(n^{5/2})
. If the answer is "yes" then
G_{1}
contains w.h.p. a planted clique, i.e., the clique
K_{1}
. Otherwise, the answer is "no" and
G_{1}=G^{\prime}
as no clique was planted. Since we recover
K_{1}
in
G_{d}
in polynomial time via
Alg
, this finishes the proof.
∎

Appendix C
RIGs with small
p

C.1
Coupling sparse and dense RIGs

The following statement tells us that any
G\sim{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
can be coupled with a
G^{\prime}\sim\text{RIG}(n,d,p^{\prime},q^{\prime})
for any
p^{\prime}>p
and some
q^{\prime}>q
which is a function of
p,p^{\prime},q
in such a way that
G\subseteq G^{\prime}
while every ground-truth clique
S_{\ell}
is identical in
G,G^{\prime}
.

The idea is to simply ramp up the noise-levels in
G^{\prime}
as compared to
G
while keeping
\delta
intact. This is captured in the following lemma.

Lemma C.1
.

Consider
G\sim{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
and
G^{\prime}\sim RIG(n,d,p^{\prime},q^{\prime})
with
p<p^{\prime},q\in[0,p)
and
q^{\prime}=1-\frac{(1-p^{\prime})(1-q)}{1-p}
. Then
\delta(d,p,q)=\delta(d,p^{\prime},q^{\prime})
and
q<q^{\prime}
.

Proof.

To show that
\delta(d,p,q)=\delta(d,p^{\prime},q^{\prime})
we get in similar fashion to (
4.1
)

\displaystyle\delta(d,p^{\prime},q^{\prime})=\sqrt{1-\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1-q^{\prime}}{1-p^{\prime}}}}\right)\frac{1}{d}}}\right)}.

By our choice of
q^{\prime}
this yields

\displaystyle\delta(d,p^{\prime},q^{\prime})=\sqrt{1-\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{(1-p^{\prime})(1-q)}{(1-p^{\prime})(1-p)}}}\right)\frac{1}{d}}}\right)}.

Then, again invoking (
4.1
), it follows that

\displaystyle\delta(d,p^{\prime},q^{\prime})=\sqrt{1-\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1-q}{1-p}}}\right)\frac{1}{d}}}\right)}=\delta(d,p,q),

as desired.
Next, to see that
q<q^{\prime}
, note that due to our choice of
q^{\prime}
it holds that

(1-p)(1-q^{\prime})=(1-p^{\prime})(1-q).

Moreover, by our assumption
p^{\prime}>p
it also holds
1-p>1-p^{\prime}
and thus,

(1-p)(1-q^{\prime})<(1-p)(1-q)\text{ implying }1-q^{\prime}<1-q,

that is,
q<q^{\prime}
.

∎

With this at hand, our coupling follows.
See
3.2

Proof.

Consider
G^{\prime}\sim RIG(n,d,p^{\prime},q^{\prime})
where
q^{\prime}=1-\frac{(1-p^{\prime})(1-q)}{1-p}
. Then, by our choice of
q^{\prime}
and
p^{\prime}
, we have
q^{\prime}>q
and
\delta(d,p,q)=\delta(d,p^{\prime},q^{\prime})=:\delta
using
Lemma

C.1
. Note that, intuitively,
G
can be considered a sub-graph of
G^{\prime}
, where all ground truth cliques that are present in
G^{\prime}
are also present in
G
, while edges that exist due to noise in
G^{\prime}
only have a certain probability to carry over to
G
and a non-edge in
G^{\prime}
also implies a non-edge in
G
. To make this more formal, draw
G
and
G^{\prime}
as follows:

•

1. Labels:
The labels underlying
G,G^{\prime}
are identical. This is a valid coupling due to
Lemma

C.1
and yields that all ground truth
S_{\ell}
in
G,G^{\prime}
are identical too.

•

2. Noise for
G
:
For each pair of vertices
u,v\in\binom{[n]}{2}
that are not adjacent after step 1, draw a number
r(u,v)
from
(0,1)
uniformly at random. If
r(u,v)\leqslant q
then add the edge
\{u,v\}
.

•

3. Noise for
G^{\prime}
:
For every pair of vertices
u,v\in\binom{[n]}{2}
that does not have an edge after step 2 add the edge
\{u,v\}
if
r(u,v)\leqslant q^{\prime}
.

Observe that the resulting graph after step 2 follows the same distribution as
G
and the graph after step 3 that of
G^{\prime}
. Moreover,
G
and
G^{\prime}
are identical after step 2. Finally, after step 3,
G\subseteq G^{\prime}
since we only add further edges to
G^{\prime}
.
∎

C.2
Simple algorithm for exact recovery in sparse
{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
for
p\leqslant n^{-\varepsilon}

We show that exact recovery in the sparse case (
p\leqslant n^{-\varepsilon}
) is not a very challenging problem (even for
n^{\varepsilon}\ll k\ll\sqrt{n}
) and can be accomplished by a simple, polynomial-time combinatorial algorithm. This phenomenon is in line with existing work on other variants of planted clique: it was observed e.g. in
[
31
]
that this also applies to ordinary planted clique. This suggests that the dense case is actually the only challenging regime. However, we remark that due to the fact that our algorithms work in the semirandom model (i.e. under presence of a monotone adversary), they also achieve recovery in sparse graphs that are arbitrarily “sandwiched” between the noiseless case and the dense, noisy case, and not captured by the simple algorithm we present in the following.

Our algorithm essentially choses a random
t
-tuple, computes a neighbourhood reduction and checks if the entire neighbourhood is a clique. Very similar ideas were presented a long time ago in
[
13
]
, however, the analysis there only covers a much more restrictive range of parameters, mainly because the authors did not have a single label clique theorem at hand. For the sake of completeness, we decided to add this section here, showing that the simple greedy algorithm succeeds whenever
p\leqslant n^{-\varepsilon}
for arbitrarily small
\varepsilon>0

We focus on the following small-
p
-regime to ensure that
k
is still of size polynomial in
n
as captured by the following lemma.

Lemma C.2
.

Let
\alpha\in(0,2),\varepsilon^{\prime}\in(0,2\alpha)
and
k=\delta n
. Let
G\sim{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
with parameters
d=n^{\alpha},p=n^{-\varepsilon^{\prime}}
and
q\in[0,p)
. Then, for all
\varepsilon>0
any
\ell\in[d]
it holds
(1-\varepsilon)k<|S_{\ell}|<(1+\varepsilon)k
w.h.p. and
k=n^{\Omega(1)}
.

Proof.

By our assumption that
q<p
it holds via
Lemma

4.1
that
\delta\geqslant\frac{(1-o(1))}{\sqrt{d}}
. Making use of this we obtain for a fixed label
\ell\in[d]

\mathbb{E}[|S_{\ell}|]=k=\delta n=n^{\Omega(1)},

since
\alpha\in(0,2)
and
\varepsilon^{\prime}\in(0,2\alpha)
. Two Chernoff bounds yield both desired concentrations for
|S_{\ell}|
with probability
1-n^{\-\omega(1)}
since
k
is of polynomial size. A union bound over all
d
labels finishes the proof.
∎

The following lemma tells us that for each ground-truth
S_{\ell}
, there exists a
t
-tuple
T\subset[n]
such that the neighbourhood of
T
is exactly one of our cliques.

Lemma C.3
.

Let
\alpha\in(0,2),\varepsilon^{\prime}\in(0,2\alpha)
and
t=\lceil\frac{1+2\alpha}{\varepsilon^{\prime}}\rceil
. Let
G\sim{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
with parameters
d=n^{\alpha},p=n^{-\varepsilon^{\prime}}
and
q\in[0,p)
. Then, for all
\ell\in[d]
there exists a
T\subset[n]
of size
t
such that
N_{G}(T)=S_{\ell}
w.h.p.

Proof.

Fix a label
\ell\in[d]
. First, reveal the randomness of label
\ell
. Consider the clique
S_{\ell}
which by
Lemma

C.2
has polynomial size w.h.p. Partition
S_{\ell}
into
m=\lfloor|S_{\ell}|/t\rfloor
disjoint subsets
T_{1},..,T_{m}
each of size
t
. Next, reveal all the missing labels
[d]\setminus\ell
for the set of vertices
[n]\setminus S_{\ell}
. Using
Lemma

4.3
, it holds for all
v\in[n]\setminus S_{\ell}
that the number of labels is bounded by
|M_{v}|\leqslant(1+o(1))(d\cdot\delta)
w.h.p. This implies by
Lemma

4.2
that for any pair of vertices
u\in T,v\in[n]\setminus S_{\ell}
that
\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(u\sim v}}\right)\leqslant(1+o(1))p
over the randomness of revealing the rest of labels for
T
. Thus, the probability that a vertex
v\in[n]\setminus S_{\ell}
has an edge to all vertices in
T
is

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v\sim T}}\right)\leqslant((1+o(1))p)^{t}=O(n^{-(1+2\alpha)}),

using that
t=\lceil\frac{1+2\alpha}{\varepsilon^{\prime}}\rceil
and
p\leqslant n^{-\varepsilon}
. Using a union bound over all vertices
v\in[n]\setminus S_{\ell}
yields that
N_{G}(T_{i})=S_{\ell}
with probability
1-O(n^{-2\alpha})
. Another union bound over all
d=n^{\alpha}
labels gives the desired result, that is, for any label
\ell\in[d]
there exists w.h.p. a
t
-tuple
T
of size
t
such that
N_{G}(T)=S_{\ell}
.
∎

We now use these
t
-tuples as witnesses to find all ground truth cliques in polynomial time.

Proposition C.4
.

Let
\alpha\in(0,2)
and
\varepsilon^{\prime}\in(0,2\alpha)
. Let
G\sim{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
with parameters
d=n^{\alpha},p=n^{-\varepsilon^{\prime}}
and
q\in[0,p)
. Then, there is a polynomial-time algorithm for exact recovery that succeeds w.h.p. over the draw of
{\textsc{RIG}(n,d,p,q)}
.

Proof.

Let
t=\lceil\frac{1+2\alpha}{\varepsilon^{\prime}}\rceil
,
S=\emptyset
and for each
T\subset[n]
of size
|T|=t
consider the following procedure:

If
0.99\cdot k\leqslant|N_{G}(T)|\leqslant 1.01\cdot k
and
N_{G}(T)
is a complete graph do
S:=S\cup N_{G}(T)
.

We claim that with high probability the set
S
contains all ground truth cliques while not containing any other set and that the above procedure takes polynomial time.

Runtime
Since procedure runs through
\binom{n}{t}\leqslant n^{t}

t
-tuples and we have to check
O(k^{2})
edges for each iteration, the runtime is
O(n^{t}\cdot k^{2})
. Since
t=O(1)
this is polynomial in
n
.

Correctness
First, we show that every ground truth clique is contained in
S_{\ell}
. By our choice of
t
and using
Lemma

C.3
there indeed exists a
T
such that
N_{G}(T)=S_{\ell}
w.h.p. Moreover, using
Lemma

C.2
the size of
S_{\ell}
is
0.99\cdot k\leqslant|S_{\ell}|\leqslant 1.01\cdot k
w.h.p. and thus, every
S_{\ell}
is included in
S
by our procedure. All that is left to show is that w.h.p. there is no set of vertices contained in
S
that is not a ground truth clique. Note that in this case for a non-empty set
U\subseteq[n]\setminus S_{\ell}
it holds
N_{G}(T)=S_{\ell}\cup U
such that
S_{\ell}\cup U
is a complete graph. However, using
Corollary

6.3
no such clique exists w.h.p.
∎
